# PREFACE.

---

There is no term, perhaps, that has been more frequently and more grossly abused and misapplied than that of *Science*. The word, in its proper and legitimate sense, unquestionably denotes something known, or, at least, something worthy of being known; and it is generally, and most correctly, employed to denote a series of combined facts which tend to establish a certain general law, or series of laws, of Nature, either in the physical, the intellectual, or the moral world.

In order to serve as a foundation for any general conclusion in matters of science, however, it is necessary to demonstrate, in the first place, that those facts upon which we rely do really and permanently exist in nature, under certain conditions of development; that they are not exceptional, fictitious, or illusory; that they, under the requisite conditions, are not merely isolated phenomena of an accidental or capricious, equivocal and transitory

<vocab>Science, Nature, general law, physical world, intellectual world, moral world, isolated phenomena</vocab>

<summary>This preface opens with a definition of science, arguing that the term is often misused and should properly refer to established facts that reveal the laws of nature. The author asserts that scientific conclusions must be based on permanent and real natural phenomena rather than isolated or illusory events.</summary>
<keywords>definition of science, laws of nature, scientific method, physical world, intellectual world, moral world, isolated phenomena, empiricism</keywords>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>This page continues the preface, following the discussion of how scientific facts must be permanent and real rather than transitory or illusory.</meta>

# PREFACE.

nature; and that their existence and character fully warrant us in drawing the general inferences which, on the principles of a sane philosophy and sound logic, we are disposed, or compelled, to deduce from them.

We may remark, however, that in recent times, and particularly in this country, the term *science* has been generally, and, we think, most improperly restricted to Physics alone; for there are facts in psychology and moral philosophy which are equally, if not more important, equally susceptible of analysis, and equally capable of being reduced to general laws; and these facts and these laws, therefore, are at least as deserving of our research and investigation as those which have been discovered in the economy of the physical universe; indeed, the former are of even more real interest and utility to man, as an intelligent and rational being.

> “There are some persons,” says PLATO, “who draw down to the earth all heavenly and invisible things, grasping with their hands rocks and trunks of trees, maintaining that nothing exists as real except what offers resistance and can be felt, holding body and existence to be synonymous. And when others say that something may exist that is incorporeal, they pay no regard to this, and will no longer listen to the subject.” — PLATO; *Ed. Stephan.* <note>Referring to the Stephanus pagination, the standard international reference system for the works of Plato.</note>, p. 246.

<summary>The author argues that the definition of science should not be limited to physics but should also include psychology and moral philosophy, which follow their own general laws. He quotes Plato to criticize materialists who refuse to acknowledge the existence of anything beyond the physical and tangible.</summary>
<keywords>science, physics, psychology, moral philosophy, logic, Plato, materialism, incorporeal, Stephanus pagination</keywords>

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>This page continues the preface, following the discussion that scientific facts and laws in psychology and moral philosophy are as real and important as those in physics.</meta>

# PREFACE.

The study of physics, it is true, as well as that of psychology, has been discouraged by influential individuals and sects at various periods under the mistaken impression that the knowledge acquired in this way must ultimately prove harmful to certain other moral or social interests which, in their estimation, ought to be held as most important among mankind. Consequently, the progress of all science has been occasionally much hindered and its cultivation discouraged during almost every period of world history—as if ignorance were productive of the most perfect happiness and most conducive to the interests and well-being of the human species.

These latter notions, however, so far as they relate to physical science, have now been almost entirely discredited. We are now permitted to freely examine the material objects and physical laws of the universe without becoming liable to an accusation of heresy. We believe the same result must ultimately follow in the case of psychological investigation, regarding the presumed tendency of which much prejudice still continues to be held. In this latter department of science, indeed, facts are daily being developed which are not only of great practical importance but also, in other respects, of the highest interest to mankind as social, intelligent, rational, and responsible beings.

<summary>The author explains that while both physics and psychology were once suppressed by those who feared scientific knowledge would harm social or moral values, physical science is now freely studied. He anticipates that psychology will eventually gain the same level of acceptance as its practical importance for humanity is recognized.</summary>
<keywords>physics, psychology, science, heresy, psychological investigation, scientific development, social interests, history of science</keywords>

<page-num>iv</page-num>

# PREFACE.

About eighty years have now passed since <insert>Anthony Mesmer</insert>—a German physician—first announced a new and very remarkable discovery he had made in the course of his research. Although it was little appreciated when first announced, it was later found to be of no small importance toward the expansion of our scientific knowledge of nature and, especially, of the constitution of man. For a considerable period, as is well known, this very interesting discovery made slow progress in the learned world. It was, indeed, new and startling; it was thought to be inconsistent with some of the already accepted principles of established science, and, therefore, it received little support from the supposedly learned men of the day.

It was, moreover—although on clearly false grounds—considered a dangerous doctrine: a complete scientific and religious heresy. The few who gave it their honest support and encouragement, therefore, were publicly denounced as mystics and ridiculed as fools, or pitied as madmen. The magnetic discovery, indeed, was generally regarded, even by many philosophers, as a gross deception of the ignorance and gullibility of the age; and no terms of contempt were considered too strong to be applied to the few faithful supporters of the seemingly excessive and heretical doctrine. 

<meta>catchword: Time,</meta>
Time,

<summary>The author recounts how Anthony Mesmer's discovery of animal magnetism was initially dismissed as a dangerous heresy and its supporters ridiculed by the scientific establishment. Despite this early hostility, the discovery was eventually recognized as a significant contribution to the understanding of human nature.</summary>
<keywords>Anthony Mesmer, animal magnetism, mesmerism, scientific discovery, scientific prejudice, heresy, human constitution</keywords>

<header>PREFACE.</header>
<page-num>v</page-num>

Time, however, works wonders in the moral as well as in the physical world; and science has its revolutions and reactions as well as empires. A considerable number of intelligent and inquisitive men—undismayed by the denunciations of the ignorant and the self-interested—gradually obtained instruction from the modern discoverer of <term>Animal Magnetism</term>, or his immediate disciples—made experiments themselves—succeeded in eliciting the much-controverted phenomena, and thus became convinced of the truth of the facts, and of the utility and importance of the forbidden magnetic doctrine.

But the more obstinate among the skeptics would not even look at the facts alleged to have been discovered; or, if they did condescend so far, they would not believe their own eyes, unless the causes in operation were immediately and satisfactorily explained to them. Such an explanation, in those early times, however, was not an easy matter; and, at all events, even had it been practicable, probably <term>MESMER</term> was not the man to provide it. Besides, it is well known that many phenomena may be observed long before it becomes possible to explain them, or to demonstrate their rank and value in the scale of human achievement; and, therefore, the skeptics were too unreasonable and impatient. How many natural phenomena are there, too, which have been known for centuries before their scientific causes, and various

<summary>The author argues that time eventually validates scientific truths, noting that many researchers confirmed the reality of animal magnetism through experiments despite initial skepticism. He critiques skeptics for demanding immediate explanations, noting that phenomena are often discovered long before their causes are understood.</summary>
<keywords>Animal Magnetism, Mesmer, scientific skepticism, empirical evidence, scientific revolutions, magnetic doctrine</keywords>

->vi<-

In the meantime, the opponents of <term>Animal Magnetism</term>—and these were a very numerous class—found it more convenient to deny the facts altogether than to submit to the necessary labor of investigation; and in adopting this course, they were sure to have all the weak, the lazy, the timid, the ignorant, and the incompetent on their side. Besides, they might probably have heard a great deal about supernatural powers, fascinations, enchantments, divinations, magic, witchcraft, sorcery—and, perhaps, thousands of ridiculous stories intended to alienate all sober persons from the serious examinations of phenomena which, without due investigation, must have appeared very marvelous and utterly incredible; and, consequently, a fair subject for skepticism. Moreover, there is always a multitude of individuals, even among the better educated classes, who, being themselves incapable of conducting a new and serious investigation or unwilling to undertake the task, are content to await the decision of those who are accustomed to guide the opinion of the public in such matters before they consent to support new and unaccredited doctrines. But, in this par-

<summary>The author explains that opponents of Animal Magnetism often denied the facts out of laziness or because the subject was associated with superstitions like magic and witchcraft. He notes that many people, even the educated, prefer to wait for the approval of public authorities before accepting new scientific ideas.</summary>
<keywords>Animal Magnetism, skepticism, scientific investigation, public opinion, superstition, scientific methodology</keywords>

<header># PREFACE.</header>

->vii<-

particular instance, unfortunately, those who should have been most capable of directing public opinion on the subject of Mesmer's alleged discoveries chose to assume an attitude of perfect indifference, or of actual, violent, uncompromising, and most unreasonable hostility.

It may be noted, however, that not all of the great contemporary luminaries of science viewed the reality of the early magnetic discoveries in this way; and the opinions of such men as Jussieu, Laplace, Cuvier, Treviranus, Hufeland, Sprengel, Schleirmacher, Oken, Reil, Autenrieth, Burdach, Humboldt, and many other eminent authorities—philosophers, naturalists, physiologists, and professional physicians—should have carried more weight with the intelligent and candid portion of the public. Indeed, the very simple, yet productive and most interesting nature of the discoveries alleged to have been made by Mesmer and his associates and disciples should, at least, have had the effect of stimulating curiosity and promoting inquiry.

In the whole history of scientific discovery, indeed, there is perhaps nothing more incomprehensible—we might add more paltry and contemptible—than the indifference and hostility which were so long displayed toward the interesting labors of the early <term>Magnetists</term>. What could be

<summary>The author criticizes the scientific community's hostile and indifferent reaction to Mesmer's discoveries, noting that many distinguished scientists actually supported their validity. # PREFACE.

->viii<-

more strange, in a pretended age of reason and of scientific progress, than to find a discovery so simple, yet so prolific in its consequences—a discovery which was intended to throw so much new light on our knowledge of human nature and the flexibility of the animal organism in general, and to increase our therapeutic powers—to see such a discovery confined within the narrow circle of a small number of inquisitive individuals, who made no mystery of the knowledge they had acquired, yet who dared not speak of the truths they had discovered and embraced without exposing themselves to the scorn or ridicule of powerful and influential antagonists, even among those who were otherwise learned and talented?

Had the question related merely to certain uncertain theoretical notions, or to the adoption of some novel system of abstract truths, we might easily conceive that there might have been ample materials for controversial discussion; but here the subject in dispute was merely a matter of fact, which was capable of being almost immediately verified or disproved by a direct appeal to experiment and observation; and, moreover, the related investigation was exceedingly simple and, besides, open and accessible to all the world.

But it would appear that a large majority of the learned men of the age were, for one reason or

# PREFACE.

->ix<-

another, obstinately biased against the subject of inquiry and little inclined to offer their help in investigating the relevant facts. The members of the medical profession, although perhaps the ones most interested in the ultimate result of the inquiry, distinguished themselves throughout by their bitter opposition to the new discovery, driven by very obvious, though not very generous or even respectable, motives.

The greater part of our scientific prejudices undoubtedly arises from mental bias—from the partial or inadequate nature of our previous investigations. Being unable to comprehend the entirety of nature's diverse phenomena, and therefore confining our attention to a small portion of those most familiar to observation, we nevertheless proceed, based on this limited view, to form our judgment regarding the totality of her laws. This premature and therefore narrow process necessarily leads us to a partial, unsatisfactory, deceptive, and imaginary conception of the powers and operations of nature, which we feel ourselves unable or unwilling to embrace in their full scope or in the infinite variety of her manifestations. Accidental circumstances also frequently direct the attention of mankind, in every age, toward a specific line of inquiry, resulting in the neglect of almost every other field of knowledge; and,

<keywords>medical profession, scientific prejudice, mental bias, laws of nature, natural phenomena, scientific inquiry, professional opposition, human nature</keywords>

# PREFACE.

->x<-

From this, there arise, at different periods, completely different and sometimes contradictory notions regarding the probability or the possibility of certain alleged facts.<note>“No one can learn what he is not prepared to learn. A chemist may tell his most precious secrets to a carpenter, and the carpenter will be no wiser; secrets the chemist would not reveal to another chemist for a fortune. God always shields us from premature ideas. Our eyes are restrained so that we cannot see things that stare us in the face until the hour arrives when the mind is matured; then we behold them, and the time when we did not see them is like a dream.”—EMERSON; *Essay on Spiritual Laws.*</note>

Even during such times when the phenomena that now form the basis of <term>magnetic doctrines</term> were generally known and recognized, they were, unfortunately, enlisted in the service of superstition and regarded as far too sacred to be investigated based on the principles of <term>profane science</term>. Mankind is unwilling to look at the phenomena of nature except through the clouded spectacles of their prejudices; accordingly, even the most simple facts are frequently wrapped in a shroud of mysticism and fallacy. From this source of illusion, all the fables of magic and sorcery—once so prevalent throughout the world—appear to have derived their origin. Ages frequently pass in this state of mental darkness and delusion, and mankind actually becomes afraid of even attempting

<summary>The author explains how scientific prejudice and superstition have historically clouded the understanding of natural phenomena, such as magnetic doctrines. He argues that humanity’s inability to perceive truths often results from mental unpreparedness and a tendency to view facts through the lens of mysticism rather than science.</summary>

<keywords>magnetic doctrines, profane science, superstition, magic, Ralph Waldo Emerson, prejudice, mysticism</keywords>

# PREFACE.

to emerge out of this obscurity of ignorance into the light of knowledge. They are surrounded, for a long period, by a dense cloud of prejudices and false notions which, at length, mingling with their habitual conceptions, become exceedingly difficult to dispel by the torch of science and truth. The simple facts, which may now be satisfactorily explained by the modern psychological discoveries of Animal Magnetism, are, in a great measure, identical with those which in former times—adopted without any investigation into their true origin and nature, and disguised by the coloring of a vivid imagination and a lively fancy—lay at the foundation of all the fables of magic and sorcery, with which they were subsequently identified:

->*"Somnia, terrores magicos, miracula, sagas."*<-

Mankind, in barbarous ages, is accustomed to look at nature through the mist of their ignorance and prejudices, which conceal the true aspect of objects from their bewildered eyes. Hence the long reign of intellectual darkness, false philosophy, and impure religion.

Erroneous notions, indeed, which have been allowed to prevail for ages, are eradicated with difficulty, even from such minds as have become emancipated, in some measure, from many of the

<meta>This page continues the discussion on how even great thinkers are often influenced by the prejudices and superstitions of their time, using historical examples from the history of science.</meta>

# PREFACE.

errors and prejudices of previous and less enlightened ages; and even those philosophers who have devoted themselves to the cultivation of what have been called the exact sciences, are not always exempt, as is sometimes imagined, from the common weakness of being seduced by the common prejudices of less enlightened minds. **Tycho Brahe**, the modern restorer of astronomical science, that most tireless observer of the starry heavens, who made such a large number of valuable observations within the field of his favorite studies, and was so tireless in the investigation of facts;—even this great matter-of-fact philosopher divided his time between the study of astronomy and the research of alchemy. He also supported the doctrines of judicial astrology, and a large portion of his books was devoted to the defense and propagation of these empty daydreams. His successor, **Kepler**, the precursor to **Newton**, the most profound physical philosopher of his age, attributed the motions of the celestial bodies to certain animal forces, and wrote a treatise on the mysterious properties of numbers. **Newton** himself—the most illustrious physical philosopher of his own, or of any age—after explaining the laws of the material universe, wrote a commentary on the Apocalypse. Indeed, the influence of this mystical disposition, even among very practical men, appears to be

<summary>The author argues that even the most brilliant scientific minds, such as Tycho Brahe, Kepler, and Newton, were not immune to the superstitions and mystical prejudices of their eras. Despite their contributions to astronomy and physics, they also engaged in alchemy, astrology, and mystical interpretations of numbers and scripture.</summary>

<keywords>Tycho Brahe, Kepler, Newton, astronomy, alchemy, astrology, physical philosophy, Apocalypse, celestial bodies, exact sciences, prejudice, mysticism</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on how mystical hallucinations can affect even the most rigorous scientific minds, transitioning into a critique of the modern scientific focus on purely mechanical and material forces.</meta>

# PREFACE.

more common than is generally suspected; and a notable instance of the occasional predominance of such hallucinations, even in men who have distinguished themselves in the department of material science, occurs in the well-known case of **Emanuel Swedenborg**.

But another, and to some degree opposite, aberration in the domain of scientific research deserves to be mentioned as even more relevant to the science we are about to present for our readers' consideration. For a considerable period, the efforts of philosophers have been mainly directed toward the discovery and understanding of those mechanical forces which appear to regulate the motions of the material universe, and which have been found to be capable of precise calculation. The labors of those eminent men who have worked in this field of investigation have given a decided bias toward the study of material nature and the action of those physical forces which are recognized as predominant in the external universe. We would be very unwilling to try to detract, in any way, from the legitimate fame of those enterprising and intelligent philosophers whose commendable efforts have led to the development of so many interesting and useful results. But we feel compelled to acknowledge that, in our humble opinion, they have

<summary>The author identifies Emanuel Swedenborg as an example of a scientist influenced by hallucinations and argues that modern science has become overly biased toward studying purely mechanical and material forces.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the author's critique of the one-sided nature of modern science, arguing that the focus on the external universe has led to the neglect of the "percipient mind" and psychology.</meta>

# PREFACE.

attempted to solve only one portion of the grand
problem of Nature; indeed, we suspect that the very
success of their achievements has had a tendency
to discourage, and, consequently, to retard the
solution of the other, and still more interesting
portion of the inquiry.

Nature—we would observe—presents us with
two different—but, in our opinion, correlative—
subjects of investigation—the external universe,
and the percipient mind. All philosophy must be
incomplete if it does not embrace both of these
objects of research. Without a mind to perceive
and comprehend it, no external universe could
exist; and the mind has its peculiar properties as well
as matter.

But in the midst of that scientific regeneration
which has taken place in modern times, philosophy
has become almost entirely one-sided—our attention
is principally, or almost wholly, directed
toward external objects; and the study of the
intelligent and percipient mind, with all its active
energies and passive susceptibilities (*psychology*),
has been utterly neglected, or even scorned,
amidst the materialistic tendencies of the age. In
short, we would appear to have become incapable
of distinguishing the various accidental modes of
the exercise of our perceptive faculties, occasioned
by the different conditions of our psychical organs,

<summary>The author argues that modern science is incomplete because it focuses exclusively on the external material world while neglecting the study of the percipient mind and psychology. He suggests that without the mind to perceive it, the external universe could not exist.</summary>

<keywords>Nature, philosophy, percipient mind, psychology, materialistic tendencies, psychical organs, external universe</keywords>

--- Page 23 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's argument that our neglect of the mind's internal states leads us to misunderstand or ignore phenomena that do not fit into our established materialist systems.</meta>

<page-num>XV</page-num>
<header>PREFACE.</header>

in the various states and conditions of the sensitive powers. The consequence of this has been that when we happen to stumble upon a phenomenon which appears foreign to the usual train of our ideas, but which we find it impossible to reject *in toto*, we become incapable of appreciating its true nature and value, and are induced either to overlook it altogether, as something utterly anomalous and incomprehensible, or to refer it to certain imaginary causes. In such cases, those who are considered learned are, probably, the least capable of exercising a sound and impartial judgment, in consequence of their prejudices. To all such problems, they immediately give a dogmatic solution, without giving themselves the trouble of instituting an experimental inquiry.

A new truth, however—which, when rightly understood, is capable of throwing additional light upon some particular department of our knowledge—frequently substitutes reality for illusion, and shows that things are occasionally different in nature from what they appear to be in our preconceived systems. But unreasoning dogmatism is itself a mental disease; frequently a very stubborn or even incurable disorder; and it is always a very difficult matter to abandon opinions once seriously entertained, even upon insufficient evidence;

<summary>The author argues that scientific prejudices cause the "learned" to ignore or misinterpret phenomena that contradict their existing theories. He suggests that dogmatism acts as a mental disease that prevents the acceptance of new truths that could clarify our understanding of reality.</summary>

<keywords>sensitive powers, phenomenon, dogmatism, experimental inquiry, new truth, mental disease, scientific prejudice, reality vs illusion</keywords>

--- Page 24 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's argument regarding the difficulty of overcoming established intellectual habits and prejudices when faced with new scientific truths.</meta>

<page-num>xvi</page-num>
<header>PREFACE.</header>

and a particular habit of thinking, according to a common proverb, becomes a second nature:

> “Ponere difficile est quæ placuere diu.” <note>It is difficult to set aside what has long been pleasing.</note>

If we look back over the ages that have passed, we will likely find that a considerable number of the obstacles to the introduction of new truths generally arise from the specific direction given to inquiry by the previous speculations of otherwise distinguished men. New discoveries in every succeeding age may conflict with some of the opinions and dogmas, or even the prejudices, of the learned men of their time; and the learned—or those reputed to be learned—have, at all times, been the most stubborn opponents of new truths.

> “*Turpe putant parere minoribus, et quæ*
> *Imberbes didicere, senes perdenda fateri.*” <note>They think it shameful to yield to their juniors, and to confess as old men that what they learned as beardless youths must now be discarded.</note>

In the investigation of nature, indeed, it is a very difficult thing to shake off preconceived notions—to maintain the clarity and unbiased impartiality of our judgment, and to avoid being misled by our prejudices. It is almost unnecessary to remind our readers of the well-known examples of GALILEO, COLUMBUS, HARVEY, JENNER, etc., or to expand upon the opposition made to the introduction of Inoculation, Quinquina <note>Cinchona bark, used to treat malaria.</note>, Antimony, etc.,

It is well known that the *vaccine*, upon its first introduction, was equally offensive to the profession. This last-mentioned discovery had been originally made, within the memory of living men, in a province of England at some distance from the capital; and the practice, like that of Magnetism, was placed in the category of dangerous superstitions and delusions, until JENNER, after its condemnation by the medical faculty, at length obtained a notable triumph over all prejudices by its general introduction into practice.*

Such instances, certainly, should have the effect of making us more cautious about rejecting facts without a proper investigation of their nature, truth, and value, simply because they may appear, at first sight, to be inconsistent with some of the notions we may have been previously led to hold regarding the powers and phenomena of nature. Theories—frequently the product of misconception, or of too partial and limited an inquiry—must not be allowed to invalidate facts; and there is nothing more harmful to the advancement and ultimate establishment of truth than deep-seated prejudices and preconceptions.

painful struggles in its promotion."—See *Lives of British Physicians*.

A case similarly illustrating the jealousy of the medical profession occurred not very long ago in France.

M. BOUDIN attained eminence as Chief Physician to the army of the Alps. He is considered a leading authority in military medicine and wrote some instructive letters on the French colony of Algeria. Some years ago, he was one of the managers of the Hospital at Toulon, and after some interesting experiments on the effects of arsenic, he introduced an arsenical treatment for marsh fever <note>malaria</note>, from which the soldiers from Algeria suffered. The Paris faculty made a great outcry; the Minister was besieged with protests; M. BOUDIN was stopped in his treatment and threatened with a judicial inquiry. But he had *succeeded*; the Government protected him; he was allowed to proceed; and his method was soon afterward professionally recognized. He later rose rapidly in his profession.

<term>Animal Magnetism</term>, upon its first introduction to the scientific world, experienced the same fate as those other discoveries to which we have alluded. By the learned men of the day, it was rejected and ridiculed as a complete fraud; and its adherents were stigmatized as charlatans and dupes. But after the passing of years of contentious controversy—and that, too, in a scientific age—this important discovery also obtained a signal triumph over its ignorant, self-interested, and prejudiced opponents. Many of those who had previously opposed it on philosophical grounds finally had the honesty to acknowledge their error and became its most valuable supporters; indeed, the serious opponents are now reduced to a very small number, and those are not remarkably distinguished for their scientific achievements or philosophical honesty.

It must not be hidden, however, that while magnetic science is becoming more and more widely spread, there are still a few who regard this branch of science with considerable jealousy and suspicion, as apparently tending, in its consequences, to overturn certain other notions which are supposed to be of primary importance to society. But this idea, too, in our humble opinion, is entirely founded upon a misunderstanding. No one truth can possibly conflict with another truth; the antagonism, if any, consists not in the things them-

<meta>This page continues the argument that apparent contradictions in scientific facts often arise from human error rather than reality, using the example of geocentrism to illustrate why Animal Magnetism should not be dismissed prematurely.</meta>

<page-num>XX</page-num>

selves, but in the erroneous conceptions of the human mind; and we must not determine the reality of one fact by its presumed inconsistency with another. We ought to accept the phenomena of nature as we find them developed by our experience and endeavor to reconcile them with each other, and not to aggravate presumed discrepancies; for by adopting the latter course, we shall retard, instead of promoting, the advancement of general science. That the sun makes a daily circuit around the earth is believed to be a fact by many even at the present day. Their belief is founded upon the apparent evidence of their senses; and they laugh at the philosophers who maintain the contrary proposition, viewing them as visionaries and mystics.

We are all convinced, or at least profess to be convinced, of the uncertainty of mere theories and of the absurdity of denying positive facts merely because they appear to be hostile to our preconceived notions of the powers of nature and their various modes of manifestation. And yet we still find individuals who, although apparently satisfied of this truth, do not hesitate to reject the doctrine of <term>Animal Magnetism</term>, not exactly because it absolutely contradicts any of the known laws of nature—for that has never yet been proved—but merely because the phenomena it presents to our view appear to

<meta>This page continues the author's critique of scientific prejudice, moving from a general discussion of erroneous conceptions to the specific "vicious method" of reasoning that leads philosophers to reject animal magnetism without investigation.</meta>

<page-num>xxi</page-num>
<header>PREFACE.</header>

lead to consequences different from the dominant notions of the age regarding the powers and susceptibilities of the animal organism. We reason *a priori*, from the presumptions of our own minds, instead of *a posteriori*, from the phenomena actually presented by nature to our observation. We first endeavor to persuade ourselves that a thing is impossible, and then proceed to deny the fact of its actual existence based on that presumption, in the face of all evidence, even the most convincing. We begin by asking ourselves whether a certain phenomenon is possible, instead of inquiring into the means of establishing the fact of its reality; and we then proceed to pronounce judgment, not based on evidence, but on prejudice. The result of such a flawed method of proceeding is just this: from a limited number of established phenomena, we deduce certain general laws, which we regard as the sole laws of nature applicable to the particular circumstances, and we reject all other facts except those which we conceive to be capable of being explained by this arbitrary criterion.

It is this flawed method of reasoning which has led some philosophers to the rejection of the phenomena of <term>animal magnetism</term>, without adequate investigation, and upon the absurd pretext that, by recognizing these, we would run the risk of bringing the minds of men back to the belief in occult

--- Page 30 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's argument against those who dismiss animal magnetism simply because its causes are not yet understood, shifting into a philosophical discussion on the nature of causality and the limits of human inquiry.</meta>

<page-num>xxii</page-num>
<header>PREFACE.</header>

...causes. And do we not daily find men who continually reproach the magnetists for reporting facts which they do not claim to be able to explain? But it may be reasonably asked, “What do we actually know? Of what can we thoroughly explain the causes?” Let us suppose two phenomena, A and B, which are so connected together that when A appears, B will invariably follow; we necessarily assume that B takes place because A exists, and, therefore, we say that A is the cause of B. On this occasion, we have no need to enter into any abstract metaphysical discussion regarding the nature of the connection between cause and effect: It is sufficient for our present purpose to state the simple fact. Whether this connection results from repeated experience, or from a necessary law of our mental constitution, we may leave to the determination of metaphysicians; we are only concerned with the fact itself.

But the human mind is not content to rest at this stage of the inquiry—it desires to proceed further—and after having found the proximate cause of a particular phenomenon, it attempts to discover the cause of the cause, and so onward, until it arrives at a primitive cause, beyond which it cannot go.

Several phenomena appear to stand in no regular relation towards each other; and when we attempt to ascend to their common source, we feel ourselves

<summary>The author argues that scientific facts should not be rejected just because their underlying causes remain unexplained, noting that human knowledge of causality often relies on observing consistent sequences of events. --- Page 31 ---
<meta>This page concludes the author’s discussion on scientific methodology, specifically focusing on inductive reasoning and the inherent limits of human understanding when discovering the laws of nature.</meta>

<page-num>xxiii</page-num>
<header>PREFACE.</header>

compelled to ascribe them to the same general law which we recognize as primitive—as one of the fundamental conditions which are necessary to the existence of the universe. This process of reducing several phenomena to one general law is what is commonly called inductive reasoning. Such a process led NEWTON, from the simplest observation, to the discovery of the great law of universal attraction, or *gravitation*. A somewhat similar process, originally founded, it is true, upon a hypothesis, led COPERNICUS to his grand discovery. From the motion of the earth, he inferred the movements of all the celestial bodies, and this inference was confirmed by correct calculations. Beyond this we cannot proceed. When we have once established a general law of nature, we have reached the limit assigned to our faculties, and must take our stand on the primitive will and *fiat* of the great Creator of the universe; for who would otherwise attempt to explain the cause of a general law? The true philosopher endeavors to connect the various phenomena of the universe in such a manner as to elicit one or more of these general laws; and it is in this way—and in this way alone—that we can best contribute to the completion of the sciences. To attempt to go beyond this point is an error into which no person of sound sense and philosophical tact will readily fall.

<summary>The author explains that inductive reasoning allows scientists like Newton and Copernicus to identify general laws of nature, though the ultimate cause of these laws rests with the Creator and remains beyond human comprehension.</summary>
<keywords>inductive reasoning, Isaac Newton, gravitation, Nicolaus Copernicus, laws of nature, philosophy, scientific method, Creator</keywords>

<page-num>xxiv</page-num>
<header>PREFACE.</header>

Therefore, once we have arrived at such general laws in any one department of investigation, it is evident that we can proceed no further in the explanation of particular natural phenomena. But it is equally evident that, as we cannot flatter ourselves with the notion that we have arrived at a knowledge of all the laws of nature in the material and the moral world, we are not entitled to reject any real phenomenon merely because we cannot immediately explain it by any of those theories deduced from the facts which have already been discovered. Such conduct would imply a gratuitous and unwarrantable limitation of the progressive march and development of the human mind, and an attempt to describe a narrow circle beyond which we must deny that anything can exist or become known. In some cases, indeed, we resort to the expression, *occult cause*, but as a primary cause can only be known from the effects it produces, it is evident that by *occult cause* we can only mean a cause whose full effects have not yet been properly determined;—if it were otherwise, we should be compelled to acknowledge that everything in the universe was governed by occult causes. What, we would ask, is there more occult than the influence of man’s will on his corporeal <note>physical</note> movements?

NEWTON ascribed the physical motions of the universe to attraction. The Cartesians attributed the same motions to certain vortices, which, on their hypothesis, drew these bodies along with them in their movements; and the latter accused the former of having recourse to an occult cause. VOLTAIRE, who was one of the first propagators of the Newtonian doctrines in France, said, in discussing this subject:
> “Those who believe in occult causes are subjected to ridicule; but we ought rather to ridicule those who do not.”
And, in truth, we find nothing but occult causes in the universe, not even excepting the vortices of DESCARTES, were they otherwise admissible. The hypothesis of NEWTON, however, became generalized into a universal law of material nature, and thus explained the principle of the mundane motions. <note>In this context, "mundane motions" refers to the movement of the worlds or celestial bodies.</note>

They have appeared so strange and unaccountable that, until a very recent period, mankind seems to have universally agreed in ascribing them to supernatural causes, and in referring them to the immediate action of the Deity, or, at least, to certain divine or demoniacal beings. In comparatively recent times, however, certain investigators into the phenomena of nature have attempted to debunk this superstitious view of the matter, to explain the phenomena in question using natural principles, and to bring them under general laws. The facts themselves have been carefully collected and exposed to the light of philosophical investigation.

A religion built up entirely on the foundation of *pretended miracles* must necessarily be a weak and perishable thing; and the progress of general intelligence wreaks havoc upon all miraculous beliefs.*

However, let it not be imagined that we absolutely deny the possibility of miracles, or foolishly pretend to limit the power of the Almighty—which would be equally irrational and impious. But the inscrutable wisdom of the great Creator and Governor of the universe cannot be supposed capable of manifesting itself in action in an arbitrary, capricious, and contradictory manner; for such conduct would imply imperfection, and would, therefore, be derogatory to the character and attributes of the Deity, in whom there can be “no variableness nor shadow of turning.” All nature is God’s nature, constant and invariable in its manifestations under their proper conditions; and we may be assured that these manifestations must have been the same in kind, however apparently modified by circumstances, throughout all ages of time.

On the other hand, the faculties of man are gradually developed both in the individual and in the species. The knowledge of the infant is corrected and enlarged by the mature judgment of the adult; and the same system of development is manifested in the progress of society at large. The ideas of one age are corrected, modified, and extended by the more matured experience and judgment—

Some time after the preceding portion of this Preface was written, my clever and respected friend, Dr. Braid of Manchester, was kind enough to send to me a copy of his recently published "*Observations on Trance, or Human Hibernation;*" in which some curious cases are narrated of individuals who permitted themselves to be buried alive for considerable periods of time, and were afterwards exhumed and brought back to life again. Hypnotism,* indeed, embraces only a small portion, or fragment, of Animal Magnetism or Mesmerism; and it is evidently nothing more than an offshoot from that science. Perhaps it may embrace as much as may be required for mere medical purposes; but it totally excludes the philosophical scope and importance of the Magnetic doctrines. We had at one time hoped that the learned Doctor himself would have ultimately become aware of this position of his favorite science; but now, we strongly suspect that he is anxious to claim the merit of being an original discoverer, and to get rid of his troublesome and embarrassing precursor. Dr. MAYO afterwards very properly observes, “that the entranced person is probably always liable to mislead you, either through his view being at that time accidentally obscured; or through the influence of preconceived notions on his mind; or through the thoughts of others who are currently influencing him. And an observer must always be on his guard against these unintentional sources of error, as well as against premeditated deception.” This is a caution worthy of being more strictly observed by careless, and perhaps skeptical, experimentalists.

According to Dr. MAYO, “it is easy theoretically to explain the beneficial results which follow from the daily induction of trance for an hour or so, in various forms of disorder of the nervous system—in epilepsy—in *tic douloureux*—in nervous palsy, and the like. As long as the state of trance is maintained, the nervous system remains in a state of repose. It is more or less completely put out of gear. It experiences the same relief which a sprained joint feels when you place it, in a relaxed position, on a pillow. A chance is thus given to the strained nerves of recovering their healthy tone. And it is wonderful how many cases of nervous disorder get well at once through these simple means.

> “As it is certain that there is no disease in which the nervous system is not primarily or secondarily involved, it is impossible to foresee what will prove to be the limit of the beneficial application of *Mesmerism* in medical practice.”

> “In operative surgery, the art is no less available. In a trance, the patient is insensible; and a limb may be removed without the operation causing disturbance of any kind. And what is equally important, in all the follow-up treatment, at every dressing, the process of *Mesmerizing* may be resorted to again with no possible disadvantage; instead, it is soothing and useful to the patient, quite apart from the elimination of the dread and suffering of pain.”

The following account, given by Dr. MAYO, regarding the phenomena exhibited by a patient in a state of cataleptic trance, is applicable to a variety of other cases, and may assist us in explaining many of the most curious phenomena of *Mesmerism*.

> “1. The organs of sensation are abandoned by their natural sensitivity. The patient neither feels with the skin, nor sees with the eyes, nor hears with the ears, nor tastes with the mouth.

> “2. All these senses, however, are not lost. Sight and hearing, if not smell and taste, reappear in some other part; at the pit of the stomach, for instance, or the tips of the fingers.

an animal are produced from its food. Production, in the strict sense of the word, means the conversion of one substance into another, weight for weight, in accordance with, or under, mechanical, chemical, and vital laws. If the mind is the product of the brain, it must be the conversion of a certain amount of brain, weight for weight, into thought and feeling, which is an absurdity.

> “It is, indeed, true that with the manifestation of each thought and feeling, a corresponding decomposition of the brain takes place. But it is equally true that in a Voltaic battery in action, each movement of electric force developed there is accompanied by a waste of the metal plates which help to form it. But that waste is not converted into electric fluid. The exact quantity of pure copper which disappears may be detected in the form of sulfate of copper. The electricity was not *produced*; it was only *set in motion* by the chemical decomposition. Here is the true material analogy of the relation of the brain to the mind. Mind, like electricity, is an imponderable force pervading the universe: and there happen to be known to us certain material arrangements, through which each may be influenced. We cannot, indeed, pursue the analogy beyond this step. Consciousness and electricity have nothing further in common. Their further relation to the dissimilar arrangements, through which they may be excited

<summary>This page argues that the mind is not a physical product of the brain's mass but rather an imponderable force set in motion by it, similar to how electricity is generated by a battery without the metal plates being converted into the electrical fluid itself.</summary>

> “It is remarkable how early in the history of mankind the belief in the separate existence of the soul developed as an instinct of our nature.”

We are truly happy to find our opinions on these complex subjects corroborated by the clever research of a gentleman who stands in the very highest rank of British physiologists.

Without further comment, we leave these opinions—which we adopt as our own—to be considered at leisure by the hypnotists and other medical skeptics. And, in the meantime, we sincerely trust that, despite the physical infirmities that come with age, the life and spirits of Dr. MAYO may be preserved for a long time to come. This will enable him to continue pleasing and instructing his friends and the general public with his valuable scholarly writings on scientific subjects and the refined and playful character of his writing style.

The only fault we have been able to detect in this spirited and entertaining volume is the clever author’s assessment of MESMER, whose labors, it seems to us, he has greatly undervalued. Too little allowance is made for the spirit of the times, the nature of the discovery, and the unique circumstances in which the modern reviver of <term>magnetic doctrines</term> was placed.

Ever since it was first introduced to public notice in modern times by MESMER, the science of <term>Animal Magnetism</term> has been exposed to much persecution, slander, and ridicule, which have considerably delayed its progress. It is quite obvious, however, that all this opposition has arisen from ignorance, misconception, or self-interested motives. The opponents, therefore, may be divided into two classes. The first includes those who are unable or unwilling to conduct such an investigation as might lead to a reasonable conviction. The second embraces a considerable proportion of the members of the medical profession who, after a laborious course of professional study, are unwilling to go to school again and are, therefore, inclined to belittle the real value and practical utility of the magnetic discovery. Indeed, among the majority of those who are uninstructed in the principles of this discovery, or who have not thoroughly examined the phenomena with which it deals, there is a strong and perhaps not altogether unnatural tendency toward skepticism. this we do not much object: For it is a remarkable fact, as the author has observed elsewhere, that all the most stubborn scientific opponents of the system have been subsequently converted into enthusiastic supporters of the magnetic doctrines, and that, so far as our inquiries have extended, not a single rational convert has afterward been persuaded to abandon his conviction. On the contrary, many of these original skeptics have become the most valuable supporters and practical explainers of the science, and among these we find many of the most eminent physicians and philosophers in Europe.

It is indeed quite true that we frequently meet people in society who show astonishment and skepticism when any apparently extraordinary or unusual magnetic fact happens to be mentioned; but this astonishment and skepticism are the products of ignorance. *This*, in particular, is a subject on which no individual is competent to give a definitive opinion without previous careful and honest investigation. It is comforting, however, to observe that in the present times, the doctrine of <term>Animal Magnetism</term> is gradually becoming less mysterious, and that many new discoveries of the reality of these facts are almost daily clearing away skepticism and spreading belief in the public mind. Serious inquiry is rapidly taking the place of irrational doubt and narrow-minded prejudice.

The author of the following treatise, however, is perfectly aware that he may be exposed to a charge of credulity regarding some of the facts and narratives to which he has had occasion to refer in the following pages. For such a charge, therefore, he is not unprepared. Some authors, indeed, are afraid of relating, or even of alluding to, facts which may possibly excite skepticism, or even ridicule, among the ignorant and the prejudiced. Facts, however, when fully ascertained and accredited by competent inquirers, must be boldly and faithfully proclaimed, especially when they tend, in the opinion of the author, to advance the interests of science and humanity. Truth, in all matters, but more especially in relation to scientific research—and even more so when it tends to advance the improvement and welfare of mankind in any particular direction—can never be injurious to society. To every philosopher, therefore, we would recommend the advice of <term>Cicero</term>: *Ne quid falsi dicere audeat; ne quid veri non audeat.* <note>Let him not dare to say anything false; let him not dare to leave anything true unsaid.</note> Only the cowardly and incompetent are afraid of truth; perhaps because it is beyond their reach, or is believed to be incompatible with what they consider to be their interests. But banish truth from the world, and what remains to mankind? A labyrinth or a desert!

One of the most important duties of a philosopher, indeed, and one of the most difficult, too, is

There are few, however, who become capable of maintaining this moral and intellectual equilibrium.

It is well known, for example, what a perplexity an eminent professor of mathematics in Edinburgh (Mr MACLAURIN) was once thrown into, on receiving from a friend abroad an account of a few of the first discovered and least remarkable effects of that astonishing power, the *electric fluid*. The professor, liberal, knowledgeable, and candid as he was, could hardly credit the testimony of his friend; and not doubting the veracity of a man he highly esteemed, concluded that a delirium had seized his imagination. A more satisfactory instance of the necessity of suspending a positive judgment, in many things where one is inclined to decide without adequate investigation, can hardly be imagined. GOETHE, the celebrated German author—a keen and most intelligent observer of nature, although not exactly, as far as we know, a self-declared magnetist—appears to have been firmly convinced of the existence and phenomena of magnetic power and susceptibility, as appears, in particular, from his conversations with ECKERMANN; and he gives several instances of their manifestation. He appears to consider magnetic influence as something instinctive, and peculiar to animal sensibility. “We are all groping,” he says, “among mysteries and wonders. Besides, one soul may have a decided influence upon another, merely by means of its silent presence, of which I could relate many instances. It has often happened to me, that when I have been walking with an acquaintance, and have had a vivid image of something in my mind, he has at once begun to speak to me of that very thing. I have also known a man who, without saying a word, could suddenly silence a party engaged in cheerful conversation, by the mere power of his mind. The following observations of the same illustrious author are equally just and appropriate to our subject. "In the sciences," said he, "that which has been handed down or taught at the universities is also looked upon as property. And if anyone advances anything new that contradicts, or perhaps threatens to overturn, the creed that we have repeated for years and have handed down to others, all passions are raised against him, and every effort is made to crush him. People resist with all their might; they act as if they neither heard nor could comprehend; they speak of the new view with contempt, as if it were not worth the trouble of even an investigation or consideration; and thus a new truth may wait a long time before it can make its way."

The medical application of Animal Magnetism, or Mesmerism, has always been viewed with great jealousy by the profession, especially in this country; and the most extraordinary subterfuges are occasionally resorted to in order to evade the evidence, or, at least, to make the practice seem suspicious. We fully concede to the <term>Phrenologists</term>—should they consider this any advantage to their science—that the brain is a most important organ in the <term>animal economy</term> <note>The functional system of a living body</note>, and that much may depend upon its regular and healthy development. But further than this, it is conceived, we cannot go;—beyond this, speculation cannot proceed with any certainty of a satisfactory result. Thought is not to be found in the <term>viscera</term>, any more than music can be considered as inherent in the strings of a fiddle, or the keys of a harpsichord; but, in the latter case, in the vibrations of the air. But there are secrets in nature connected with the <term>science of mind</term>, which, perhaps, will never be revealed to our perceptive faculties.

It is probably known to many inquisitive readers that the author of this publication has long devoted himself to the study of <term>Animal Magnetism</term>; and through reading, conversation, and experience, he has been led to form very definite opinions regarding the nature of the phenomena produced, as well as the practical uses and advantages of that study, from both a scientific and a practical perspective. He has ventured to publish some works on the subject, and he has always considered the advantages of this science to be of infinitely greater value in a practical sense than in a speculative one. Indeed, he has always feared that the very extraordinary phenomena produced by the practical application of this method might turn the heads and disturb the intellects of certain speculative devotees of this branch of science, and thus compromise the solid advantages of this knowledge—and so it has happened. Through a forced and unnatural association with <term>Phrenology</term>, attempts have been made to make it serve the interests of <term>materialism</term>, <term>infidelity</term>, and <term>atheism</term>. In short, through these speculations, the universe has been deprived of its God, and man of his immortal soul.

The author of the following treatise is now an old man, having nearly reached that age which the <term>Royal Psalmist</term> <note>King David, the traditional author of the Psalms; the age mentioned is seventy years (Psalm 90:10).</note> has assigned as the ordinary limit

# A HISTORY OF MAGIC, etc.

## CHAPTER I.

There is perhaps no part of the wide field of science which has been less cultivated, especially in modern times, than the philosophy of the human constitution, encompassing its unique characteristics and the various phases in which its more interesting phenomena may be occasionally presented for our serious consideration. The study of this particular subject, indeed, appears to be not only unpopular in the present day; it is even seriously condemned by many timid or prejudiced inquirers, who seem to believe—erroneously, in our opinion—that the results to which such an investigation leads us may eventually prove contrary to certain other dogmas of belief which they have been accustomed to cherish and to regard as demonstrated and incontrovertible truths, or to subvert some other opinions which they may have thoughtlessly accepted as fundamental and supreme facts. Such notions and such

<vocab>philosophy of the human constitution, phenomena, dogmas of belief, investigation</vocab>

<summary>The author introduces the study of the human constitution and its unique phenomena, noting its neglect in modern science. He observes that many avoid this research out of fear that it might contradict established beliefs or religious dogmas.</summary>
<keywords>human constitution, philosophy, science, dogmas, investigation, human phenomena, prejudice</keywords>

--- Page 74 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from Chapter I regarding the intellectual prejudices that hinder the study of human phenomena and the nature of truth.</meta>

<header>A HISTORY OF MAGIC, etc.</header>

Such conduct, however, betrays a degree, not of ignorance merely, but of moral weakness or cowardice, which is utterly degrading to an intelligent, candid, and inquiring mind. It is incompatible with all freedom of thought and impartiality of judgment; and, consequently, these attitudes become a serious impediment to the progressive advancement of science and civilization. But, in opposition to all such prejudices, we are disposed to hold with the poet, that

->“The proper study of mankind is man;”<-

and we may be perfectly certain that no one truth, when once satisfactorily ascertained to be a truth, can possibly militate against another truth. The incompatibility exists only in the mind which creates it and demonstrates the narrowness of its conceptions. No one truth was ever substantially injured by another truth when both were properly understood and duly restricted within the just limits of their own particular application; although, indeed, our conceptions may be occasionally enlarged, modified, or corrected by the diligent exercise of our intellectual faculties in the gradual investigation of nature in all its various forms and stages of development. We hear much, indeed, about credulity in the acceptance of phenomena. Now, credulity may be defined as a belief without any adequate grounds of conviction in regard to the reality of its object; and such a credulity, when it is combined, as it frequently is, with superstition or with some other mental hallucination, becomes

<summary>The author argues that fearing new truths is a sign of intellectual cowardice and that no two truths can truly contradict each other when properly understood. He defines credulity as belief without evidence, which often leads to superstition or hallucination.</summary>
<keywords>prejudice, truth, science, civilization, human nature, Alexander Pope, credulity, superstition, intellectual inquiry</keywords>

--- Page 75 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on credulity and superstition, exploring how they hinder the pursuit of truth and the role of religion and science in intellectual life.</meta>

<header>A HISTORY OF MAGIC, etc.</header>

one of the most powerful, yet most deceptive, and, in some cases, the most harmful motivations for false beliefs. The superstitious man is unable, or afraid, to use his powers of reason. He is unwilling to ask questions, or incapable of directing his intellectual and moral abilities toward the unbiased search for truth. He is completely satisfied with the first biased convictions that his untrained mind was once led, however carelessly, to adopt, and is stubbornly unwilling to allow them to be disturbed or changed by any other, even more mature, viewpoints. This explains the powerful and lasting influence that all false systems of religion and philosophy have had over the minds of their respective followers, and the harmful effects they have frequently had on society. Christianity alone, when accepted in its genuine purity and truth, can endure the test of the most rigorous philosophical examination and emerge from the trial unharmed. But even the Christian religion itself can be, and has been, corrupted and degraded throughout history by unwise cultivation in a wrong direction. Superstition—the product of false and degrading views of religion—when opposed to science, as it often is, has a strong tendency to serve the goals of ignorance. It does this by discouraging the pursuit of learning and philosophy; yet these last can never be harmful to genuine and pure religion, however hostile they may be to false perspectives and degrading worship. There are few subjects, therefore, more interesting to the philosophical inquirer than the history of human superstition, which itself results, in large part, from ignorance of the existence and operation of proximate causes. This branch of research, indeed, when diligently, accurately, and impartially pursued, cannot fail—regardless of its value in other respects—to reveal to our view some of the most powerful sources of speculation and action in the human mind; and it must unquestionably tend to make us more intimately acquainted with many of the more important and apparently more mysterious feelings and impulses of our common nature. Some significant truths, too, may thus be developed in the course of our inquiries, although these may frequently be distorted or rendered obscure because they are directly attributed to erroneous, or perhaps even fictitious, causes.

Before entering into the particular investigation of this interesting subject, however, it may be necessary to remind the reader that superstition—the offspring not of actual depravity, as has been alleged, but of ignorance and credulity—may be manifested either in arbitrary, false, and fantastic notions of things that have no real existence, or in crude and erroneous ideas respecting the true character and proximate causes of phenomena that have an actual existence in

Facts themselves may be perfectly authentic, while the explanations commonly given for them are false and fantastical; having their origin in ignorance, misconception, or prejudice; and opinions of this nature are frequently transmitted, unquestioned, from generation to generation, long after these explanations ought to have been rectified and superseded by the general diffusion of a more enlightened and rational science. This circumstance, indeed, as we shall have an opportunity of showing more at length in the sequel, is believed to have been a principal source of the many erroneous and perverted notions so generally held in society in regard to the interesting conclusions to which we are naturally led by the curious phenomena of <term>Animal Magnetism</term>, which have been so fully clarified by the selfless labors of those learned and ingenious men who, in defiance of skepticism, obloquy, and ridicule, have endeavored to <term>expiscate</term> <note>to search out or discover through diligent investigation</note> the facts, and to explain the <term>Mesmeric doctrines</term>.

The earth, the ocean, the stars, the winds, the mountains, the woods, the rivers, etc., were all placed under subjection to a particular supernatural influence—each had its own special and appropriate god. The diseases which occasionally afflicted humanity—probably less frequent in the earlier than in the more advanced stages of society—were believed to be produced by malevolent spirits; dreams were the gift of benevolent spirits; nervous crises, originating in an abnormal condition of the organism, were held to be prophetic inspirations. Hence that varied mythology, embraced by the devotional conviction of entire nations, and subsequently enlarged, embellished, and perpetuated by the imagination of the poets. Although this mythology has long since been discredited and debunked by the revelation of a more pure and genuine religion and the gradual development of a more sound and rational philosophy, it may still be recognized in many of the habits, prejudices, and ceremonial observances of the people, up to a recent period. In the course of time, however, a spirit of meditative inquiry was combined with the contemplation of nature. Metaphysical systems were devised by men of powerful faculties and cultivated minds; these were more reasonable, indeed, and better formulated, but still, for the most part, were founded upon no substantial basis. Because they were addressed only to men of superior understanding, they were incomprehensible, and therefore valueless, to the majority of mankind. At length,

In the fullness of time, Christianity superseded paganism; and although a considerable influence of ancient heathenism still remained incorporated with the popular acceptance of the new faith, yet the mind of man gradually became emancipated from the bondage of many erroneous conceptions—a more accurate observation of the phenomena of nature, and of their causal connection, ultimately led to more rigorous and more just methods of investigation and reasoning; and physical as well as intellectual science, at length renouncing the errors and hallucinations of premature speculation, ultimately cast off the shackles of superstition and fable:

> "The old fantastic faith had lost its power;
> The ancient gods were exiled from the earth."

<margin>term magi</margin>
It appears to be now universally admitted by the learned, that science and civilization had their origin in the Eastern regions of the earth, among the ancient Assyrians, Bactrians, Chaldeans, Babylonians, Egyptians, Hindus, Medes and Persians. Now, it is of some importance to observe, that, among these primitive nations of the world, the term MAGIC appears to have been employed to designate both natural and supernatural science—philosophy and religion—including principally, theology, astronomy, and medicine. The individuals who devoted themselves to these studies, and were presumed to have made the greatest proficiency in their acquisition, were denominated MAGI, or wise men—philosophers; the students and teachers

--- Page 82 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Magi as the original priest-philosophers of the East, exploring how their scientific knowledge was intertwined with religion and kept secret from the masses.</meta>

->A HISTORY OF MAGIC, etc.<-

...alike of natural and moral wisdom; the professors, the priests, and the prophets among the people. Since learning of any kind was a rare achievement in these early ages, these priest-philosophers were universally regarded with veneration and awe by the uninstructed and superstitious common people, who believed that their superior knowledge and talents could only be obtained through regular interaction and intimate conversation with certain superhuman beings. The study of nature, accordingly, among the early Eastern nations, thus became merged with religion, and both were considered the exclusive domain of the <term>MAGI</term>—the priesthood—by whom the knowledge thus acquired was combined with their devotional worship and ceremonial observances.

The origin of <term>MAGIC</term>, therefore, in its current meaning, must be traced back to the most ancient traditional records of the primitive nations of the world and the earliest dawn of human civilization. Babylon, Chaldea, Assyria, Bactria, Persia, Media, Egypt, and India were probably the cradles of infant science in early times—the primary seats of the ancient <term>MAGI</term>, and consequently of the primitive philosophy spread among mankind. <term>ZOROASTER</term>—a personage now difficult to identify as an individual—the Chaldean astronomers and soothsayers, the Egyptian priests, and the Indian Brahmins appear to have been the early guardians and teachers of this mysterious knowledge, which was considered too sacred to be communicated to the general public.

In all ages, knowledge may be said to be power, or, at least, to provide the most effective means of gaining and keeping control over the mass of the people; but this is especially the case in the infancy of human society, when learning and ignorance are separated by a wider gap, and when all science is generally believed to have a superhuman origin.

The <term>Magi</term>, therefore, in these early times, were held in the highest regard by mankind, as the revered keepers of all science, sacred and secular, and consequently as the mediators between earth and heaven, the interpreters of the divine will to the inhabitants of this lower world. Their social rank matched the dignity of their sacred functions. They were either themselves princes of the land, or the chief tutors and essential advisors to princes, as we learn from the Old Testament Scriptures, and from other ancient records. As their duties, however, were supreme, so were their responsibilities great and strict. The qualifications required of them, in addition to learning and practical wisdom, were a strict devotion to truth and justice, and a pure selflessness of moral character. From all that has been said, it would appear that the word <term>MAGUS</term>, in its original meaning, denoted both a philosopher and a priest—a lover and cultivator of all natural and moral wisdom. As in the primitive notions of mankind, all science was believed to emanate directly from above—from the immediate inspiration of divine power and wisdom—and was carefully preserved, from generation to generation, as the peculiar inheritance of the priesthood (the <term>MAGI</term>), the individuals of that consecrated caste were regarded not only as the special favorites of Heaven and the hereditary ministers of the national religion, but as the rulers, the advisors, and the physicians of the people; for even medicine itself was, in these times, regarded as a mystery, and consequently considered as a portion of the sacred science.*

## CHAPTER II.

In ancient as well as in modern times, **Magic**—or that type of learning and science which was thought to be beyond the reach of the common mind—was also believed to include the art of exercising powers which have always been considered supernatural. These included the gifts of divination and prophecy and the ability to work miracles, as was generally believed, upon other persons, whether present or at a distance. **Magic**, understood in this way, was diligently cultivated by its followers throughout the whole of the Eastern world. It formed the essence of the ancient mysteries in Egypt and in Greece, which we shall have occasion to speak of later; and it was spread, at a later period, by the Jewish sect of the **Essenians**, by **Pythagoras** and his disciples, and, subsequently, by the school of the **Neo-Platonists** at **Alexandria**. The supposed origin of this science, so generally prevalent at different periods of history, was clearly founded upon ignorance and the resulting misunderstanding of the actual powers and established laws of nature; and the belief itself was encouraged by those superstitious feelings which, to a certain extent, dominate the intellect in all ages, and are pecu-

liarly characteristic of barbarous and uncultivated times. **Magic**, indeed, when considered as a science transcending the limits of mere human acquisition, was a natural product of the infancy of learning and civilization. The extent of the powers of Nature, even in her more ordinary and obvious manifestations, could not yet have been generally ascertained and determined, much less accurately defined and correctly appreciated; and, consequently, all those more remarkable occurrences which surpassed the most familiar experience of life, or whose scientific causes the crude knowledge of the times was incapable of comprehending, were at once accounted supernatural and ascribed to the immediate intervening agency of the gods, or to that of beneficent spirits or malignant demons. Miracles, prodigies, and portents are things of frequent occurrence in the earlier ages of the world; but they become rare in proportion as science and civilization advance and dispel the darkness of mental vision. As a learned and eloquent author has observed, "the farther men advance into the light, the less apt are they to start."

But the exclusive possession of this mystical science by the priesthood—the <term>Magi</term>—in these crude times was natural enough and might, in some respects, have been beneficial, as it unquestionably constituted a powerful spring in the machinery of government. Hence the many abuses that have arisen, and the many atrocities which have been committed, at various periods, by individuals and by governments, in their absurd and insane attempts to enforce conformity with particular dogmas of faith, and to protect and promote the interests of the national religion by halting the progress of scientific knowledge; as if an ignorant and blind belief were preferable to an enlightened and reasonable conviction. Whenever excessive power has been conferred upon the priesthood, or gradually seized by that ambitious, influential, and, perhaps, irresponsible body—especially in the more advanced stages of society—it has been almost invariably abused to the harm and hindrance of truth, and consequently to the great disadvantage of the general community. The very sanctity of their calling, and the prestige of their divine authority, in the general estimation of the people, appear to free the members of that profession from all those responsibilities which act as a beneficial restraint upon the conduct of every other class of the people, and which tend to prevent them from abusing that power over the

Besides, as Lord Bacon and other distinguished philosophers have justly observed, every effort made in any stage of civilization to combine physical science with theology has consistently ended in giving us bad philosophy and worse religion. When confined within the proper limits of their respective spheres, there is really no necessity for any harsh collision between them. The foundations of the one rest upon veneration, faith, and hope; those of the other rest upon observation, experience, and reasoning. It would be clearly absurd to attempt to demonstrate a mathematical proposition by moral reasoning, or to prove the truth of a religious dogma or an ethical principle by mathematical demonstration—using the properties of the circle, the square, the triangle, or the hypotenuse. Faulty reasoning is equally harmful to religion and to science.

Accordingly—to whatever cause the circumstance may be attributed—like almost every other branch of human knowledge, early Magic, or supernatural science as it was then considered, along with all the practices resulting from its study and application, degenerated in later times. It ceased to be held in high regard among the influential classes, and it is alleged to have been frequently employed in subservience to the most dishonorable, dishonest, and dangerous
