## CHAPTER III.

The doctrine of the opposing and rival powers of God and the Devil is certainly of great antiquity, and was, we think, unquestionably derived from the ancient eastern superstition—from the tenets attributed to <term>ZOROASTER</term>, relating to the good and the evil principles, personified in the dominant spirits—<term>ORMUZD</term> and <term>AHRIMAN</term>.* It is extremely

---

<note>Footnote at bottom of page</note>
* <term>ROLLIN</term> in his *Ancient History* (Book 4, chapter 4) gives a very fair and impartial account of the religious doctrines of the <term>MAGI</term>. He adopts the opinion of <term>Dr. PRIDEAUX</term>, that there were two people named <term>ZOROASTER</term>, between whose lives there might be a distance of six hundred years. He observes that, throughout all the Eastern countries, idolatry was divided into two principal sects—that of the <term>SABEANS</term>, who adored images, and that of the <term>MAGI</term>, founded by <term>ZOROASTER</term>, who utterly abhorred images, and worshiped God only under the form of fire—"as the most perfect symbol or representative of the Deity." Their chief doctrine was, that there were two principles; one the cause of all good, and the other the cause of all evil. The former is represented by light, the other by darkness. The good God they named <term>YASDAN</term> and <term>ORMUZD</term>; the evil God, <term>AHRIMAN</term>. Now, it appears to us that the degeneracy and fall of man was induced solely by his contempt for the commands of his Maker; by the violation of those laws which were imposed upon him at the period of his creation. He was tempted by his own evil passions, and he forfeited his paradise. The whole history of his temptation and fall is obviously an allegory. The Devil, the tempter, consisted of the weakness and consequent disobedience of our first parents. The common notion of the Devil appears to have crept surreptitiously into religion, no doubt as a consequence of a misconstruction of certain figurative or metaphorical expressions in the sacred Scriptures.

One of the principal objects of the blessed advent of Jesus Christ, moreover, appears to have been to abolish this pernicious satanic doctrine, so derogatory as it is to the dignity and prerogatives of the only one Supreme Being—to overthrow the empire of the Devil in the minds of men, and to introduce a milder, a purer, and a more beneficent theology.

In these unhappy times, learning was indeed a very dangerous thing and frequently proved fatal to its unfortunate possessor. During those days of blind and bigoted zealotry, it was no easy matter for men of independent and philosophical minds to escape the accusation of heresy and its accompanying persecution. There were probably as many, or more, martyrs to scientific heresy than to orthodox Christianity.

The Christian zealots of every sect and persuasion were indeed deeply imbued with the spirit of a dark and virulent intolerance toward all differences of opinion regarding matters of religious belief. Everything was considered heresy which did not exactly accord, in the minds of the common devotees, with the prevailing doctrines of the day, while the powerful influence of a bigoted priesthood sanctioned and encouraged the most intolerant delusions and superstitions of the people. <note>* It would appear that in France, and probably in some other European countries during the 13th and 14th centuries, even the study of mathematics was pursued cautiously and in secret because of the dread of incurring the fearful accusation of dealing in sorcery. Indeed, the *Mathematici* were frequently associated with magicians and conjurors.

M. MONTEIL, in his *History of the French of Various Estates, etc.*, published in Paris in 1827, makes one of his imaginary characters, the Cordelier of Tours, express himself on this subject in the following manner: “I will not dissuade you from teaching mathematics, if you are determined upon it; but such instruction must be given with precaution and with prudence—that is, in a private room, without permitting geometrical figures, algebraic letters, or conjunctions to be traced on the walls or floors. No person's character should be endangered; one ought especially to guard against attaching the accusation of sorcery to anyone.”</note>

Such was the condition of learning prior to the religious Reformation, after which the human mind gradually recovered its elasticity and freedom.

In the first place, then, we would observe that for some time after the discovery of Mesmer, the phenomena of somnambulism and the ecstatic affections—which, as we shall see by-and-bye, were occasionally developed during the magnetic treatment—constituted something that was generally believed to be quite new, apparently miraculous, and rather questionable, if not entirely imaginary. These were seen as abnormal states of the organism in which the human mind was alleged to acquire certain peculiar, anomalous faculties, previously unknown and undreamt of, which enabled it, as it were, to carry on a communication with disembodied spirits and to acquire supernatural intelligence from otherworldly sources. Now, many of these notions regarding the nature of the discovery in question were notoriously incorrect; some of them were quite preposterous. It should have been well known to every physician, at least, if not to every philosopher, that the conditions in question were merely the result of certain pathological states of the human organism of rather uncommon occurrence. These states sometimes appear naturally, spontaneously, or without any immediately perceptible cause; they are not infrequently developed in particular diseases and in certain abnormal states of the nervous system, as well as by the application of artificial stimulants. The phenomena manifested in such circumstances were perfectly

In the second place, we may remark that these phenomena, although well known to the ancient philosophers and physicians, were, for a long period, considered much too sacred to be submitted to the undiscerning eyes of the common people, or to be made the subject of profane speculation. They were almost universally held to be the direct result of divine agency and were made subservient to the purposes of idolatrous worship. This last circumstance, indeed, brought the phenomena themselves into complete discredit among the early Christian converts in later times, who chose to consider them as resulting from the impious, deceptive, and diabolical practices of the heathen priesthood. Consequently, they viewed them as a main pillar of Paganism and, therefore, as a satanic obstacle to the recognition and spread of their own faith.

These false impressions, arising from the incorrect interpretation of certain well-known natural facts, continued to prevail for many centuries after the establishment of Christianity in Europe. At length, they gave rise to and supported the abominable delusions of witchcraft and its accompanying cruelties, which were spread and practiced to a most alarming extent—as shall be shown later—by the ignorant and ill-directed zeal of an infatuated priesthood. It is a curious fact, however, that the Roman Catholic clergy—much as they professed to be scandalized by the impious worship and idolatrous practices of the heathen world—condescended to borrow a fragment from the pagan creed; and ecstatic and nervous crises, having a natural or constitutional origin, or arising from a diseased or anomalous condition of the physical system, were, according to the peculiar nature and manifested symptoms of the affliction, reputed to be the immediate effects of divine agency or of satanic schemes. These were thought to be capable of being used for spiritual edification in the one case, or cured or alleviated in the other, by the invocation of the Deity, or of some patron saint, in their solemn prayers and exorcisms. In these dark ages, it seems never to have occurred to even the most learned among the clergy—or, at least, they carefully avoided the task—to conduct a philosophical investigation into the true psychological causes of these extraordinary phenomena, with a view to discover whether they might not have had their origin in the natural order of things; thus removing the necessity of constantly resorting to the immediate and direct intervention of God or of Satan, or to any other supernatural influence. Such an investigation, indeed, in those times, would probably have been considered sufficient to justify a charge of impiety.

Indeed, it preceded philosophy; and the earliest philosophy was of a poetical character. The more ancient poets were probably also the earliest philosophers.

*Sensibility* and *Intellect* appear to bear the same relation toward each other as *Instinct* and *Reason*; and each class of faculties and susceptibilities probably has its own peculiar seat and source in a different portion of the nervous system. The sensitive faculties, and instinct as well, are generally found to be predominant in the earlier periods of life, and especially in the female sex; the intellectual powers are more fully developed in people of mature age—the latter requiring exercise and experience to bring them to perfection. The same observation applies to the infancy and maturity of human society, as will become more apparent during the progress of the present investigation. The moral sense, and even religious feeling, both have their source in the sensitive system of the human economy, and both may be improved by judicious cultivation, or become depraved by neglect or improper treatment.

> ...system, the natural disposition of men was in some way ecstatic; in their actions, most appeared exuberant and inspired, while in their words they were inclined toward poetry and allegories; in all things, they were entirely suited to be led by imagination and the external appearances of things. They possessed minds endowed with the greatest religious feeling, but were for that very reason more superstitious; in most of their works, they were guided by a certain sudden instinct or rapture rather than by reason—not because of any contempt for reason, but from a lack of it. —M. CASAUBON, *On Enthusiasm*.

<meta>This page continues the author's exploration of human development, shifting from the psychological distinction between sensibility and intellect to the historical prevalence of magical influences in ancient societies.</meta>

<page-num>37</page-num>
<header>A HISTORY OF MAGIC, etc.</header>

Sensibility, indeed, and the predisposition toward what may be called magical influences, pervaded the whole of remote antiquity to an extraordinary degree. The sacred institutions of the early nations of the world were founded upon the basis of these influences as their principal support; the reputation of individuals was chiefly derived from their real or presumed possession of magical powers, and their oracles were considered divine. Mankind willingly submitted themselves to the dominion of those who were believed to exist in a state of continual communication with supernatural beings. The Jewish institutions were founded upon a theocracy; and all the other ancient nations of the earth had similar constitutions, although based upon a less rational system of supremacy. Even among the Jews, however, despite professing a purer theology, magical arts and influences—partly borrowed, no doubt, from the surrounding nations—prevailed to a considerable extent, as we learn from the Old Testament Scriptures. The ancient Egyptians were celebrated for their knowledge and practice of Magic. Among the Greeks, in the temples of ISIS, of APOLLO, of AESCULAPIUS, and of their other deities or deified mortals—where the ancient oracles were delivered and the sick were cured—ecstasy, somnambulism, or the "magnetic sleep" (as it is called in modern times) appears to have been diligently cultivated as an art. We have an abundance of evidence to establish this fact, which will be presented to the reader in the chapters that follow; and we shall have occasion to show that

the proceedings in these temples did not consist of mere disingenuous trickery and delusion, or other mean and disreputable practices, as many modern writers on the subject have represented with a view to discredit the whole system. The proceedings to which we allude were clearly founded upon a knowledge—from whatever source derived—of the effectiveness of certain artificial processes in producing those abnormal phenomena in the living organism to which our attention has more recently been directed by the practical Magnetists of the present day. In modern times, however, the natural instincts and sensitivities of the human constitution have been almost entirely overlooked and neglected; the study of psychical manifestations has been utterly abandoned; and all our educational efforts have been exerted in the training of the intellectual and mechanical powers. The former, therefore, might perhaps be appropriately called the age of instinct; the latter, the age of reason. In consequence of this altered tendency of mental cultivation, the study of the primitive powers and sensitivities of the sensory portion of the human constitution—which form a most important ingredient in the mixed nature of our species—has been almost entirely replaced by that of the purely intellectual processes and the formal deductions of reasoning. In general, we think it may be plausibly argued that there is more truly religious feeling and principle developed in the writings of some of those heathen philosophers than in the works of some modern authors who have lived and written under the influence of the Christian gospel. The Deity was deemed to be universally present in all the more remarkable phenomena of nature. Every extraordinary occurrence was attributed to the direct influence of divine power and wisdom in its production or revelation; and these phenomena themselves were regarded as direct manifestations of the divine will and pleasure to mankind. Religion, therefore, plays a most important part in the history of all the primitive nations of the world. Even their philosophy, in general, took on a theosophistic aspect. That this natural theology had a decided tendency to degenerate into idolatry in the minds of the common people, there can be no doubt; for the fact itself is historically proven by the crude opinions and absurd ceremonial observances of the people. The **Magi**, then, especially among the more ancient Eastern nations, as we have already observed, were the wise and learned men of their day and generation—the philosophers, the physicians, the priests and the prophets among their countrymen. As a consecrated caste, they were held in the highest esteem by all ranks of the people, and were consulted, even by the rulers of empires, in all cases of difficulty and national importance, as we learn from the Old Testament Scriptures, as well as from other sources. This led not only to the spontaneous manifestation of prophetic powers in individual instances, but to the establishment and endowment of many public institutions, specially dedicated to particular divinities, for the magical or magnetic cure of the sick, and the cultivation and evolution of the power of divination:—the Temples of Health, Oracles, etc. These celebrated institutions, at one time so flourishing, were, it is true, like all things human, liable to degeneracy and consequent abuse; but there is no doubt that, originally, they were pure and highly beneficial in their tendencies. It appears to us to be a great misconception of their origin and purposes to suppose, as many otherwise learned and distinguished writers on the subject do, that they were founded and conducted entirely on foolish, delusive, and impious principles. We have evidence of their having produced beneficial results while in a flourishing state; and at the same time that they are believed to have become corrupt, MAGIC itself, at first synonymous with knowledge and wisdom, had begun to degenerate—like many other ancient religious institutions and doctrines—into worldly trickery, mischievous superstition, and complete folly.

This most remarkable physiological or psychological condition, indeed, as we shall see later, was well known and much appreciated in ancient times; and in those days of theosophical simplicity, the explanation for it was attended with no difficulty. The Oriental sages, without resorting to any profound and elaborate philosophical investigation of the subject, at once attributed the apparently mysterious phenomenon to the immediate agency of the Deity or of celestial spirits; while the Jews attributed it to subordinate angels and demons, who were supposed to be of various ranks, endowed with different functions, and to hold familiar intercourse with the human race. A similar belief appears to have prevailed among the Greeks and Romans. Prophetic dreams and visions were frequently produced as a result of the artificial means employed for healing purposes in the ancient Temples of Health; and the different theories by which the learned men of those times attempted to explain the facts will be found in the recorded opinions of DEMOCRITUS, HERACLITUS, PLATO, the Neo-Platonists, etc. The last-mentioned class of philosophers, however, by mixing Platonic ideas with the Jewish Cabbala, unfortunately involved themselves, and consequently their disciples, in a series of mystical doctrines. These were subsequently spread throughout the Middle Ages of Europe and had a tendency to render the facts themselves obscure and suspicious; and these subtle speculations, although never held in high estimation by more modern schools, have not yet been altogether abandoned, nor have they entirely lost their influence over the speculations of a certain class of visionary theosophists, even down to our own times.

### CHAPTER VI.

AMONGST all nations, at all times, and, especially, at certain periods of extraordinary excitement,

<meta>This page continues the exploration of ecstatic and prophetic phenomena, moving from the philosophical theories of the Greeks and the Cabbala mentioned on the previous page to the psychological and cultural manifestations of these states across different religions and societies.</meta>

<page-num>46</page-num>
<header>A HISTORY OF MAGIC, etc.</header>

and, more particularly, during times of religious excitement, we can clearly trace the occasional, and sometimes very widespread, development of ecstatic phenomena—the effects of the sensitive faculties prevailing over the intellectual faculties. Independent of the immediate external causes of excitement, this psychological development may depend partly on natural predisposition and temperament, or bodily infirmity; and partly on education, climate, an addiction to mystical and ascetic habits, or other dominant influences; and these dispositions may ultimately become strengthened and confirmed into permanent states of the physical system.

To this latter class belong the religious enthusiasts, mystics, fanatics, and ecstatics of every age, every country, and every variety of form and character—the Brahmins, the Bonzes, the Fakirs, the Dervishes, the Israelite Prophets, and the Pagan and Christian Seers; whose revelations, foresight, divinations, and denunciations of every kind—frequently expressed in an elevated, poetic, or symbolic language and allegorical style—have often astonished and awed the multitude with their prophetic warnings, advice, commands, promises, and threats.

These phenomena, although similar in kind and sharing a common origin, have been found to be characterized by specific differences among different nations and at different periods; they are modified, no doubt, by situation and circumstances, and by peculiarities of temperament, education, and religious belief.

Passing over the subject of dreams and visions for the present, we would first direct the attention of our readers to the opinions of some of the most eminent authors of antiquity regarding the reality and probable nature of some of those phenomena of *Clairvoyance*, or lucid vision, whether natural or artificial. Although fully authenticated by many competent and credible observers, these have, perhaps quite naturally, excited the greatest amount of incredulity in the minds of modern physical philosophers; for there is a fund of extremely irrational and stubborn bigotry of unbelief in philosophical skepticism, just as there is in religious fanaticism. Many persons, even those of limited attainments, are easily induced to reject such facts as they cannot immediately explain based on their own arbitrarily assumed principles, however contracted and inapplicable they may be.

**CICERO**, as is well known, has written a particular treatise (*De Divinatione*), specifically directed to the question relative to the prophetic faculty, in which he begins by calling the attention of his readers to the universality of the belief in such a power—a belief which, we assume, must necessarily have been founded upon ample and direct evidence of the truth of the facts, although this belief may not always have been supported by the most correct and convincing philosophical reasoning. The ancients, indeed, do not appear to have always referred to the maxim that demonstrated facts are independent of all reasoning. *Ubi experientia constat, ratio peti non debet.*

**CICERO** proceeds to observe that there is no people, whether civilized or unrefined, among whom this belief has not, to a certain extent, prevailed; and he condemns the skepticism which would pervert or slander things so generally accepted, and corroborated by such ample and blameless testimony. (*Quæ est igitur calliditas, res vetustate robustas calumniando belle pervertere.*)

Over time, however, it appears that the number of skeptics gradually increased until, at length, **Cicero** himself—who was never particularly steadfast in his philosophical opinions and convictions—having evidently adopted the current free-thinking notions of his own time, rejected this doctrine altogether as unfounded.

At the beginning of our modern era, the more ancient belief revived again, although in a somewhat altered form. During the Middle Ages of Europe, and even until a late period, it prevailed to a great extent among European nations; while, in our own time, it appears to have been almost utterly abandoned again, as a result of the tireless efforts of skeptical philosophers and the materialistic tendencies of the age. Thus, it appears that in the earlier periods of ancient learning and philosophy, and also of Christianity, skepticism was the exception; whereas in the later periods of both, belief in the existence of the prophetic faculty was the exception to the rule.

**Plato**, in his *Phaedrus*, as is well known, distinguishes between two modes of divination—the one by means of the intellect, the other by inspiration. **Cicero** mentions that the Stoics also assumed two modes for the exercise of this faculty: *unum (genus) quod*

The latter—the immediate intuition of the future—was most highly valued by the ancients as the most pure and infallible—the more immediate and more precious gift of the gods. *Carent autem arte ii*, says Cicero, *qui non ratione aut conjectura, observatis ac notatis signis, sed concitatione quadam animi, aut soluto liberoque motu futura præsentiunt.*

remarks exclusively to the true prophetic faculty, as it has been developed in human beings at various periods.

In treating this subject, PLUTARCH employs the following beautiful and appropriate simile: Just as the sun shines not only when it emerges from the clouds, but always retains its splendor, although temporarily obscured from our sight by the vapors which surround it; in like manner, the human soul does not first receive the prophetic faculty only when it manifests through the body, but possesses it at all times—though obscurely, as it were, as a consequence of its current mixture with mortality. The prophetic faculty, then, being innate and imperishable in the soul, but only latent in the ordinary conditions of life, is capable of being excited by a superior power, or manifests itself freely and openly when, by any means whatever, the energy of the body has been diminished.

This is particularly the case in those states in which the soul apparently has the least connection with the body and is permitted to see into the internal essence of things. Such lucid intervals are more conspicuous in *sleeping and dreaming, and on the approach of dissolution.*

XENOPHON observes (*Cyrop. viii. 7. 21.*) that, in sleep, the souls of men appear to be more unfettered and divine, and are enabled to cast a look into the future; and JOSEPHUS remarks (*I. vii. 8. 7.*) that, in sleep, the soul, undisturbed, holds converse with the Deity, to whom it is related, and roams about every

where it is unconstrained and perceives future events. The spirit of prophecy, frequently manifested on the approach of death, was a phenomenon well known even in the most ancient times. This belief in the prophetic powers occasionally manifested by the dying was so prevalent in Greece that in Plato's *Apology*, Socrates is made to speak of it as a thing universally accepted. Cicero expresses himself to the same purpose, as do Arrian (*de exped. Alex. vii.*), Aretæus (*de caus. et sign. morb. acut., etc.*), and a great variety of other learned and distinguished writers.

In ecstatic states, whether occurring spontaneously or as a symptom in certain morbid or abnormal conditions of the human organism, the occasional manifestation of the prophetic faculty is a fact which has been generally recognized; it has also been noted in many cases of reputed insanity. The phenomenon has been attributed partly to immediate divine agency and partly to the operation of certain physical causes. This phenomenon was denominated by Plutarch, in his *Morals*, μαντικὸν ῥεῦμα καὶ πνεῦμα <note>A prophetic stream and spirit</note>. Plato speaks of the former species of prophetic *mania* in his *Phædrus*, and Pliny adduces several remarkable instances of cataleptic ecstasy. PLUTARCH considers it improbable that, in these circumstances, the human soul should, for the first time, acquire an entirely new power; but thinks it much more likely that this faculty always exists, although in an undeveloped state, and that the soul is only enabled to manifest it when no longer oppressed by the burden of the decayed members and corrupted humors of the body. ARETÆUS, and several other philosophical physicians, appear to have entertained a similar opinion; and the late accomplished Sir Henry Halford published an elegant treatise upon this subject of <term>moribund clairvoyance</term>.

described by Greek and Roman authors; and our modern physiologists—to whom it has generally proved a most embarrassing theme—have occasionally attempted to explain it using principles that are almost always unsatisfactory and, in many respects, incorrectly or gratuitously assumed.*

By the terms <term>Somnambulism</term> and <term>Noctambulism</term>, we denote a condition of a very peculiar nature, which may occasionally occur by day or by night. In this state, the patient, appearing to be in a profound sleep, performs a variety of actions requiring the utmost attention of a waking man; and this, as has been abundantly demonstrated by innumerable examples of the natural and artificial crisis, even when the patient is entirely deprived of the use of the natural organs of external sensation. The somnambulist walks or runs about with great freedom and confidence—he reads, writes, and performs, while in this state, the most difficult and dangerous feats, which no sane man would ever think of attempting. Numerous instances of the operations performed in these states have been cited in the author's for—

---

* It is quite remarkable that a phenomenon so curious in itself, and so frequently observed, should have attracted so little attention from philosophical minds for such a long period. This circumstance, indeed, can perhaps only be explained by the fact that the phenomenon itself was generally considered to be of a sacred character and, consequently, placed far beyond the limits of secular speculation. Even when it was examined, the attention of philosophers was principally directed to the phenomena displayed, rather than to the peculiar sensitive condition of the specific organs.

Aristotle observes that the development of the prophetic faculty is by no means an extraordinary occurrence in individuals afflicted with *melancholia*; and Cicero speaks of divination as being frequently manifested in different species of insanity. Indeed, both the Greek and Roman authors were accustomed to speak of ecstatic affections as a species of *mania*, to which, however, they ascribed a divine character. Plutarch and Pliny made similar observations, as did Aretæus, Galen, and many more modern writers. Idio-somnambulism, occurring in nervous affections, has been described by several of the most eminent pathologists.

It is not at all surprising that, previous to the great discovery of MESMER and the subsequent elucidation of the magnetic doctrine, occurrences such as we have alluded to should have been generally regarded as miracles—the immediate work of God—and that they should have been appealed to by enthusiastic religious sectaries as indisputable proofs of divine favor and of the orthodoxy of their own particular faith. The phenomena, it is true, were apparently very wonderful; and at those different periods, science had yet no means of providing a rational and adequate explanation for them. The facts themselves, indeed, were abundantly attested—they were notorious and undeniable; and as no natural cause could be assigned for their appearance, we cannot wonder that they were directly attributed to superhuman influences.

In the condition called St. Vitus's dance, patients sometimes experience brief visions of a prophetic nature, relating to themselves and others. A curious instance of this symbolic somnambulism is related in the *Blätter aus Prevorst*; and a similar case was observed by Dr. ENNEMOSER. The same phenomena occasionally occur in syncope <note>Fainting or a sudden loss of consciousness.</note>, and in states of apparent death. There is a curious and very interesting narrative of a case of this last description concerning the daughter of Montezuma in CLAVIGERO's *History of Mexico*. We shall probably have the opportunity to refer more particularly to some of these cases later on.

<page-num>65</page-num>
->A HISTORY OF MAGIC, etc.<-
VOL. I. F

In the female sex, especially, somnambulistic phenomena are very frequently developed at the age of puberty, and, in that case, they are generally regarded as ordinary symptoms of hysteria. In insanity, they often assume a more permanent form, constituting the lucid intervals, which are of a completely different character from the temporary delirium of fevers. *Ecstasy*, indeed, occurs very frequently in insanity, and, for this reason, those with mental illnesses have sometimes been regarded as Saints and Prophets. The Hebrew word *Nabi*—a prophet—also signifies an insane person; and the Greeks, too, used the word *Mania* to denote a state of inspiration. Therefore, PLATO affirms that much benefit may be derived from insanity.* The ordinary paroxysms of those in ecstatic states are temporary phenomena, which, in insanity, take on a more permanent form. Prophetic announcements of all kinds, relating both to the patient's own life and to the fate of other individuals, frequently alternate with fits of insanity and nervous excitement. (See PINEL'S treatise *On Mental Alienation*). The

* The theory of insanity—although its treatment appears to have been much improved in many respects in recent times—seems to still be very imperfectly understood. Medical professionals, in general, refer merely to the apparent physical causes and symptoms of mental derangement. The discoveries of Animal Magnetism, and particularly the study of ecstatic conditions, are undoubtedly destined to shed much new light upon this highly interesting subject. PUYSEGUR speaks of many insane persons as merely *disordered somnambulists*.

<meta>This page transitions from discussing insanity as a form of ecstasy to exploring the connection between the soul's language, artistic genius, and mental derangement, using literary references from Shakespeare and Dryden (attributed here to Pope) to support the argument.</meta>

<page-num>66</page-num>

The language of the soul in these states resembles inspiration and occasionally exhibits a symbolic character. In lunatic asylums, it is not unusual to hear songs sung in the purest dialect and with the most perfect intonation by completely uneducated people.

In one of those brilliant flashes of his powerful genius—in which he frequently exhibits the most profound intuitive understanding of human nature in all its various phases—SHAKESPEARE expressed in poetical language an idea, or rather a fact, which modern scientific investigation has demonstrated to be a general philosophical truth:

> “Lovers and madmen have such seething brains,
> Such shaping fantasies that perceive
> More than cool reason ever understands.
> The lunatic, the lover, and the poet,
> Are all composed of imagination.

> “The poet’s eye, rolling in a fine frenzy,
> Glances from heaven to earth, from earth to heaven;
> And, as imagination gives body to
> The forms of unknown things, the poet’s pen
> Turns them into shapes, and gives to airy nothing
> A local dwelling and a name.”

We may generally regard these states as symptoms of a predisposition to ecstatic states. Every person of original genius is, in fact, in certain respects, a *somnambulist* or a *clairvoyant*. Indeed, the close alliance between *genius* and *madness* is proverbial:

> “Great wits are so nearly allied to madness,
> That thin partitions divide their boundaries.”—POPE.

->*There is no great genius without a mixture of madness.*<note>*Nullum magnum ingenium sinè mixtura dementiæ.*</note><-

CICERO says, in *On the Orator* (Book II, no. 64): "No good poet can exist without an inflammation of the mind, and a certain inspiration of madness."<note>*Poetam bonum neminem sine inflammatione animorum existere, et sine quodam afflatu furoris.* (There can be no good poet without an inflammatory state of the mind, and a certain inspiration of madness [*furor poeticus*].)</note>—Hence the proverb: "We are born poets—we become orators."<note>*Nascimur poëta—fimus oratores.* (We are born poets—we become orators.)</note> A great orator, however, may be inspired—may be a genius, as well as a poet. We are inclined to think that Genius does not proceed from the cerebral portion of the nervous system—the head—the seat of the intellect; but from the ganglionic nerves—the seat of the instinctive feelings—of the sensitivity.

LUCRETIUS, the poet, was subject to fits of insanity. TASSO composed poetry during his most severe paroxysms; LEE, the dramatist, was subject to insanity; and BABOEUF is said to have written his best verses during the most violent delirium of fever. All great poets, too, have been regarded as prophets and seers; and the poetic *frenzy*, or *mania*, is a common expression. Some curious discussions upon this subject will be found in several of PLATO'S dialogues.

The poet, the painter, the sculptor, the musi-

The minds of all the most eminent artists, in every department, appear to have been in a state of enthusiastic rapture, or frenzy, while engaged in the composition of their most celebrated works. There is a holy inspiration, an enthusiasm of genius, which enables it to transcend the formal rules of art. This truth was recognized by that successor of the apostle Peter, when he inquired of GUIDO RENI, “into what heaven did you look, when you painted this angel?”—the Madonna. RAPHAEL said of himself and his productions—“A certain idea arises in my mind;—to this I hold fast, and endeavor to realize it, unconcerned about its artistic value.” He trusted, in short, to the inspiration of his own genius. In one of his letters, the same distinguished painter informs us that he could give no reason why his pictures should have assumed one form or another. “The world,” says he, “discovers many excellences in my pictures, so that I myself frequently smile when I find that I have succeeded so well in the realization of my own casual conceptions. But my whole work has been accomplished, as it were, in a pleasant dream; and, while composing it, I have always thought more of my object than of the manner of representing it. That I have a certain manner of painting, as every artist generally has his own—this seems to have been originally implanted in my

It is a well-known fact that DANNECKER, the Danish sculptor, finally obtained his idea of Jesus Christ upon the cross in a dream, after many unsuccessful efforts to realize it in his waking hours.

PLATO, in his dialogue entitled *Ion*, expresses his sentiments as one well aware of this distinctive character of genius. “All true poets,” says he, “speak not by art, but as persons inspired and possessed.” KANT, the celebrated German metaphysician, makes some remarkable observations upon the distinction between talent and genius in his *Anthropology*. Talent is partly inborn and partly acquired by exercise; Genius is altogether intuitive—instinctive.

Let us listen for a moment to the words in which MOZART describes his own state while engaged in the composition of his celebrated musical pieces. We use his own homely style. “When I am in good spirits and in the right mood,” says he—“for example, when traveling in a carriage, or walking, perhaps, during the night when unable to sleep—thoughts flow in upon me more readily and, as it were, in a stream. Whence they come and how, I do not know, and I have no control over them. Those which come upon me I retain in my head and hum them to myself—as others, at least, have told me. <meta>This page continues the quote from Mozart describing his intuitive musical composition process, which he experiences as a vivid, unified mental "feast" rather than a series of detached parts.</meta>

one thing, sometimes another, comes into my head to help make a piece of confectionery, according to the rules of counterpoint and the tone of the different musical instruments, etc. Now, this warms my soul, provided I am not disturbed. Then my mental work gradually becomes more and more extended, and I spread it out further and more clearly, until the piece really becomes almost ready in my head, even should it be of considerable length; so that I can survey it, in spirit, with a glance, as if I saw a beautiful picture or a handsome person before me; and I hear it in my imagination, not in detached portions, but, as it were, altogether, as a whole. Now, this is a feast. All my feelings and composition go on within me only as a lively and delightful dream. But to hear all this together is the best.”

Indeed, the poet, the painter, the sculptor, the musician—in short, every enthusiast in art—accomplishes his most striking performances in a state of intellectual transport—as if in an ecstatic dream; and he is himself ignorant of the *<term>modus operandi</term>*, and of the reasons for the excellence of the product. His intellect is overpowered by his genius. Inspiration produces masterpieces which the most laborious study can never attain.

All sleepwalkers and those in ecstatic states appear to be endowed with a peculiar intuitive power when in these states, and they describe their sensations at such times as uncommonly agreeable. The prophetic glimpses of the partially insane are also often very remarkable; these are occasionally manifested as immediate sensory intuitions, frequently expressed in symbolic language or representative action. **CLAUS**, the reputed fool, on one occasion entered hurriedly into the privy-council room at Weimar and exclaimed: “There you are consulting, no doubt, about very important matters; but nobody is thinking how the fire in Colmar is to be extinguished.” At this very moment, as was learned afterward, an alarming fire was actually raging in the town of Colmar.

**NICETAS GONIATES** relates, in his life of **ISAAC ANGELUS**, that when the emperor was at Rodostes, he paid a visit to a man called *Basilacus*, who had the reputation of possessing the faculty of seeing into the future, but who was otherwise regarded by all sensible persons as a fool. *Basilacus* received the emperor without any particular marks of respect and returned no answer to his questions. Instead of doing so, he walked toward the emperor’s picture, which hung in the apartment, scratched out the eyes with his staff, and attempted to strike the hat from his head. ### CHAPTER VIII.

That man, in the internal recesses of his organism, possesses a higher and more indestructible faculty—a soul, or spiritual essence—which is not always affected by the insanity of the other portions of his sensory and intellectual system; which, amidst the greatest aberrations of his mental powers, still preserves its higher and more independent vital energies, and, in lucid moments, and especially before death, shows itself elevated above the disorder of its physical instrument, exhibiting its still balanced, undisturbed internal harmony—indeed, even in defiance of a long period of stubborn insanity, it still continues to be capable of an expansion and exaltation of its natural gifts: all this is clearly demonstrated by many striking and relevant instances of the fact, which are, or ought to be, well known to all philosophical psychologists. On the present occasion, we shall only refer in detail to the following instructive case, which is related by Dr. STEINBECK in his learned and highly interesting work, entitled *Der Dichter ein Seher*:

A woman in Ukraine, after twenty years of continuous insanity, died in the year 1781. It had been previously remarked that, in her occasional lucid moments, she had exhibited a pious fortitude

in her sufferings, and a calm resignation to the divine will. Four weeks before her death, she, at length, awoke out of her long dream. Those who had previously seen and known her could now no longer recognize her as the same person; so enlarged and exalted were her intellectual powers, and so refined was her language. She expressed the most sublime truths with such clarity and internal lucidity as are seldom developed in common life. She was visited by many individuals on her deathbed, and all who saw her declared, that if, during the whole period of her insanity, she had been interacting with the most cultivated minds, her ideas could not have been more enlightened and comprehensive.

Among the abnormal manifestations of <term>Somnambulism</term>, which are somewhat similar to those of delirium and the visions of the insane, we may include the phenomena of all those anomalous states which either occur accidentally, or may be voluntarily produced by means of certain poisons. In these cases, we find a species of temporary intoxication, combined with mental exaltation, which are frequently succeeded by a greater or lesser degree of debility and stupor. Such effects are generally produced by all narcotics. VAN HELMONT, by the way, appears to have been very well acquainted with the various phenomena of the visionary and ecstatic states, and with the causes that operate in producing them. He was one of the first philosophers who seem to have anticipated the modern discoveries of MESMER and his disciples in magnetic science; and that accomplished physician gave explanations of the facts he elicited that were very similar to those which have been held and promoted by later magnetists. (See, in particular, his treatise entitled *Imago Mentis*.)

The state of *Ecstasis* constitutes the highest degree of what may be called the visionary life—the term visionary being used in a sense somewhat different from the ordinary meaning of the expression. This peculiar state of the organism may be produced either by constitutional causes, as a symptom of other morbid or abnormal conditions of the system, or—although perhaps more rarely—by the processes of Magnetism. In the most exalted degree of this extraordinary condition, too—whether it was induced by natural or by artificial means—recollection frequently remains in the waking state; there is still some species of connection, therefore, with the external world; and the body, while in this abnormal isolation, is generally endowed with greater vigor, and energy, and pliability, than in its ordinary condition.

Ecstasy very frequently assumes a religious form, or type, as in the saints, prophets, seers, and martyrs, under every species of devotional worship; and it may then become productive of the most extraordinary revelations, or the most fanatical delusions. The phenomena of this particular modification of the ecstatic condition have been generally attributed to divine influence, or to satanic agency, according to the peculiar modes of its manifestation; and this branch of the subject, therefore, might be considered as falling more appropriately into the province of the theologian, than into that of the philosopher; and, accordingly, such has generally been the case until now. But the theologians, we believe, have not been particularly successful in their attempts to explain the nature of these conditions; philosophy, with the assistance of psychological

->*Nec Deus intersit, nisi dignus vindice nodus.*<-

Examples of this peculiar species of the ecstatic condition (religious ecstasy) will be found in abundance in the lives of St. Francis, St. Anthony, St. Macarius, St. Bernard, St. Ignatius, St. Catherine, St. Bridget, St. Hildegard, etc.—in short, of almost all the distinguished Saints in the Roman Catholic calendar; and the actions and passions of these memorable personages have been carefully recorded and regarded by the orthodox as divine inspirations—as significant and decisive proofs of an immediate intercourse with the angelic world. The Protestants, too, are not without their ecstatics, although those on record are neither as numerous nor as remarkable as those belonging to the Catholic community. All of these cases, however, provide ample materials for magnetic history. But we shall have occasion to treat all of this at greater length in the following sections.

Indeed, cases of this description—occasionally varied in regard to some of the phenomena manifested by the various patients, especially in Catholic countries—are by no means very rare, although they are seldom publicly exhibited. The two last-mentioned ecstatics have been visited and described by the EARL OF SHREWSBURY, among others, to whose interesting publication on the subject, as it gives the orthodox Catholic view of the question, we beg leave to refer our readers. The similar case of A. K. EMMERICH, called the *Nun of Dulmen*, in Germany, has been noticed and amply described by a number of authors.

From ignorance of the true nature and real causes of these very curious states, or from the less creditable motive of conferring an adventitious support upon a system of superstitious worship, such natural ecstatics have been occasionally canonized and awarded a place in the calendar of saints; indeed, they have even been elevated, by pious zeal, into objects of religious adoration or invoked as intercessors at the throne of the Almighty. But in regard to all abuses of this description, passing over the obvious impiety, we may take the liberty of using similar language (*mutatis mutandis*) to that

The study of Animal Magnetism tends to dispel all those erroneous, fantastic, and harmful notions by attempting to explain the natural causes of the phenomena in question through physiological, psychological, and rational principles. In this respect, indeed, it may be of the most essential and beneficial use by preventing popular delusions and scientific difficulties, dispelling groundless and superstitious fears, and attributing all such phenomena to their appropriate natural causes. Such explanations cannot fail to be of service both to orthodox religion and to sound philosophy.

# CHAPTER IX.

It was a primary objective of the most ancient Magic to endeavor to discover the simplest and most effective means of affecting the organisms of other individuals, chiefly with the aim of curing diseases. The <term>MAGI</term>, as we previously observed, were not only the priestly caste but were also the earliest physicians. At a somewhat later period, when experience and research had gradually brought to light several of the more obscure powers of nature and their effects upon the living organism, this knowledge became liable to abuse in the hands of evil-disposed persons and was employed to achieve other mischievous and unlawful purposes. In the course of time, the science of Magic, in the hands of unworthy practitioners, degenerated from its original purity into a base and sordid art; and those who pretended to be proficient in this field of knowledge sought unholy means of exploiting the ignorance and credulity of the masses by pretending to cultivate an infamous alliance and wicked compact with the infernal powers of darkness. Hence arose *goetic* or false Magic, or the *Black Art*, which was always held in well-deserved disrepute among the truly learned and good. Even in the times of authentic prophecy, however, a distinction

was always made between the true **SEERS** and the vulgar miracle-mongers.

In consequence of the limited knowledge of the inherent powers of nature in early times, as has been already observed, and the gross ignorance and superstition of the great mass of the people, the operation of these powers was utterly incomprehensible to the multitude; and, hence, many phenomena, however familiar in themselves, were generally considered miraculous and attributed to the action of supernatural influences. The superstitious notions included in Pagan worship had their source in this cause. Even the magnetic cures, to which we have already alluded, were generally considered to be the immediate effects of a divine influence communicated to the priesthood—the sole mediators between mankind and the Deity. Faith, therefore, implicit faith, was held to be a necessary and indispensable condition for the beneficial efficacy of the means employed; and the magical, magnetic, or remedial virtues, supposed to be inherent in certain substances, were believed to be activated by the mysterious energy of the operator's will, strengthened by the use of certain words, prayers, incantations, and other ceremonies, which were preserved in the worship of the gods. To these remedial operations belonged the cure of diseases by the laying on of hands, by breathing, etc.—or by the use of talismans and amulets, the wearing of consecrated rings, and so forth. Hence arose a systematic treatment of diseases,

Our present generation of doctors and professors, however skillful in the mere technicalities of their art, and however learned in all the knowledge of a meager, material, and narrow-minded system of philosophy, are for the most part—or at least profess to be—complete skeptics and infidels in regard to the influence of any spiritual powers over the modifications and manifestations of the human organism. They endeavor to depreciate all devotional feeling by branding it with the label of mere mysticism; as if the value of facts could be annihilated by the use of contemptuous expressions. But when they make use of the term *mysticism*, we may well be permitted to doubt whether they really attach any intelligible and definite meaning to the term, or whether they have not resorted to it for the purpose of concealing their own ignorance and incapacity. To the true philosopher, the entire universe of matter, thought, and feeling may be said to be, in one sense, a great complex of mysticism, which cannot be comprehended by the human mind without adopting the hypothesis—if it be but an hypothesis—of some great spiritual influence under divine direction and the control of inscrutable power and wisdom, constantly pervading, activating, and governing every portion of the entire system. properly speaking, it had no essential connection; as, for example, the doctrine of ANAXAGORAS regarding eclipses, which, like many other branches of science, was originally proposed in secret from the fear of offending the dangerous prejudices of the common people, who could not easily be brought to recognize the distinction between proximate and ultimate causes. Even the divine PLATO himself, according to his own confession, put forth his peculiar doctrines in the names of other individuals in order to avoid a similar responsibility. SOCRATES—that "old man eloquent"—fell victim to his sincerity.

In later times, the term MAGIC was brought into discredit for various reasons. The science itself came to be considered a relic of Paganism—a remnant of heathenish divinity—and, as such, it was violently denounced by Christian converts during the barbarous ages of Europe. The stigma then attached to the name has not yet been entirely erased, even in the present more enlightened times. In the very mildest sense, ancient MAGIC is still regarded by many as a mere system of trickery and deception. To what extent it deserves this degradation, we shall have occasion to inquire later.

character they occasionally manifest. HIPPOCRATES wrote a treatise, *De Insomniis* (On Dreams), of which an abstract was drawn up by JULIUS CAESAR SCALIGER, the elder. The following is a short summary of the opinions held by that most eminent of the ancient physicians upon this curious subject:—

> “After the soul has become emancipated, not entirely from the body, but from the oppressive thraldom of its grosser parts, it withdraws into itself, as into a harbor of refuge, in order to protect itself from external storms. It there sees and recognizes everything that takes place in the interior of the body, and represents this state in different figures and colors, and thus explains the particular condition of the corporeal frame.”

In the third book of his treatise *De Vita* (On Life), he repeats this statement in the following words: 

> “The soul sees everything that takes place in the body, even with closed eyes.” 

SCALIGER observes that Galen, and other philosophical physicians, not only recognized this faculty of the soul in order to take advantage of it in their medical practice, but even considered it as something divine. GALEN, indeed, makes use of almost the same expressions as HIPPOCRATES in order to designate the prophetic character of dreams. In sleep, he says, the soul retires into the innermost part of the body, abandons all external operations, and points out everything connected with the corporeal functions; and, in relation to itself, it sees everything as actually present. GALEN also confesses that he derived some portion of his own practical knowledge from the accurate observation of such phenomena. Hence, it would appear that these ancient physicians and philosophers were quite well acquainted with some, at least, of the more remarkable phenomena of sleep and dreams, and even with many of the characteristic features of the somnambulistic or ecstatic states. A great deal of the professional knowledge and tact possessed by GALEN, indeed, may, no doubt, have been derived from actual personal experience of diseased action; but his *prognoses* were sometimes of such singular acuteness and exactitude that they can only be explained by assuming the existence of an internal magnetic instinct. Thus, for example, he foretold that the senator, SEXTUS, who was at that time in perfect health, would, on the third day thereafter, be attacked by fever, which would abate on the sixth day, return on the fourteenth, and finally leave him on the seventeenth day, as a result of a general perspiration—all of which was verified by the event. The physicians wished to bleed a young Roman who lay sick with a fever; but GALEN remarked that this was unnecessary, because the patient would be relieved in a natural manner by losing a sufficient quantity of blood through the left nostril, and would thereafter recover, which actually happened.

Some of these ancient sages considered it no more wonderful that man should be able to foresee the future than that he should be capable of recalling the past. They held that both faculties were originally implanted in our human constitution and called into action under their proper conditions. CICERO, in his treatise *De Divinatione* <note>On Divination</note>, has given us a reasonably satisfactory summary of the opinions of the ancients regarding this entire interesting subject, illustrated by many relevant and curious facts.

### CHAPTER XI.

Magic, as we have already observed, had its principal seat, and became most universally diffused, among the primitive Oriental nations. Of the history and phenomena of this early science we shall soon have occasion to speak at some length; but, in the meantime, we may take the opportunity of observing, that it is impossible to conceive how that history, and these phenomena, can be correctly appreciated, without keeping steadily in view the great modern discovery of **MESMER**, and the labors of his most ingenious successors in the magnetic art. Without some such preparation, indeed, we may become acquainted with the facts, but we cannot be in a fit state to appreciate their nature, or their scientific value. Animal Magnetism, it is true, may not be found capable of providing us, at once, with the means of adequately explaining the whole series of those curious psychological phenomena which are presented to us by history—more especially when we consider that this doctrine is, in itself, in many respects, still a philosophical enigma; but, in consequence of this most important discovery of **MESMER**, the facts themselves have, un-

In the early records and traditional history of the primitive Eastern nations, we find the most numerous instances of the manifestation of those peculiar states of the human organism in which the phenomena of somnambulism, ecstasy, clairvoyance, prophecy, etc., are most prominently, most conspicuously, and most frequently developed. In the course of time, magic became distinguished into two kinds—the *theurgic* or celestial, and the *goetic*, or demoniacal—according to whether the devotee was supposed to invoke and do homage to the beneficent or to the malevolent being—to the Spirit of Light, or to the Spirit of Darkness—to God or the Devil. It appears, indeed, to have been almost universally held in the most ancient times that mankind was placed in intimate connection with a supersensible world, which was governed by the antagonistic powers of a good and an evil principle; and that this connection between the sensible and the supersensible world was indirectly maintained through the means of intermediate agents, who were always ready to offer their services at the summons of their respective followers. The pure original idea of MAGIC, as the profound study of nature and of the power, wisdom, and benevolence of the Deity they worshiped, was gradually lost sight of; and the first of sciences, at length, degenerated into the practice of absurd and superstitious arts and brutalizing ceremonies—as may be observed even in many of the Jewish rites. Even as early as the time of Zoroaster, indeed, MAGIC had begun to be thus deteriorated and abused, as appears from the books of the Zendavesta. Hence, as we have already observed, it ultimately came to be distinguished into the *white* and the *black* art. The most ancient theological books of the Hindus, which some learned men have considered to be the earliest secular records of the human race, are the *Vedas*, or the Brahminical revelations, and the Code of MENU. These books contain the theological notions of this very ancient and remarkable people, their philosophical doctrines, and a continual reference to those magical or magnetic states of the soul in which it was supposed to be separated from the body and to hold immediate communication with the original source of all intelligence. These ancient doctrines, narratives, and expositions were for a long time regarded by the modern world as empty mystical fables or, at most, as inscrutable mysteries or fanciful and extravagant inventions—until, in these later times, physiology finally provided the means for a more adequate explanation of their true meaning and genuine purpose, and a careful comparison with recent

They were believed to exist upon the mercy of God in a state of fasting and strict self-denial, continually immersed in profound contemplation. They would thus continue for hours, absorbed in a state of the deepest ecstasy and deprived of the use of their internal senses, contemplating the Deity as a pure, white, clear, and inexpressible light. *Soma-drink* is prepared, in part, from the juice of the *Asclepias acida*, or *Cyanchum viminale*, which makes up the main ingredient of the potion. This juice is pungent and intoxicating. In larger doses, it may prove poisonous; and, in many cases, the nervous system is affected by it in a similar way as by the use of other narcotics. WINDISCHMANN observes that, in more ancient times, the *Soma-drink* was taken as a holy act—a type of sacrament; and that, through this means, the soul of the communicant became united with Brahma. It is frequently said that even PARASHAPATI partook of this juice—the essence, as it was called, of all nourishment. In human sacrifices, the *Soma-drink* was prepared with magical ceremonies and incantations, by which means the virtues of the lower and higher worlds were supposed to be incorporated into the potion. Mention is also made of opium, which was likewise intended to promote a stupefying sleep and ecstatic visions. KAEMPFER mentions that, after having taken a preparation of opium in Persia, he fell into an ecstatic state in which he imagined himself to be flying in the air, beyond the clouds, and associating with the inhabitants of the celestial spheres. PROSPER ALPINUS also relates that, among the Egyptians, dreams of paradise and celestial visions are produced by the use of opium.

state. The state of waking in the external, sensory world provides no true knowledge of things. Ignorance and illusion predominate as a consequence of external contemplation and the influence of the animal passions. This, therefore, is a state of darkness. In sleep and dreaming, the solar influence is manifested in phantasms. This state may be compared to the twilight. The ecstatic sleep first develops the light of true knowledge; and the real, internal waking state presents a contemplative vision of objects inaccessible to ordinary natural sight. The internal eye of the soul is opened, and the sight is no longer sensory and confused; but there is a clear-seeing (*Clairvoyance*), an accurate seeing, a thorough seeing of the whole magic circle, from the circumference to the center. This ecstatic sleep, however, has different gradations of internal wakefulness and lucidity.

Here, then, we have a pretty accurate description of the somnambulism of the modern Magnetists, nearly in their own language, from the lower states of *sleep-waking* to the higher *Clairvoyance*.

According to the narrative in the *Upanishad*, one of the ancient Indian philosophers gave the following answer to a question relative to waking and dreaming, and the seat of the ecstatic state. When the sun sets, his rays retire into the center, and, in like manner, the different physical senses withdraw into the *Manas*, or great common sense. The individual then sees nothing, hears nothing, tastes and feels nothing, etc., and becomes absolutely

But when the latter are withdrawn into the region of the heart, they melt into unity—*they become one common sense*; the individual attains his true personality in the light of these <term>*Pranas*</term>; and while the doors of the body are closed, and he is in a state of profound sleep and physical insensibility, he becomes internally awake, and enjoys the fruit of the knowledge of Brahma daily, during the continuance of this blessed sleep. He then sees anew, but with different eyes, all that he did in his ordinary waking state; he sees everything together, visible or invisible, heard or unheard, known or unknown; and because <term>*Atma*</term> (the pure spirit) is itself the originator of all actions, he likewise performs, in his sleep, all these actions, and returns to his original form. In order to attain this elevated point, the senses and desires must be closed up, and, in the interior of the body, this power must enter into the <term>*vena portæ*</term>, and prevent the flow of the bile: for the <term>*Manas*</term>, at such periods, binds up this vein, which is the passage of physical passion, and the sleeper then sees no more fantasies, but becomes wholly spirit (<term>*Atma*</term>), luminous,

From the preceding observations, when stripped of the mystical phrasing of the Eastern sages in which they are wrapped, it is impossible, we assume, not to recognize a full and distinct knowledge of the phenomena of the ecstatic state, as well as its causes. Even from the remark made regarding the *vena portæ* and the influence of biliary secretion, we may infer insights that are not shallow regarding the physiology of this extraordinary state. In their explanations of this obscure subject, too, the Eastern philosophers assigned considerable importance to the influence of the sun and moon—an influence whose reality has been recognized in modern times and demonstrated by a variety of striking and authentic facts.*

The faculty of divination, occasionally manifested by individuals in the states described above, was a phenomenon well known to these Eastern sages. They were also aware, as are modern magnet-

But the experimental research of the magnetists in our own time has enabled those who have studied the subject of <term>Animal Magnetism</term> to understand the language and appreciate the doctrines of the Oriental philosophers. The Indian or Brahminical philosophy, too, is intimately interwoven with Eastern theology and mythology; anyone who would comprehend the former must necessarily devote themselves to the study of the latter. Here, too, as in other sciences, we must look past the symbolic and mystical character of the language in which these peculiar doctrines and speculations are embodied; we must also endeavor to acquire an adequate knowledge of the particular facts upon which their doctrines and speculations are founded.

The religion and philosophy of the ancient Hindus became the special inheritance of a particular caste or sect—the Brahmins. These have been transmitted down from age to age by the most learned and accomplished individuals of that sacred body, mingled, probably, with many of those natural and inevitable corruptions with which the passage of time generally disfigures all ancient dogmas and renders them, in their literal interpretation, more or less unintelligible to the modern scholar. The substance of these dogmas, however, has been

His Hindu friend encouraged him to proceed along that virtuous path which would lead him to wealth and honor in this world, and finally lead him to eternal happiness. To reinforce these teachings, he assured him that he would gradually rise from the position he then held at Cambay to other residencies and lower chief positions in the Company’s service; he predicted that he would then succeed to the higher appointment of chief at Tellicherry and Surat, and would end his Indian career as the governor of Bombay. Since Mr. Hodges had not been sworn to secrecy, he spoke of these Brahminical predictions among his associates and friends from the time they were first shared; their author was generally called "Mr. Hodges’s Brahmin." For some years, these predictions made but little impression on his mind. Afterward, as he gradually climbed the ranks of the Company’s service, he placed more confidence in his Brahmin, especially as he approached the peak of his ambition and found himself chief of Surat—the position next in wealth and honor to the governorship of Bombay.

The Indian philosophers, too, appear to have been well acquainted with the processes of magnetic manipulation; for we find it frequently mentioned, or alluded to, in their writings. The Jesuit Missionaries, indeed, appear to have learned this practice from the Brahmins.

The same magical or magnetic knowledge seems also to have been diffused, to a certain extent, among the Chinese, and that, too, from a remote period. KIRCHER and other early travelers and residents among this ancient people inform us that, from the most remote times, diseases were cured in China by means of manipulation, breathing, and other simple processes. Similar practices appear to have been in use among the Chaldeans, the Medes, Persians, and Babylonians—indeed, throughout the whole of the ancient Eastern world.

## CHAPTER XIII.

The <term>*Dualism*</term> of <term>*Zoroaster*</term> became incorporated, in different forms, with the ancient theological and metaphysical dogmas of the Hindus, the Chaldeans, the Egyptians, and the Israelites. According to PLUTARCH, the Chaldeans assumed two good and two bad deities, along with their attendant spirits. Indian <term>*Dualism*</term> was of a milder character than that of the Parsi; but their <term>*Demons*</term> and <term>*Genii*</term>—their good and evil spirits—were no less numerous than those of the Chaldeans and Babylonians. This is evident from their <term>*theosophic*</term> systems, their poetical productions, and mythological traditions. From Babylon, the Jews, after their captivity, appear to have brought back to Canaan Eastern <term>*Magic*</term>, <term>*Theurgy*</term>, and <term>*Demonology*</term>, which afterwards became essentially, but only partially, incorporated with Christianity; although, in the New Testament Scriptures (John 3:8) we are told that one of the chief objects of the mission of JESUS CHRIST upon earth was to destroy the empire of Satan and the demons, to annihilate the doctrine of devils, and to restore the undivided empire of the one supreme GOD. We shall see, in due course, how the writings of the Alexandrian Jews and the spread of Christianity subsequently contributed, indirectly, to revive and even to extend the previous Oriental belief, and to modify the doctrine of MAGIC.

All of these theosophic and demonic notions lay at the foundation of ancient **MAGIC**, or sacred science, and ought to be carefully separated from the facts it embraces. For these ancient <term>theosophists</term> also studied and claimed to practice the pretended art of holding conversation with spirits, and of rendering them favorable and subservient to their own wishes and designs. The artificial means employed to accomplish this object were, among others, the use of certain narcotic substances, such as opium, the juice of hemp seed, <term>stramonium</term>, <term>henbane</term>, etc., in certain doses; and these means, it is believed, have been preserved to the present day among the Persians, Arabs, Turks, and, generally, throughout the Muslim tribes. The phenomena produced by these means are, in many respects, similar to those which are frequently the result of <term>magnetic processes</term>, although infinitely more harmful and, in most cases, highly injurious to physical and mental powers.

Among the Persians, as in other countries, the *Maguse* (*Magicians*, *Magi*) presided over the priestly office, and <term>MAGIC</term>, as we previously observed, thus became combined with religious worship. <term>PLATO</term>, in his *Alcibiades*, informs us that the “Kings of Persia learn <term>MAGIC</term>, which is a worship of the gods.” <term>MAGIC</term>, therefore, in those ancient times, referred to everything which was supposed to relate to human and divine science—to medicine and to philosophy, as well as to religious worship.

The visionary and ecstatic states, to which we have already referred, are frequently observed in a uniquely modified form among the current inhabitants of certain parts of Asia—as, for instance, among the Siberian Shamans, the Arabian Dervishes, the Samoyeds, and the Laplanders, as well as among the Hindus. In the most ancient period of Egyptian history, we find medicine, theology, and religious worship combined in the profession of the priesthood; the first, indeed, to such a degree that it appears to have occupied as much of their attention as the latter, if not more. For in Egypt, we find the first regular practice of therapeutics incorporated, as it were, for thousands of years with their religious ceremonies and observances. In the treatment of the sick, they appear to have carefully watched what they conceived to be the messages of their deities; and for this purpose, the patients themselves were artificially prepared to receive and declare them. They also devoted themselves to the study of medicine and the practice of the healing art. Indeed, their mode of treatment appears to have been exceedingly simple and in harmony with nature—consisting, principally, of a few general procedures and dietary observances. They did not possess that immense quantity of liquid and solid remedies—those salts, earths, gases, vegetable and mineral poisons, etc.—which fill our pharmacopoeias and are so plentifully dispensed by the empirical practitioners of medicine in modern times. Yet, according to all accounts, their practical methods were remarkably successful. Their treatment appears to have consisted, principally, of bathing, anointing, manipulations, fumigations, etc. By these means, combined with exercise and fasting, the patients were prepared, in the temples, for those prophetic dreams for which the oracles became so famous. The officiating priests oversaw these prophetic manifestations in their sleeping patients, and, upon their waking, suggested the remedies predicted during the prophetic sleep and the probable outcome of the particular case; a circumstance which led to the mistaken belief that the priests were themselves the prophets. It is extremely probable, indeed, if not absolutely certain, that in most, if not in all cases, these predictions originated from the patients themselves.

The Egyptians adored her as the discoverer of many remedies, indeed, even of the science of medicine itself. The temples of Isis were the most famous for the resort of patients, who, during their sleep, received her oracular directions for their cure. Her priests were generally called **ISIACI**.

**HORUS**, the son of Isis, is said to have learned the arts of medicine and divination from his mother, and was called by the Greeks **APOLLO**. (*Horus is interpreted as Apollo, who was taught the art of healing and divination by his mother Isis, etc.*) **SERAPIS** was another no less celebrated deity, who had many temples built for him in Egypt, and also in Greece and in Rome. In all of these temples, medicine and divination were practiced, along with the worship of the gods.

In regard to the proceedings in these temples, especially in relation to the treatment of the sick, we possess very imperfect accounts. The uninitiated were strictly excluded from them, and those actually initiated appear to have faithfully kept the secret. Even those among the Greeks who were fortunate enough to obtain admission into these temples have remained silent on the subject of the mysteries, and have passed down to us only a few scattered hints. **JABLONSKI** observes that only a few select members of the priesthood were admitted into the inner sanctuary, and that foreigners were

## CHAPTER XV.

When we compare all the fragmentary accounts that have been collected regarding these ancient Egyptian institutions, we must certainly be convinced that the treatment of the sick and the responses of the oracles in the temples were very similar to the magnetic processes of modern times. The influence of the doctrines of Christianity produced a significant change in the previous beliefs regarding MAGIC. At the time of the coming of **JESUS CHRIST** to the earth, the belief in demons and malignant spirits was universally widespread, not only among the heathens, but also among the Jews. To these demons and spirits, an unholy and almost unlimited power over this earthly world was ascribed, rivaling, opposing, and even almost surpassing that of the Deity Himself; and extending not only over the spiritual world, but throughout the entire domain of

---

* For my own part, I am free to confess that I have always felt great difficulty regarding the subject of miracles, and I strongly suspect that many other respectable people are in the same situation as I am. Some German theologian has said: *The argument derived from miracles is not suitable; since we cannot distinguish true miracles from false ones by any certain argument.* <note>Latin: Argumentum a miraculis petitum non est conveniens; cum vera miracula a falsis nullo certo argumento discernere possumus.</note> How, with our limited abilities, can we discover whether a particular event is natural or supernatural? What is the infallible test of a miracle?

The belief in the necessity of specific miracles, as proofs of the existence of a Supreme Being, may be compared to the notions of children, who show little surprise when a perfect piece of machinery is shown to them as the work of an artist—such as a clock or timepiece with its regular movements and striking of the hours; but who are inclined to love and admire the clock-maker when he stops the machinery, or produces some extraordinary irregularity in its action, as often as he pleases, or as the child desires it. It is not perfection, but imperfection, which, in their minds, creates veneration for the artist.

By any means, lawful or unlawful, they endeavored to direct these harmful influences toward others or to procure supernatural aid for the purpose of promoting their own selfish goals.

MAGIC, in short, had completely degenerated from its original uses into what has been called the *Black Art*, falling further and further away from its primitive, lofty purposes and essential dignity into a base and sordid profession.

The Supreme Ruler of heaven and earth was no longer seen as the God of universal nature—the sole disposer of all earthly events—but only as the God of the Jewish people. He was worshiped only because he was believed to be more powerful than the gods of other rival nations and more capable of making them victorious in their career of conquest.

We are told, indeed, that one of the chief objects of the blessed arrival of the Savior upon earth was to annihilate the works, frustrate the designs, and overthrow the kingdom of Satan throughout the universe; to illuminate the spiritual darkness of a benighted world; to substitute truth for falsehood and delusion, and faith for superstitious fear; and to introduce confidence in the decrees of heaven and the love of God and of our neighbor, instead of doubt, despair, and hatred.

In this view—even apart from the divine character of his per-
