### CHAPTER XVII.

Among the Greeks, magic and demonology had a similar origin as among the ancient Eastern nations, from whom much of their learning, philosophy, and theology appear to have been derived, although manifested in a somewhat different form, corresponding with the unique character of the people. Here, too, magic, in its best and most original significance—*white* magic—long preceded that pseudo-science, *black* magic. This latter spurious art was designated by the Greeks by the name of γοητεια, which, unquestionably, was a bastard science and, in fact, merely the illegitimate offspring of the former, patronized by the common people and never held in high regard by the truly learned.

The knowledge of the real and beneficial influence of the spiritual powers and sensitivities implanted in the human constitution—the phenomena of instinctive or ecstatic *clairvoyance*—was, indeed, at all times unknown to the uninitiated public, and appears to have been confined exclusively, or at least in a great measure, to the priesthood—the Magi—by whom it was carefully cherished and preserved—and to the select few whom they conde-

scended to initiate into their sacred mysteries. Hence the erroneous notions that came to be held by the bulk of mankind, in all ages, on this very curious subject.

It is certain that the celebrated mysteries at Samothrace reach back into a very remote antiquity; and it appears equally clear that the earliest and most genuine MAGIC was nothing else than a species of Natural Philosophy, combined with religious worship. Almost all the modern writers on this subject appear to have associated the mysteries in the ancient temples with demonology and witchcraft; an opinion which, if not actually originated by them, was, at least, diligently spread by the early Christian converts; while many eminent authors, in more recent times—even the learned and shrewd, but frequently negligent and superficial, and certainly not very philosophical DEFOE—appear to have carelessly adopted the common belief, and to have confused these sacred ceremonies with the absurd and superstitious art, as it was then called, of summoning the Devil. But all such extravagant and unwarranted opinions clearly arose from the neglect of proper investigation, or from willful misrepresentation. The establishment of the mysteries in question appears to have been intimately connected with the development of the national religion. That religion may have been, and certainly was, imperfect, unsound, and perverted; but it is unfair to contrast these early aspirations with the subse-

quent and purer institutions and tenets of Christianity, which were not in existence for a long period afterward.

The ancient priesthood, as we have already shown, were the curators or conservators of the sacred dogmas, the religious worship, and the ceremonial observances of the ancient world. We cannot doubt that they alone were in possession of all the higher knowledge of those times, which they consecrated to the service of the gods and carefully guarded from profanation. Meanwhile, they endeavored to conceal its mystical application from the untutored minds of the common people; and, hence, the latter were accustomed to regard these unappreciated ceremonies and their unintelligible phenomena as not only mysterious, but magical, in the more depraved sense of the term—an opinion which has been generally passed down to our own times. The rationale of this opinion is abundantly obvious. The God of the Christians could not be the God of the Pagans; and as the former was the only true God, the latter must, of necessity, be a false god, or the Devil. Therefore, the mysteries in the heathen temples must, *ex hypothesi* <note>according to the hypothesis</note>, have consisted of an unhallowed worship of the Devil and the cultivation of diabolical arts and enchantments. The reasoning here is, evidently, not the most correct, nor the conclusion the most logical; but it appears to have satisfied the minds of most of the primitive Christian fathers.

But all this does not warrant the hypothesis of any invocation of the Devil, or any cooperation of diabolical agency; on the contrary, the whole of this apparent mystery is capable of being satisfactorily explained by perfectly natural causes, without the necessity of constantly having recourse to the hypothesis of any extraordinary divine or demonic aid.

ORPHEUS and MUSÆUS, his pupil and successor, are said to have been the original founders, if not the actual inventors, of these Pagan mysteries; and, therefore, they have generally been considered as the original representatives of the most ancient natural philosophers among the Greeks, and the true authors of temple worship and religious ceremonies. ORPHEUS, indeed, like most of the alleged instructors of barbarous times, appears to have been, in a great measure, a fictitious and mythological character; and, as is usual in such circumstances, many romantic, improbable, and even impossible actions, have been liberally ascribed to him: *Omne ignotum pro magnifico* <note>Everything unknown is taken for magnificent</note>. He is represented to have been a prophetic poet, who flourished before the Trojan war; and he is re-

PYTHAGORAS is almost equally celebrated as a philosopher and a mystic. From the ancient accounts of this remarkable man, he would appear to have been initiated into all the mysteries of the Egyptian MAGI. He was the founder of a particular and very celebrated philosophical school, and had many eminent disciples and followers. But, upon the present occasion, it is unnecessary for us to enter into any general discussion of the unique principles of his philosophy, which have already been investigated by many other authors and, besides, constitute a subject rather foreign to our immediate purpose. It is probable, however, that these mysteries ultimately became involved in the general corruption of the national religion; and that their original purity gradually became defiled by the mixture of a common demonology, and a tendency toward the adoption of more profane and unholy practices. Hence the different, and, in many respects, contradictory representations of the character and objects of these mysteries, and the resulting difficulty of distinguishing and appreciating their true nature. This much, however, appears certain—that, although the original MAGIC was gradually replaced by the common arts of sorcery, yet the latter were long held in deserved contempt and abhorrence by the more cultivated minds of the rational devotees; and that the practice of these ignoble arts was utterly rejected and condemned by all the learned, upright, and orthodox followers of the science.

We shall see more particularly later how this fusion, or combination of the ecclesiastical and therapeutic functions, came to be perpetuated in the Christian monasteries.

The celebrated men among the ancients who are said to have traveled in pursuit of knowledge to Egypt and Asia—such as **Pythagoras**, **Democritus**, **Plato**, etc.—fell under the suspicion of having brought back with them a knowledge of the magical arts, just as a similar imputation was attached in modern times to **Roger Bacon**, **Albertus Magnus**, **Galileo**, and, indeed, to all those philosophers who surpassed the average standard of genius and achievement in their respective ages. Theology and theosophy have, in all ages, maintained a constant and deep-rooted warfare with natural philosophy. Hence, MAGIC (or science) and Atheism (or religious heresy and infidelity) came to be associated together; and both have been included under the same condemnation, as was observed by **Apuleius** in his treatise *De Magia*: "Indeed, these charges are commonly brought against philosophers through a general error of the unlearned. They consider those who investigate the simple and pure causes of physical bodies to be irreligious, claiming they deny the gods—as with **Anaxagoras**, **Leucippus**, **Democritus**, **Epicurus**, and other defenders of the laws of nature. On the other hand, those who more carefully investigate the providence of the world and more fervently celebrate the gods, they call 'Magi,' as if they knew how to perform the very things they know to be done by nature. Such were, in former times, **Epimenides**, **Orpheus**, **Pythagoras**, and **Osthanes**. Similarly suspected were the <term>katharmoi</term> (purifications) of **Empedocles**, the <term>daemon</term> of **Socrates**, and the <term>to agathon</term> (the Good) of **Plato**."

In Greece, from the earliest times, we find the medical art practiced by individuals from specific families and by the priesthood; <note>* This union of the priestly and medical functions continued for a considerable period during the Middle Ages in Europe. The monks and other ecclesiastics, as we shall see hereafter, succeeded to the roles of the ancient priests.</note>

According to HERODOTUS (*Book II, c. 50*), the Greeks borrowed their temple ceremonies from the Egyptians, and their principal temples were dedicated to Egyptian deities. From the same authority we learn that the most ancient temple of *Venus Urania* was situated at Ascalon in Syria. ISIS had a splendid temple at Phocis; and SERAPIS had one at Messene, and another at Athens, etc.

But besides the Egyptian deities, the Greeks had their own medical divinities. JUPITER, JUNO, and APOLLO were worshipped in this capacity; indeed, even HERCULES, according to PAUSANIAS (*in Boet. c. 24*), had a particular temple of health. For a long period, too, the Greeks revered the monument of the celebrated seer CALCHAS, to whom the sick sacrificed a ram and slept upon its skin in order to obtain prophetic dreams.

One of the most celebrated among the medical deities of the Greeks was APOLLO, who was also called PAEAN (Παῑαν), the physician of the gods. PINDAR ascribes three professions to APOLLO—medicine, music, and divination. Music, indeed, was frequently employed as a therapeutic agent in ancient times, and much that is now considered utterly fabulous has been attributed to its efficacy. By the later poets and historians, APOLLO is principally distinguished as a physician and a seer. In the mythology of the Greeks, this deity is generally designated as the inventor of medical

**ORPHEUS**, who was thought to have derived his science and wisdom from the Egyptian priests, has also been considered by others as the inventor of all religious ceremonies and mysteries, as well as of medicine and poetry, among the Greeks. In this capacity, he has been commemorated by **SOCRATES**, **PLATO**, **EURIPIDES**, and **HERODOTUS**; and the faculty of divination is said to have been hereditary in his family. Hence the many singular and fabulous adventures which have been ascribed to him. **MELAMPUS** was another celebrated physician among the Greeks. He acquired an extraordinary reputation as a result of his care for *Iphiclus* and the three daughters of *Proctus*.

But the most famous among the Greek physicians was **AESCULAPIUS**, said to have been the son of **APOLLO**, who was also numbered among the gods and had numerous temples erected to him. ### CHAPTER XVIII.

WE have already referred to the mode of treatment adopted by the priests in the Egyptian and Greek temples, and attempted to point out, in general, their relation to the peculiar processes reintroduced in modern times by MESMER. We shall now proceed with our remarks relative to the same subject, more especially regarding the practice of the ancient Greeks; upon which, as it appears to us, later physicians have attempted—apparently in ignorance of its peculiar nature and efficacy, or from some less justifiable motive—to cast unmerited ridicule and contempt. course of our further inquiry, that the ancient system of medical treatment, when properly understood, was founded upon a more consummate knowledge of the human constitution, physical and psychical, than the boasted therapeutic practice of the present day.

In these temples, then, the most remarkable subject of investigation is their oracles, which flourished in Greece, even at the period when the inhabitants of that wonderful and most intelligent country had attained their highest degree of development in literature, philosophy, and the arts. The oracles, indeed, were anxiously consulted by all ranks of men, even the most cultivated, upon every important public or private occasion—even upon matters relating to affairs in which the interests of the commonwealth were most deeply concerned. This practice of consulting the most celebrated oracles, indeed, has been stigmatized or ridiculed by almost all modern authors as a system of fraud, deceit, and delusion; and the priests themselves, who officiated upon these occasions, have been generally denounced as complete knaves and impostors. But these opinions appear to have been formed without any serious investigation of the subject; and those who entertained them were probably swayed also, in no small degree, by misapprehension and prejudice. Let us, therefore, examine the whole matter a little more closely, and with greater attention to the particular processes.*

In these sacred temples, then, it appears to have been the usual practice to place the patients, after

opinions of all the later Christian writers, who frequently appear to conceive—very preposterously, we think—that, by depreciating these institutions, they are, at the same time, advancing the credit of the Scriptural miracles and prophecies. Even the learned and amiable ROLLIN, forgetting his duties as a faithful and disinterested historian, does not hesitate to make use of occasional assertions and arguments—*argumenta ad vulgus*—upon this subject, which appear totally inconsistent with his usual candor, and can scarcely be conceived capable of imposing upon the minds of such men as will submit to fair and impartial inquiry.
“The general character of oracles,” says this popular author, “was ambiguity, obscurity, and convertibility, to use that expression; so that one answer would agree with several different, and sometimes directly opposite events. By the help of this artifice, the Demons”—so the Archbishop believed in Demonology—“*who of themselves are not capable of knowing the future, concealed their ignorance, and amused the credulity of the Pagan world.*”
After referring to the response of the oracle in the well-known case of Croesus, King of Lydia, Mr. Rollin observes that, “under the cover of such ambiguities, the god eluded all difficulties, and was never in the wrong.”
The learned and eloquent historian, however, afterwards finds himself compelled to make pretty ample admissions in favor of the oracles.
“*It must, however, be confessed,*” he says, “*that sometimes the answer of the oracle was clear and circumstantial. I have repeated in the history of Croesus the stratagem he used to assure himself of the truthfulness of the oracle, which was to demand of it, through his ambassador, what he was doing at a certain prefixed time. The Oracle of Delphi replied that he was causing a tortoise and a lamb to be prepared in a brass vessel, which was actually the case.*”

The explanation which M. ROLLIN gives of these transactions is curious enough, and would probably have been deemed very ingenious and satisfactory some centuries ago.

> “Admitting it to be true,” argues he, “that some oracles have been followed precisely by the events foretold, we may *believe* that God, to punish the blind and sacrilegious credulity of Pagans, has sometimes permitted demons to have a knowledge of things to come, and to foretell them distinctly enough. Which conduct of God, though very much above human comprehension, is frequently attested in the Holy Scriptures.”

The simple-minded Principal of the University of Paris does not appear to have perceived that these arguments of his savored not a little of Jesuitry, if not of absolute impiety. With similar simplicity, the learned Principal informs us, that Father *Baltus*, the Jesuit, Professor of the Holy Scriptures in the University of Strasbourg, composed “a very solid treatise, wherein he demonstrates invincibly, *with the unanimous authority of the Fathers, that demons were the real agents in the oracles.*” And he afterwards asserts that “*all the Fathers of the Church, and ecclesiastical writers of every age, maintain and assert, that the Devil was the author of idolatry in general, and of oracles in particular.*”—And yet the learned and Reverend Principal reprobates credulity!

and others. These temples, indeed, were generally provided with regular dormitories, especially those most frequently visited by patients who desired to obtain divine assistance and counsel in their various afflictions. The revelations that were mysteriously received by the patients in these circumstances were believed to come from the patron deity and were, consequently, accepted as oracles. This temple-sleep itself was named by the Greeks <term>εγκοίμησις</term>, and by the Romans, <term>incubatio</term>.

To show the specific procedures that took place in these temples generally, we shall take as our primary example the temples built for ÆSCULAPIUS, the most famous of which was the temple at *Epidaurus*. This building, located in the Peloponnesus, was dedicated to that religious service which later spread over a large portion of the ancient world. Epidaurus is said to have been the birthplace of ÆSCULAPIUS; for this reason, it was held to be uniquely sacred. Crowds of patients flocked to this temple to recover their health and to be enlightened by divine dreams. For this reason, ÆSCULAPIUS was also named by the Greeks <term>ονειροπομπὸυ</term>—the sender of dreams.

The temple itself was located in a beautiful spot on a significant height. It was surrounded on all sides by wooded hills where the air was exceedingly pure and there was an abundance of excellent spring water. The charms of nature

In the temple itself there were many antechambers, and, in the very innermost recess, the statue of the god. This statue, composed of ivory and gold in a sitting posture, was the work of *Thrasimenides*. In one hand of the figure was a staff, and the other was placed on the head of a serpent, which wound itself around the staff. A dog was placed near the figure. In some of the other temples, *Aesculapius* had a laurel wreath on his head; and he occasionally exhibited various other emblems: such as a large golden beard, a cloak (<term>pallium</term>), etc. In the antechamber of the temple, there were usually the symbolic representations of Fortune, Dreaming, and Sleep.

No person, unless on very rare and uncommon occasions, was admitted into the interior of the sanctuary; the priests alone had access to the presence of the deity; sometimes strangers were not even permitted to approach the temple. Those who desired access to it were obliged first to prepare themselves for the occasion in the neighboring Temple of Isis. In the antechambers of the temple, there were many votive tablets, containing descriptions of diseases and of the remedies successfully administered. These were also sometimes engraved on the pillars of the temples. Such inscriptions were intended for use in similar cases;

and it has been said that **Hippocrates** actually made use of the information provided by these tablets and inscriptions in the Temple of Cos, of which he is reported to have made a collection. **Thomasius** has preserved and published several of these inscriptions.

Another similar temple was built to **Aesculapius** at Pergamos in Asia Minor, where, in addition to the other advantages of the location, there was a wonderful medicinal spring and also warm baths. Indeed, when selecting locations for these temples, those that provided the convenience of mineral springs and the purest air were usually chosen; for these reasons, they were generally placed on high ground. The advantages of the natural setting were often improved by artificial means. Gardens were created, and institutions were established to encourage all sorts of gymnastic exercises. Upon entering the temples, the patients were bound by a most solemn promise to give complete obedience to the orders and prescriptions of the supervising priests. Dietary abstinence was strictly enforced, especially from the use of wine. The priests led the patients through the antechambers of the temple, pointed out the images and votive tablets, and told of the miraculous cures which had been performed with the help of the presiding guardian deity. Prayers were offered, and sacred hymns were sung—the latter often accompanied by instrumental music;

<meta>This page continues the description of the rituals and medical practices at the ancient healing temples of Aesculapius, detailing the physical preparations and the practice of temple-sleep.</meta>

<page-num>175</page-num>

and sacrifices were made for the purpose of winning the favor of the patron god. Baths were always used as part of the preparatory treatment, as was the drinking of pure water (the hydropathic system). The baths were usually accompanied by massages, and by various manipulations and anointments (the magnetic treatment). These massages and manipulations were cautiously administered by individuals specifically appointed, trained, and instructed for that particular purpose. Fumigations were also used prior to admission to the Oracle. (See SPRENGEL'S *History of Medicine*, vol. i.)

The general goal of all these preparatory ceremonies and observances was to induce sleep; and when this state was reached, the patients were laid down to sleep—often upon the skin of a newly slaughtered sheep (*incubatio*)—in the regular dormitory. However, according to PAUSANIAS, this temple-sleep, or incubation, generally took place at night in the various apartments of the dormitory, in darkness and solemn silence. In short, these ancient Temples of Health appear to have included a system of moral and physical treatment excellently suited to promote comfort and to achieve the ultimate cure of the patients who went to them to restore their health.

But we must now proceed to refer more specifically to that part of this ancient medical procedure which appears to have been most closely

We have seen that, in the temple ceremonies we have described above, it was usual to endeavor to set the patients asleep by means of certain artificial processes; and we have abundant reason to infer that the sleep thus induced was nothing else than a modification of that somnambulism which is so frequently produced by the modern disciples of MESMER. In this temple-sleep, as in the Mesmeric crisis, dreams and visions occurred, and the prophetic faculty was developed in a manner similar to that which is occasionally elicited by the magnetic treatment. This, indeed, is quite clear from the accounts which have reached us regarding the effects of the temple processes, and also from the descriptions which have been given of the states themselves by various ancient authors. The patients slept, dreamed, prescribed appropriate remedies for themselves and others, and predicted their own ultimate cure or death, as well as the fate of other persons—even of those who were absent and at a distance. In all this—heathenish as it has been considered by many—there was, in reality, nothing either peculiarly unnatural or diabolical. Such premonitions were merely a product of the extraordinary state in which the patients were occasionally placed. In the remainder of this work, we shall have occasion to notice many instances of the development of similar phenomena, which, with

Great attention was also paid to mental passions and emotions; and, as we have already observed, all sorts of gymnastic exercises were likewise encouraged and occasionally prescribed.

When any remarkable cure was achieved in these temples by means of the remedies or processes employed, it was common, in many cases, for the grateful recovering patient to put up a votive tablet in some conspicuous part of the building, with a suitable inscription for the purpose of commemorating the favorable event. This practice has been alluded to by the poet Tibullus in one of his elegies:—

->*Nunc, Dea, nunc succurre mihi; nam posse mederi,*<-

### CHAPTER XIX.

It would seem unnecessary to enter into any particular description of the various ancient Temples of Health, which, indeed, were all very similar to each other in their institution, objects, and modes of treatment. It might appear unpardonable, however, in a treatise of this nature, to pass over, without any special notice, the celebrated *Oracle of Delphi*, which, although ultimately subjected to the common fate of all the other institutions of *Paganism*, has preserved the memory, at least, of its once universal reputation to the latest times, and has even become proverbial in the language of all nations.

The *Oracle of Delphi* derived its name from a city in *Boeotia*, which was situated on the southern side of *Mount Parnassus*. This institution is said to have taken its origin from the following circum-

<summary>This page concludes the discussion on the recording of medical cures on temple pillars and begins a new chapter focusing on the historical significance and origins of the Oracle of Delphi.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the narrative of the origins of the Oracle of Delphi, detailing how the site's unique geological features were first discovered by local shepherds.</meta>

<page-num>180</page-num>
<header>AN HISTORY OF MAGIC, &c.</header>

stances:—Some shepherds, who were tending their goats in that neighborhood, observed that the animals, when they happened to approach too near a deep aperture in a particular spot, from which a peculiar vapor constantly issued, were seized with a species of intoxication accompanied by strange motions; and that even one of the shepherds, who went out of curiosity to examine this aperture, became similarly affected. He not only exhibited the same peculiar motions as the goats, but actually became suddenly endowed with the spirit of prophecy and began to predict future events. These circumstances soon led to the conclusion that there must be something divine—according to the notions of those times—in this subterranean aperture; and the place, therefore, eventually came to be generally frequented with the goal of exciting the faculty of penetrating into the secrets of the future. But, as it sometimes unfortunately happened that those who approached too near the aperture in question, stupefied by the vapor which issued from it, fell into it and perished, this aperture was subsequently closed up with a three-footed stool (*Tripos*), in the middle of which there was an opening upon which those who were desirous of acquiring the prophetic faculty placed themselves.

For a considerable period, this miraculous spot was dedicated to no particular deity; but, at length, it was determined that the place should be consecrated to APOLLO, and that a temple should be

erected to that tutelary divinity upon the particular spot. At first this temple is said to have been formed merely of laurel branches; but, afterwards, it was converted into a more solid and lasting edifice of stone, and also provided with priests, for the purpose of superintending the regular worship of the deity to whom it was consecrated.

It is particularly remarkable that, in the Temple of Delphi, young women were, for the most part, selected for the prophetic office; generally girls of simple manners, chosen out of the lower classes of the people. They were denominated <term>Pythiæ</term> (Pythonesses)—a designation derived from <term>Apollo Pythius</term>, or the Soothsayer. That these Pythonesses derived their prophetic inspiration from subterranean vapors, was generally believed; while different opinions prevailed in regard to the real cause. Some explained the whole matter in a perfectly natural manner. They conceived that these vapors produced a state of mental excitement, and disposed the soul to the exercise of <term>divination</term>. Others propounded explanations of a different and more mysterious character; but the fact itself was never called in question. Most of our modern interpreters, indeed, have been disposed to overlook or utterly deny the reality of these facts, and to ascribe the whole matter to fraud and delusion. But this last opinion is directly opposed to the evidence, and appears to be no better than a mere arbitrary cutting of the Gordian knot, which they have found themselves unable to unravel.

It is a very remarkable circumstance that the *Pythia* was also sometimes called the *prophetess from the breast or the stomach*—VENTRILOQUA VATES—or, among the Greeks, ἐγγαστρίμαντις, ἐγγαστρίμυθος. From the use of these expressions, in one particular sense, it would appear probable that the phenomenon of the transference of sensibility to the epigastric region, or pit of the stomach, was known to the Greeks even in these ancient times, as we previously found it to be among the still more ancient Hindus. The priests interpreted the symbolic and frequently indistinct responses of the Oracle, which—and this is another remarkable circumstance—were, for the most part, delivered in rhythmic sentences which required explanation.

This temple at Delphi, like those of AESCULAPIUS, was provided with different apartments for the sick, and for those who merely came to consult the Oracle. The *Pythia* herself had a distinct and separate sanctuary, to which no person whatever was admitted. Near her apartment, there was a small cabinet, where those who came to consult her awaited her responses. The open entrance to the cell appropriated to the *Pythia* was entirely covered with laurel leaves; so that no one who approached it could perceive the Prophetess. Among plants, the laurel, as is well known, was particularly sacred to APOLLO; and it was believed to possess the property of inducing sleep and dreams. It was also a common belief among the people, that the

->*Laurus amica bonis geniis, longeque repellit*<-
->*Nube cava tectos lemures.*<-

It is worth noting that the Delphic Oracle became so famous for its answers—not only in cases of disease, but also in other matters of both public and private concern—that it was, at length, designated as the oracle of the universe, and was visited by many individuals from all quarters of the globe. It is also mentioned by Plutarch, as a detail unique to the temple at Delphi, that in the sacred fire maintained there, no other wood but fir was burned; and that no woman was ever permitted to consult the Oracle.

We think it is quite unnecessary to go into any further details regarding the many other ancient Greek temples and oracles. They appear to have all been dedicated to nearly the same purposes—namely, to the cure of the sick, to the development of the prophetic faculty, and to religious worship; and enough has already been said for the purpose of illustrating these goals.

It has been common for many modern authors who have written on this subject to disparage, if not to ridicule, the whole of this ancient temple system as the mere product of ignorance, vanity, and folly. However, the wisest among the philosophers and historians of that time viewed

this matter in a very different light, and with very different feelings; and, because of their opportunities for observation, their opinions upon the subject ought to possess greater weight. **Plato** (*in Phaedrus*) observes that the prophetess at Delphi and the priestesses at Dodona had, in their sacred *mania*, done much good to their country by their explanation of both public and private affairs; but in their sober, waking state, they did little or nothing. Hence, it would appear to be quite certain that these pythonesses and priestesses did not utter their oracular responses in their ordinary conscious state of existence and wakefulness, but in a peculiar condition of ecstatic inspiration, produced, no doubt, by artificial means in susceptible subjects; and **Aelius Aristides**, the philosopher already mentioned and himself a temple patient, repeatedly bears witness that the priestesses of Dodona knew neither what they were about to say before their spiritual excitation, nor what they had actually said after they had recovered their natural consciousness. Here, as will more plainly appear hereafter, we have a perfect and very striking coincidence between the ancient temple-sleepers and the modern somnambulists, as described by the disciples of **Mesmer**, in all essential and even accidental circumstances.*

<meta>This page continues from the previous discussion of Aelius Aristides' accounts of temple sleep, concluding the footnote from the previous page and inferring that ancient temple healings were a form of priestly magnetism or somnambulism, comparable to the discoveries of Mesmer.</meta>

Thus, from all the accounts passed down to us, we are entitled to draw the following inferences regarding the treatment of the sick and the phenomena they displayed in the ancient temples. Those patients who came to consult the oracles about their health slept during the night in the Temple of *Aesculapius*, where, amidst the darkness and solemnity of the surrounding scene, they were subjected to certain ceremonies and manipulations—that is, they were magnetized by the priests. It is now well known from experience that a particular place, or a particular room, may be specially magnetized, and somnambulism thus rendered infectious. This fact could be demonstrated to have occurred in various instances and has been proved, in particular, by the proceedings that took place in the early magnetic associations in France, soon after the announcement of *Mesmer’s* discovery. Now, in the ancient temples, as has been shown, there was a particular place, a special apartment, or dormitory, where the patients slept; and in these circumstances were manifested all those curious phenomena which have astonished, puzzled, bewildered, and perplexed philosophers in all subsequent ages, even down to our own times.

<meta>This page continues the investigation into ancient practices, shifting the focus from the physical environment of temples to the philosophical interpretations of trance states by celebrated Greek thinkers.</meta>

<page-num>186</page-num>
<header>A HISTORY OF MAGIC, &c.</header>

Regarding the opinions of the most ancient and most famous philosophers of Greece—PYTHAGORAS, EMPEDOCLES, PLATO, ARISTOTLE, etc.—it is abundantly clear that they were all well acquainted with somnambulistic and ecstatic states, however much they may have differed from each other in their methods of explaining the phenomena. Some, indeed, considered these states to be entirely divine in origin and, consequently, impossible to explain philosophically; while others believed them to arise from natural, though in some respects extraordinary and abnormal, causes, and to belong to the field of science. Among the former may be counted SOCRATES, PLATO, and the philosophers of that school; among the latter, ARISTOTLE and his disciples, who considered the prophetic faculty, in particular, as something quite natural, depending on the imagination and the temperament, combined with a uniquely modified condition of the biological organism, or idiosyncrasy. “Many of those people who show the spirit of prophecy,” says the last-mentioned philosopher, “are under the influence of maniacal or lymphatic diseases; and they are said to be divinely inspired when the phenomena, apparently, are not produced by actual disease, but by a natural predisposition resulting from a unique temperament.” To much the same effect, BUCCAFIERRI, one of the commentators on Aristotle, says: *Qui habet habitum melancholicum, habet per se causam prædicendi de futuris, et ideo per istum habitum, prophetia erit* <note>“He who has a melancholic temperament has in himself the cause of foretelling the future, and therefore, through this temperament, prophecy will occur.”</note>

<summary>Ancient Greek philosophers like Plato and Aristotle recognized trance and prophetic states, though they debated whether these phenomena were divine in origin or resulted from natural biological temperaments.</summary>
<keywords>Greek philosophy, Pythagoras, Plato, Aristotle, somnambulism, ecstatic states, prophecy, temperament, Buccafierri, medical history</keywords>

--- Page 259 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of ancient philosophical interpretations of prophetic states, concluding the reference to Aristotle’s commentators before introducing the life and work of Apollonius of Tyana.</meta>

<page-num>187</page-num>
<header>A HISTORY OF MAGIC, etc.</header>

*secundum naturam, et melancholicus habitus erit pro propheta naturaliter, quia ille habitus est naturalis.* <note>“...according to nature, and a melancholic disposition will naturally produce a prophet, because that disposition is natural.”</note> (LUDOVICO BUCCAFIERRI; *Lectures on Aristotle’s books.* Venice, 1570.)

We trust that all of this will become clearer and more generally understandable when we come to describe and comment on the more modern instances of the natural and artificial development of somnambulism and ecstatic states.

Our goal in the preceding observations has been to demonstrate that the phenomena in question did not escape the attention of the philosophers and physicians of antiquity; but that, even in these remote times, they were a serious and interesting subject of scientific investigation for students of nature.

## CHAPTER XX.

One of the most extraordinary, active, and influential successors of PYTHAGORAS, and one who became so celebrated for his study and practice of what were then called the *magical arts*, was APOLLONIUS OF TYANA. He is, perhaps, the first prominent individual about whom it can be stated that his entire doctrines and medical practice were clearly of a truly magnetic nature.

In his sixteenth year, this enterprising physician and philosopher began his travels, for the pur-

purpose of visiting the various temples in different countries, and of being initiated into their peculiar doctrines and mysteries. His life has been written by PHILOSTRATUS, who informs us that APOLLONIUS first visited the temple of AESCULAPIUS in Aegeae, and afterwards those of the oracles of AMPHIARIUS, DELPHI, and DODONA; that he subsequently made acquaintance with the MAGI at Nineveh and Babylon: that he afterwards traveled to Egypt, Ethiopia, Crete, Sicily, and Rome; and, at length, took up his residence in Smyrna, Ephesus, and Tyana. He is said to have lived about a hundred years, and to have died, at length, in the ninety-sixth year after the Crucifixion. Wherever he went, during his lifetime, he encouraged piety, prayer, and moral conduct, performed the most wonderful cures, and prophesied. For these reasons, it is said that some of the heathens placed him on a level with, or in opposition to, JESUS CHRIST; and as there were no certain accounts of his death, the inhabitants of Tyana declared him to be immortal, and erected a temple to him in their city. His portrait was exhibited in many other temples. The Emperor ANTONINUS CARACALLA paid him divine honors; and ALEXANDER SEVERUS, and other emperors, showed the greatest respect for his memory. (*LAMPRID. in ALEX. SEVER. c. 29.*)

to have frequently happened that his personal presence was not even necessary to produce the remedial effects. By means of his talismans, he also performed wonderful cures and, it is said, even controlled the fury of the winds and waves.

As magic, probably due to its degradation and resulting abuses, had been strictly prohibited by several decrees of the Emperors and the Roman Senate during the time of APOLLONIUS, he exerted himself to maintain it in all its original purity and dignity; and, at the same time, he made a very proper distinction between MAGIC, in its purer sense, and the fake arts of *witchcraft* and *sorcery*. By MAGIC, he understood the method of performing miracles, as they were then called, or marvelous things, by means of sacrifices and ceremonies—words and prayers. Of demons and spirits APOLLONIUS says nothing.

The philosophy of APOLLONIUS was Platonic-Pythagorean, and would probably be denounced as obscure and mystical by the shallow thinkers of the present age. In the main, however, it is by no means as inconsistent with the doctrines and spirit of Christianity as many of the physiological and psychological speculations of some modern schools. APOLLONIUS, indeed, appears to have acquired very considerable achievements in physics, as well as in psychology and metaphysics, and to

The more profound inquirers among the ancient <term>MAGI</term>, indeed, appear to have been aware that the development of those spiritual or psychical phenomena which are now called *Clairvoyance*, lucid vision, second-sight, prophecy, etc., and mental *actio in distans* <note>Action at a distance</note>, although rarely manifested, proceeded from a faculty natural to the human soul when freed from the obstructing fetters of the material organism and left free to exercise its own independent energies. Similar notions have been embraced by many modern philosophers whose minds have become emancipated from the restraints of all degrading material systems of intellectual science. All schemes of philosophy, it is true—the spiritual as well as the sensual—may be carried out into the regions of extravagance and mystical speculation when extended beyond the legitimate bounds of experience and observation. However, the rigorous deductions from the cautious exercise of our reasoning faculties, when warranted by the facts presented to us by nature, must, it is believed, ultimately lead us to the clear distinction between a material and a spiritual or moral nature in man—a distinction which lies at the very foundation of all morality and all religion. Meanwhile, the proper control of common sense will compel us to restrain our speculations on such subjects within

In short, while broadened and impartial investigation forces us to acknowledge the distinction between mind and matter—intellect and sensibility—that which perceives from that which is perceived—the subject from the object; we find ourselves utterly incapable of explaining the absolute nature of either, and must, necessarily, restrict our speculations to an observation and examination of the observed phenomena of both. In this respect, the Materialists and the Immaterialists, or Spiritualists, are very much in the same situation; and both must ultimately rely upon the phenomena of nature and the common facts of consciousness. But, in all our speculations, we must beware of denouncing or forbidding any demonstrated fact—however extraordinary or incredible it may at first sight appear to be, or however incompatible with our preconceived notions—as inconsistent with our ideas of the ordinary laws of nature. Otherwise, while attempting to exhibit our own wisdom and sagacity, or the sharpness of our skepticism, we may in the end only betray our ignorance, presumption, and folly. *Qui pauca respiciant de facili pronuntiant.* <note>Those who consider only a few things find it easy to pronounce judgment.</note> It is the proper business of the true philosopher to observe and record the laws and operations of nature in the physical and in the moral world, insofar as these are capable of being ascertained by human research. Self-proclaimed philosophers and wits, in their own vanity, have combined to denounce and ridicule the phenomena of *Clairvoyance*, and, in particular, the occasional development of the faculty of divination, without even condescending to a strict and serious investigation of the actual manifestation of the facts. This conduct, we venture to think, is rather unreasonable, and very far from being fair. But, by way of illustration, let us look, for a moment, to an analogous faculty—the faculty of Memory. No person, we think, will attempt to deny that mankind possesses the power of recalling past impressions—of recollecting past events. But if it is possible for the mind to recall what is past, why should it be impossible for it to anticipate the future? Why should the existence of the one faculty be acknowledged, while that of the other is discredited? That which is past no longer has any immediately perceptible existence. But the mind, it is admitted, can retrace and recall those past impressions which no longer exist. Why, then, should it not also, in certain circumstances, become capable of foreseeing the future, which does not yet exist? It is very easy to assert that man possesses a faculty which enables him to recall the past, but no power of foreseeing the future. But this last is just the <term>*de quo quæritur*</term>; and to assume the negative is a mere begging of the question. The manifestation of the faculty, in

This last case, indeed, as in the former, must be proved by actual experience of the fact; but this is all that can be reasonably required. The actual occurrence of the phenomenon precludes all reasoning regarding its possibility. Memory itself, we may observe, is stronger or weaker in different individuals. The faculty of foresight, presentiment, or prophecy is, by comparison, of rare occurrence. But philosophy can assign no satisfactory reason why the latter may not be occasionally manifested in peculiar circumstances; and, therefore, we are compelled to rest satisfied with the fact that “coming events” do sometimes “cast their shadows before.” Nor are we bound to account for the occasional manifestation of this particular faculty any more than we are in the case of the exercise of memory. Neither memory nor foresight, indeed, is capable of being adequately explained upon merely physical principles. Both are psychical faculties peculiar to the mind in certain states of the organism; and there is no necessity for calling in the assistance of angels, or demons, or other supernatural agencies, in either case. Let us listen to the words of ATHENAGORAS: *Et cum suapte vi ac ratione, anima, utpote immortalis, plerumque moveatur et agat in homine, ita ut futura prædicat, et rerum præsentium statum dirigat aut emendat, hujus sapientiæ laudem Dæmones sibi lucrantur.* <note>“And since the soul, being immortal, is for the most part moved and acts in man by its own power and reason, so that it predicts the future and directs or improves the state of present things, demons claim the credit for this wisdom for themselves.”</note> The mind, unquestionably, has its own laws as well as the body, although both may occasionally act in concert; and they are so intimately connected together that it may

In the ancient narratives of psychological facts, too, there may be, and probably is, an occasional mixture of the mythical with the true; but modern investigation has abundantly demonstrated that these fables—if they are fables, and not merely an exaggeration of real occurrences—have, for the most part at least, some foundation in reality. Indeed, we think the mythical elements in such instances will generally be found to relate either to the exaggerated statement of the occurrences, or to the explanation of certain facts, rather than to the facts themselves.

# A HISTORY OF MAGIC, &c.

### CHAPTER XXI.

Among the Romans, at an early period of their history, we again recognize the same medical treatment—the temple-sleep (*incubatio*), and other magical arts and ceremonies—as prevailed among the Egyptians and the Greeks. *Incubare dicuntur proprie hi, qui dormiunt ad recipienda responsa ; unde ille incubat Jovi, id est, dormit in Capitolio, ut responsa possit accipere.* <note>Properly speaking, those who sleep to receive answers are said to "incubate"; hence, one "incubates" to Jupiter—that is, he sleeps in the Capitol so that he may receive answers.</note> (Servius, *on Virgil*.)

In the history of Numa, we may trace the early practice of magic at Rome, and particularly in the alleged interaction of that monarch with the nymph **Egeria**. **Plutarch** (*in* **Numa**) mentions that, at this time, **Launus** and **Picus**—two individuals well skilled in **Magic**—came to Italy, and were well received by **Numa**. The ancient Etruscans and Sabines seem to have been also well acquainted with **Magic**, in its better sense. The same may be said of the Marsians, according to **Pliny**. In ancient Rome, we likewise find the occasional occurrence of somnambulistic phenomena and magical practices, interwoven, as elsewhere, with many superstitious practices and ceremonial rites. The Romans always acknowledged the Etruscans to have been their masters in the divine science of medicine and *Clairvoyance*.

<summary>This chapter describes how ancient Romans adopted magical medical treatments like "temple-sleep" and identifies key historical figures and groups, such as Numa and the Etruscans, who practiced these arts. It establishes that Roman medicine and clairvoyance were deeply rooted in the traditions of their predecessors and neighbors.</summary>

<keywords>Rome, temple-sleep, incubatio, Numa, Egeria, Plutarch, Launus, Picus, Etruscans, Sabines, Pliny, somnambulism, Clairvoyance</keywords>

**SPRENGEL** (I. 230) observes that one of the most ancient customs in Rome, for the purpose of averting disease and the anger of the gods, was to consult the oracle in the books of the Sibyls. **ENNEMOSER**, however, remarks that the Sibylline books were only consulted at a later period; and that, in early times, advice was sought from the Sibyls themselves, not only in the case of diseases, but in regard to other public and private affairs. Little is known with certainty regarding these Sibyls: even their exact number and names have been subjects of controversy; and there is little doubt that many fables have been intermingled with their history. But the subject is of considerable importance in the annals of **MAGIC** and **ANIMAL MAGNETISM**.

**PETRUS PETITUS**, in his work upon this subject, describes a Sibyl to be—*Puella cujus pectus numen recipit* <note>A girl whose breast receives the divinity</note>; a definition consistent with the ideas of the times, and with the divine attributes generally ascribed to the particular condition of the prophetic personage. The word itself—**SIBYL**—appears to be compounded from σιος, God, in the Æolic dialect, and βουλη, will or counsel. Others derive it from σιω, *agito* <note>I agitate</note>, and βυλλος, *plenus* <note>full</note>—full of violent agitation when in the prophetic state. (See **G. CRASSET**; *Dissertation sur les Oracles des Sibylles*.) The learned upon this subject are not agreed in regard to the exact number of these Sibyls, their age, their native country, or the time in which they lived. We are not aware that it has occurred to any previous author to assign to them an origin similar to

Some assume the existence of only one; others, of two, three, or four, or even of ten Sibyls. **VARRO** (*Book IX, to C. Caesar*)—whom **SAINT AUSTIN** <note>Saint Austin is an archaic name for Saint Augustine of Hippo.</note> designated as the most learned and eloquent man among the heathens—assumes ten. **LACTANTIUS** and the greater number of the Church Fathers agree with **SAINT AUSTIN**. **AELIAN** assumes four; **SOLINUS**, three. **PLINY**, too, speaks of three, whose statues were placed in the Capitol at Rome. The three assumed by **SOLINUS** are the *Delphic*, the *Erythraean*, and the *Cumaean*. **DIODORUS SICULUS** speaks of only one, whom he calls *Daphne*; by others, she is called *Mantho*, a daughter of Tiresias. **PLATO** and **CICERO** speak of only one; the expressions of **PLUTARCH** are doubtful. This incongruity in regard to the number of the Sibyls may be explained in the following manner.

Prophecy, divination, or soothsaying constitutes but one faculty; but this faculty may be manifested by different individuals. Hence, the expression—*the* Sibyl—may have designated the individual who manifested the faculty at any one particular time, or upon any one particular occasion. Indeed, the precise number of these Sibyls cannot now be determined. We are not warranted in limiting them to two, three, or ten. Writers upon the subject may have spoken only of those who were known to themselves and their contemporaries. Thus, **MARTIAN**

**CAPELLA** mentions two; **SOLINUS**, three; **VARRO**, ten. They may all have been right, so far as their individual knowledge extended. **PETITUS** says:
> "Prophecy itself, as the counsel of God, is but one; but this faculty may be exercised by many: Several may have become celebrated by the display of this gift."

In our own times, there is only one *Clairvoyance*, but many *Clairvoyants*: Yet, in speaking of any one particular instance of the development of this faculty, we talk of *the* Clairvoyant.

The first of **VARRO'S** ten Sibyls is the Persian, or Chaldean, called *Samota*. She is said to have been the eldest, and to have composed twenty-four books, in which the coming of **JESUS CHRIST**, his sufferings, death, and resurrection, were foretold. (**CRASSET**; I. c.) The second was the *Libyan Sibyl*; the third, the *Delphic*—the **DAPHNE** of **Diodorus**, celebrated by **HOMER**, and well known for her memorable oracular responses at Delphi. The fourth, and one of the most famous, was the *Cumaean* (*Sibylla Cumana*), born in the Trojan territory; she came to Italy and was held in particular esteem by the Romans, who believed that she had predicted the entire destiny of their empire. (**Pliny** 34, 5.) Before her arrival in Italy, the people had particularly honored **CARMENTA**, the mother of **Evander**, on account of her prophetic powers. (**Pliny** I. *Carmenta, whom these nations had admired as a prophetess before the arrival of the Sibyl in Italy.*)

Many details regarding this **Cumæan Sibyl** are related by **NÆVIUS** in his *African War*, and also by **PISO** in his *Annals*. **VIRGIL**, too, has taken particular notice of this Sibyl. She is said to have delivered her oracles before the landing of **ÆNEAS** in Italy, and she is represented as having lived in a large cavern in the neighborhood of Lake **Avernus**. She was called the Virgin and Priestess of **Apollo**. It is said that she frequently wrote her responses upon palm leaves and placed them at the entrance of the cavern, from where they were carried by the wind to a considerable distance. When she delivered her responses verbally, she was in a state of the most violent agitation. **VIRGIL** has given an admirable description of this excited state of the Sibyl—a condition that has frequently been witnessed in the case of some modern magnetic somnambulists during their cataleptic crises, though in these more recent instances, the state has been found capable of being modified by appropriate treatment. (See **VIRGIL**; *Æneid*, Book VI, v. 45, etc.) This state, indeed, appears to be similar in character to the **Corybantism** described by Greek writers and the *divinatio per furorem*, which is by no means a rare occurrence in spasmodic attacks, especially in the case of hysterical women and in some species of *mania*. Consequently, **ARISTOTLE** and others were led to consider the prophetic faculty as a unique property of the melancholy temperament.

The fifth Sibyl was the *Erythrean*, whom some believe to have been the same as the Persian.

The sixth was the *Samian*, of whom ERASTOSTHENES speaks. The seventh, according to VARRO, was *Herophile*. The eighth was the *Hellespontine*, who is said to have prophesied in the time of SOLON and CYRUS. The ninth was the *Phrygian*, who appears to have been the same as the *Cumæan*. The tenth was the *Tiburtina*, called also *Albunea*, who delivered her oracles at Tibur, and was worshipped as a goddess.

For further information upon this curious subject, we would refer our inquisitive readers to the following works, in which the history of these prophetesses is more amply detailed:—PLUTARCH; *Lib. Cur nam* PYTHIA *non amplius reddat oracula* <note>Book on why the Pythia no longer delivers oracles</note>. —*Traité de la Creance des Peres à l'occasion de l'esprit attribué aux Sibylles* <note>Treatise on the Belief of the Fathers regarding the spirit attributed to the Sibyls</note>, by DAVID BLONDEL. —POISSARDUS; *De Sibyllis* <note>On the Sibyls</note>.

From these Sibyls came the Sibylline books, which, in later times, were consulted in cases of disease as well as in important affairs of state; and which were particularly respected at Rome as the tutelary guardians of their empire. The origin of these books is uncertain. They are said to have been numerous; but those of a later date were, probably, false and supposititious. It is unnecessary for us to repeat the well-known history of the acquisition of these books by the Romans, of the respect long paid to them, of their careful preservation in the public archives, and of their ultimate fate.

The residence of the Sibyls was, generally, in

It has sometimes been questioned whether the entire history of these Sibyls and their oracles was not a mere fable; and many writers have treated the whole subject with incredulity, and even with ridicule. But there is no subject upon the substantial truth of which all ancient authors—historians, theologians, orators, philosophers, and poets—are more unanimously agreed; and although some fable may possibly have been allowed to blend with the truth, yet modern research and discovery warrant our belief that their narratives and occasional allusions to this particular topic are substantially founded upon a broad foundation of solid facts. These Sibyls, it would appear—like the ancient temple-sleepers and our modern magnetic somnambulists and <term>Crisiacs</term>—frequently uttered their oracular responses in metaphorical, parabolic, enigmatic, and obscure language—either *viva voce* <note>by word of mouth</note>, or in writing, or merely by signs: *Tribus modis futura prædicit—aut voce, aut scriptura, aut signis* <note>She predicts future things in three ways—either by voice, by writing, or by signs</note>:

> *Horrendas canit ambages, antroque remugit,*
> *Obscuris vera involvens.*
> <note>She sings terrifying riddles and bellows from the cave, wrapping truths in obscurities.</note>

At this moment, it would scarcely be possible to give a more accurate description of modern magnetic clairvoyants and prophets.

## CHAPTER XXII.

It has been a very common belief, and one much insisted upon by many modern and, especially, by theological writers, that the ancient oracles had entirely ceased upon the advent of JESUS CHRIST, and that they had never afterward been resumed. This assumption, however, is sufficiently refuted, by historical authority, by many of the best-informed writers upon the subject; and it is mani-

manifestly founded upon a mistake, probably originating from the common notion held by many of the early Christians, that these institutions were essentially connected with Paganism, and utterly opposed to the new faith; and, moreover, that they had been established and maintained by the Devil, whose empire was presumed to have been utterly annihilated at the coming of our Savior. But, in reality, this notion of the total cessation of the Oracles, at the period in question, is manifestly incorrect; and it renders the credibility of the early converts to Christianity extremely suspicious. It is sad to think that so fair a structure of substantial truth should have been sullied, at so early a period, by the impure varnish of falsehood and deception. In point of fact, it has been clearly demonstrated that the Oracles did not cease upon the appearance of JESUS CHRIST; nor had the Devil anything to do with their responses. Indeed, the continued existence of these Oracles, beyond the period in question, may be clearly proved by the most unimpeachable historical evidence. PLUTARCH lived after JESUS; and that author expressly mentions that the oracle at *Libadia*, as well as those of *Trophonius* and *Delphi*, still flourished in his time. In fact, he tells us that the Temple of Delphi had been repaired, and additions made to it, which rendered it more splendid than ever. SUETONIUS (*in Vita Neronis*) mentions that the Oracle of Delphi was consulted by NERO. PHILOSTRATUS says of APOLLONIUS—who lived 90

The gradual decline and ultimate ruin of the ancient pagan temples may, no doubt, be attributed in some considerable measure to the gradual encroachments and final triumph of the new faith and purer doctrines of the Christian converts; but these events were unquestionably influenced and accelerated by other accompanying causes.

It may be observed, however, that among the Romans, as among ancient nations generally, all such operations were considered magical, and that all MAGIC, without distinction, was frequently mixed up and confused with the unlawful arts of sorcery; and that laws were enacted at various times and in different countries with a view to suppress such abuses. SYLLA decreed the punishment of death against all those individuals, *qui susurris magicis hominem occiderent; qui mala sacrificia fecerint, venenaque amatoria habuerint.* <note>Who killed a man with magic whispers; who performed evil sacrifices, or possessed love potions.</note> Many similar laws were enacted during the time of the emperors; but it is evident that these laws almost always referred to common abuses and not to the legitimate practice of MAGIC in the higher sense of the word. Consequently, although the modern administration of sulphuric ether and chloroform—which are always dangerous and not infrequently accompanied by fatal consequences—might be considered as falling under the latter of these laws, they could not be held to extend to the simple and harmless treatment of the Animal Magnetists.

The School of the Neo-Platonists at Alexandria may be considered the connecting link between ancient and modern theosophical philosophy. The disciples of that school were well acquainted with the mysteries and oracles of the pagan world—they combined, in a very remarkable manner, the mystical theology of the ancient Egyptians with the philosophy of the Greeks, and the divinatory doctrines of the therapeutic adepts with the searching spirit of metaphysical inquiry. Their writings, too, appear to have been influenced, to a considerable degree, by the early spread of the tenets of Christianity, which, however, they did not entirely embrace. The Neo-Platonists, therefore, became, as it were, the friendly mediators between the ancient heathen philosophers and cosmologists, and the science and faith of the early Christian converts.

The various writings and peculiar tenets of the philosophers of this school would lead us into an inquiry and discussion much too extensive for the limits we have assigned to this treatise. Our readers, therefore, must be satisfied with little more than a mere reference to the writings of AMMONIUS SACCHAS, PLOTINUS, PORPHYRY, PROCLUS, IAMBLICHUS, etc., in which the doctrines of the Neo-Platonist-Pythagoreans are more completely developed. We may only observe that these doctrines appear to have been a compound of Zoroastrian notions, Egyptian mysteries, Greek philosophy, and the dogmas of Christianity. It must be mentioned, however, in particular, that the philosophers of this school appear to have been most intimately acquainted with all the most remarkable phenomena of somnambulism and magnetic clairvoyance, upon which, indeed, many of their distinguishing doctrines and favorite speculations were founded. This last circumstance appears to have drawn down upon them a very general and indiscriminate charge of mysticism—a charge which, at a more recent period, has been equally applied by the ignorant, the skeptical, and the uninquiring to the modern disciples of MESMER and PUYSEGUR. Many of the doctrines of the Neo-Platonists, however, have found enlightened partisans in all subsequent ages. Of these, in modern times and in our own country, GALE, GLANVILL, and DR. Upon the early introduction and diffusion of Christian doctrines, however, everything that related to the philosophy, as well as to the religion of the pagan world, was rejected by the new converts with violent abhorrence as heathenish impiety; moreover, it was presumed to be diametrically opposed to the purer tenets embraced in the new faith. Those phenomena, especially, which were alleged to have been manifested in the ancient Temples of Health, and generally regarded as the offspring of supernatural agency, were now deemed peculiarly obnoxious. Consequently, they were zealously anathematized and proscribed by the orthodox Christians as the impure and infamous works of the Devil. These phenomena, indeed, were universally acknowledged to be not only authentic but miraculous; but then, every miracle produced outside the bounds of the Christian Church was accounted satanic or demoniacal. That phenomena of such a character could by any possibility be produced by natural causes, no man in those times was sufficiently enlightened or bold enough to maintain, for fear of being accused of heresy and

Nor is this very surprising, when we consider the gross ignorance and barbarous superstition of the era: For even those praiseworthy investigations, which were established for the purpose of promoting the more accurate study of nature, were equally subjected to inquisitorial condemnation. The solitary natural philosopher—indeed, even the sober mathematician—were, for a long period, subjected to the serious accusation of devoting themselves to the study and practice of unholy arts, by means of a corrupt pact with the Evil One; and such individuals, upon the discovery of their pursuits, were frequently subjected to the most severe punishment or forced to flee for their lives. In short, every type of secular learning was considered magical or diabolical, and was placed under the ban of a general condemnation.

In consequence of such false, absurd, and harmful impressions, humanity, even under the generous dispensation of the Christian Gospel, gradually relapsed into a state of almost primeval darkness and utter barbarism. A large proportion of the clergy became little, if at all, superior in intellectual cultivation to the uneducated people whom they were appointed to teach; and their orthodoxy was questioned whenever they attempted to raise themselves above this degrading position, or if their inclination prompted them to venture into the dangerous paths of literature or science. Even those among the NEO-PLATONISTS who were inclined to dispute some of the doctrines of Christianity always expressed the highest respect for MOSES and JESUS CHRIST; and they never thought of attacking their characters or of denying the reality of their miracles. Finally, within the entire scope of their writings, there does not exist the slightest trace of a comparison or assimilation of the miracles of PYTHAGORAS with those of the Savior.*

### CHAPTER XXIII.

Savior and his disciples. The Neo-Platonists were represented as harboring the most deep-seated hatred of Christianity; and it was believed, despite all correct chronology, that even PHILOSTRATUS—who wrote his book before AMMONIUS SACCHAS began to teach and prior to the birth of PLOTINUS—had been an assistant to these men, and had described his hero, APOLLONIUS, as a great performer of miracles, in order to place him on a level with the divine founder of our religion. This nearly universal opinion—that miracles were invented by PHILOSTRATUS, PORPHYRY, and IAMBLICHUS for the purpose mentioned above—is so inconsistent with the entire history of PYTHAGORAS, APOLLONIUS, and the Neo-Platonists, and shows such ignorance or inattention, that I can scarcely comprehend how even moderate scholarship could have fallen for it, adopted it, and allowed it to remain accepted for such a long time. Humanity is deeply interested in the overthrow of superstition. But, on the other hand, it is surely ungenerous to insult a system of religion which was held by many learned and enlightened men who have gone before us—a religion, too, which, however imperfect and objectionable, pervaded a large portion of the world for four thousand years. The pagan religion, it is true, was full of superstitions, and every sane man must admit that the Christian system is, in every respect, infinitely preferable to that which it superseded. But because the latter is the truest and the best, and founded, as we believe, on divine revelation, still it would be

---

that, in the first and second centuries, the very same representation of **PYTHAGORAS** prevailed as that which they have transmitted. The following investigation will convince everyone that the very same philosophers who are thus suspected of cleverly devised fictions not only borrowed all their materials from preceding writers, but gave their narrative, almost throughout, in the very words of the authors they transcribed. Both of these philosophers were simple, naive, and thoroughly honest; and so far from imposing upon their readers with new fables of their own invention, they even, in their excerpts, condescended to communicate the observations of such persons as believed that the Pythagoreans had attributed many false miracles to their master. If they had been as cunning as is commonly thought, they would certainly have suppressed such observations, which could only demonstrate their own credulity and weaken the credibility of their own narratives.

> “I have no hesitation, therefore, in declaring the whole charge of fabrication brought against **PORPHYRY** and **IAMBLICHUS** to be false and groundless; and that I hold the authenticity of the fragments and testimonies of which their biographies are composed to be as genuine as I am skeptical in regard to their trustworthiness.”

unjust to conclude that the former consisted altogether of error and impiety. If this mode of reasoning were to prevail, our knowledge of antiquity would be worse than useless; if it were sufficient to be born Christians in order to entitle us to claim a superiority over the ancients, we are necessarily led to think that the moderns are far below what they ought to be, in consequence of the divine origin and purer nature of their religious faith.

But what rational being can believe that the great Creator could have abandoned his creation to itself for four thousand years? Is it not more natural to conclude that, if the pagan religion was not more distinguished by its simplicity and its purity, it was because the Deity, in his infinite wisdom and mercy, was pleased to wait until mankind, through contemplation and reflection, had time to elevate themselves to a purer faith, and, like the Hebrew nation, should come to adore him everywhere in the universe, without confining him to any particular spot? If, at length, this ancient religion was reformed by Moses, and still more by **JESUS CHRIST**, was not this a striking proof that the Supreme Being still watched over the universe, and that the homage offered up to the **UNKNOWN GOD** had not been addressed to him in vain?

Where shall we find more noble expressions of reverence and piety than in the language of the ancients, particularly in the works of the divine **PLATO**? It is said, indeed, that they worshipped statues; but St. Austin <note>Saint Augustine</note> has proved the contrary.

Pausanias has named the artists who made these statues, and Pythagoras had said: "Do not lend your likeness to God—do not represent him in any form." And Apollo is said to have communicated his oracles in person, but this is a mistake. It is not, says Plutarch, the voice of Apollo which is heard, nor his words, nor his verses, but those of the Pythoness. In conferring the attribute of divinity upon their great men, they may, no doubt, be said to have abused the name of God; but, in their simplicity, they thought to honor him by comparing with him all that they deemed most pure and illustrious upon the earth. Such practices, therefore, were not absolutely impious or idolatrous: Nature was so profuse and so mysterious in her manifestations that the ancients may have been led to exalt and to deify the bodies of which she was composed; but the majestic and incomprehensible power of the Creator and Preserver of all things still governed the world. The incense which was burned on the altars of Jupiter Tonans <note>Jupiter the Thunderer</note> was always the willing tribute of a profound respect for that invisible and inscrutable Being who presided over the destiny of mortals.*—"Who by searching can find out God?"

After a violent and bloody struggle of two or three centuries' duration, Christianity, at length, prevailed over Paganism; the ancient temples were

**TERTULLIAN** was one of the most able apologists and champions of the Christian religion at the period (**200 A.D.**), when a person named **MONTANUS**, a heretic, pretended to be the direct channel through which the Holy Spirit announced the new lights of Christianity. At first, **TERTULLIAN** treated these three personages as individuals possessed by the spirit of error; but soon afterward, from what he himself saw and heard, this man—the most learned and eminent among the Christians of his age—condescended to receive instruction in the school of a heretic and two reprobate women. We have already observed that the ancient oracles did not cease immediately upon the coming of JESUS CHRIST, but that they were maintained for a considerable period after that event, as can be proved by the evidence of individuals who saw and frequented them. Their destruction was ultimately brought about by other causes. The spring at Delphi—the reputed source of the inspiration of the place—is said to have been destroyed by an earthquake, which devastated the neighboring country; the other temples are believed to have gradually become, like most human institutions, degenerate and corrupt; their wealth tempted the greed of the neighboring princes and people;

### CHAPTER XXIV.

We will likely have occasion to return, in a later part of this treatise, to the opinions of some of the Fathers of the Church regarding the phenomena of somnambulism and clairvoyance. In the meantime, we shall proceed to inquire into the history of magical science during the Middle Ages of Europe.

The study of MAGIC appears to have been cultivated at a very early period among the nations of the Teutonic race. Pliny says of them—*Magiam attonite celebrant tantis ceremoniis, ut eam dedisse Persis videre possit*; and, as in other parts of the world, it was a long time before the more general pursuits of legitimate science taught mankind to distinguish between the ordinary operations of nature and the agency of supernatural powers. The almost universally prevalent notion, too, of the existence of angels and devils, and of the continual interference of these intermediate beings in the affairs of this sublunary world, was introduced—or, at least, confirmed and perpetuated among them, as elsewhere—by the Christian religion, and caused, if not an entire change, at least a consider-

<summary>The author identifies the advancement of Christianity as the final cause for the ruin of ancient oracles and begins a history of magic in the Middle Ages. He notes that the Teutonic tribes practiced magic with great ceremony and that Christian beliefs in angels and demons further reinforced the belief in supernatural interference in the world.</summary>
<keywords>Christianity, oracles, Middle Ages, Teutonic magic, Pliny, magical science, somnambulism, clairvoyance, angels, devils</keywords>

able modification of their primitive national faith. All the extraordinary phenomena of nature, as well as those abnormal states of the human body, which their limited science did not enable them to appreciate or explain, were attributed to the direct and inherent agency of the Divine Being, or of certain subordinate angels or demons; a belief which can hardly be said to have been entirely wiped out even in our own times.

The Christian religion, it is true, is essentially founded upon the principle of the unity of God, and of His undivided sovereignty throughout the universe of nature. But Christianity, at an early period, had also borrowed from the Jews—as the latter had probably derived from the Chaldeans, or Zoroastrian teachers—a personification of the evil principle—**SATAN**, or the *Devil*; a notion which, as already observed, the Jews themselves had received from the Eastern **MAGI**. The introduction of this notion into the popular belief, besides its influence upon religious opinion, caused a considerable modification of the theory of **MAGIC**—which from then on became completely degraded into a purely diabolical art, and was placed under the authority of Satan and his emissaries. A mere metaphor thus became elevated into a substantial personality, exercising a continual and very active interference in human affairs. All the bad actions of mankind, as well as many of the more extraordinary, obscure, and incomprehensible phenomena of nature—indeed, even the maladies which occasionally afflict humanity,

The doctrine of Devils and Demons, indeed, was directly supported by early Christian societies and spread so rapidly and extensively in the minds of the orthodox believers that, for many centuries, it was predominantly prevalent as an indispensable article of the common creed of the people. It continued to be generally maintained throughout the Middle Ages of Europe by all the dogmatic sophistries that perverted learning and ingenuity could bring forward in its support. So universal did this growing mischief ultimately become toward the conclusion of the fifteenth century (as we shall see by and by) that all Christendom was plunged into a state of the most fearful and unexampled alarm. *Heresy* and **MAGIC** were considered inseparable; fire and sword, at the instigation of the clergy, were employed for their extirpation. The empire of Satan became firmly established throughout the European community; an inveterate mania appears to have seized upon mankind, and a series of the most barbarous atrocities was perpetrated under the pretext of religious zeal, which stamped indelible disgrace upon human nature. We shall presently have occasion to direct the attention of our readers to some of the more
