It has already been observed that the idea of the personified principle of evil—Satan or the Devil—had been transferred from the Jewish to the Christian religion; and that the former appears to have borrowed it from the religious creed of the Chaldeans, the Babylonians, and the Parsi during the captivity. Soon after the introduction of Christianity, the same doctrine was embodied in a peculiar form in the Manichæan heresy, in which the good and evil principles were represented as rival <term>Genii</term>, nearly equal in power and dominion; struggling for control in this sublunary world and, consequently, existing in a state of continual rivalry and antagonism. It is strange enough that some of our recent Scottish divines—among others, the late eminent but eccentric, and not always very consistent DR. CHALMERS—appear to have embraced and propagated this most extraordinary doctrine as reconcilable with the framework of Christianity. ST. PAUL, however, represents this idea in a much more orthodox, rational, and intelligible form when he contrasts the works of the *spirit* with those of the *flesh*. But this is an obscure theological subject; and it is more pertinent to our present purpose to observe that the idea of Satan, or the Devil, came to be used to explain all those extraordinary phenomena that were considered magical. The apparently inexplicable phenomenon of the somnambulistic or ecstatic states—which appear to have been much more prevalent in ancient than in modern times, and which are most frequently the product of an unusual state of the organism, of acute sensitivities, or of powerful religious impressions—were ascribed, sometimes, to the direct agency of the Supreme Being, and, at other times, to the immediate influence of the Devil (*Morbus sacer* <note>Latin for "sacred disease," historically used to refer to epilepsy.</note>), according to the peculiar characteristic nature of the condition; but, in both cases, the phenomena themselves were always accounted supernatural. The Devil, indeed, in the belief of the ages to which we refer, appears to have been a much more important personage, and to have exercised a far greater influence upon the affairs of this lower world, than the Deity himself.

During the Middle Ages of Europe, indeed, diabolical influences were all-powerful and all-pervading throughout every class of people—the belief in this incessant satanic agency spread far and wide, like a virulent and incurable epidemic; MAGIC, or the BLACK ART—as it came to be named after its corruption—was the medium through which this infernal science was cultivated and practiced; even the leaders of the Christian flock were infected with the general moral contagion or fostered the prevailing mania, probably with the view of increasing their own power and reputation; although many of them, no doubt, may have been ignorant enough to fall in with the common infatuation. Females—especially old women—were supposed to be more familiar with the powers and more accessible to the tricks of the great enemy of Heaven and of the human race; and, during a very long period, *Sorcery* and *Witchcraft* kept mankind in a state of continual agitation and delusion, which, in itself, only tended to increase, aggravate, extend, and confirm the common mania. ### CHAPTER XXV.

Before we proceed to give a more detailed account of those most extraordinary hallucinations of the human intellect to which we have alluded in the previous chapter, it may, perhaps, be helpful to request the particular attention of our readers to a very remarkable state, occasionally manifested in the human organism—to which, indeed, we have already made some passing allusions in the earlier part of our treatise.

In the peculiar state to which we have referred, internal sensibility—especially in individuals suffering under any very violent excitement, or from constitutional causes, or abnormal action of the vital processes—appears to overpower the external senses and the reasoning faculty and, as it were, to create a new world for itself. This constitutional condition, including something more than is generally understood under the category of *Imagination* in the ordinary sense of the term, may be induced by various causes, and has

<summary>The author explains how efforts to promote rational views on witchcraft were suppressed by the Church and public opinion, and begins exploring the psychological state where internal sensations overpower reason to create hallucinations.</summary>

--- Page 301 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of a peculiar state of the human organism, often triggered by intense religious excitement, which has historically been misunderstood by science but is now being recognized under terms such as somnambulism and clairvoyance.</meta>

<page-num>229</page-num>
<header>A HISTORY OF MAGIC, etc.</header>

been, perhaps, most frequently and most powerfully developed by strong religious feelings and devotional excitement. The character of this peculiar state of the system was, for a long period, much misunderstood, and its curious characteristic phenomena, consequently, were entirely misrepresented. We allude to that extraordinary state of the organism, the manifested symptoms of which have been recently designated by the various names of *Somnambulism*, *Ecstasy*, *Clairvoyance*, etc.

This very remarkable condition of the human organism, which—under certain occasional diversities of form or type—has been frequently manifested among mankind from the beginning of time—does not appear to have attracted much of the serious attention of modern philosophers and physicians, but to have been viewed, principally, as a subject for the display of skepticism and ridicule, until a comparatively recent period, when the means were, at length, discovered and displayed by which its most interesting phenomena could be developed, in a multiplicity of instances, and almost at pleasure. These remarkable phenomena, indeed, as we have already observed, are sometimes manifested as the accompaniments or consequences of some other bodily or mental affections; they appear to be occasionally employed by nature herself as remedial processes in other constitutional disorders; and, in recent times, it has been proved that they may be elicited, in susceptible subjects, by various artificial means. From certain peculiarities of idiosyncrasy,

These phenomena were well known, although incorrectly understood, during the time when ancient pagan religions prevailed; and they are still far from uncommon, though imperfectly recognized and interpreted, within the modern and purer forms of Christianity. Indeed, they have been pressed into the service of every form of religious worship, although they have no inherent connection to any specific religion in themselves. They are the simple manifestation of certain natural phenomena for which, in certain circumstances and in susceptible subjects, religious fervor may be considered one of the contributing causes.

The clearest references to these unique states of the human organism are found in the writings of PLATO and the later Platonists. Because of an ignorance or misunderstanding of the specific subject to which they relate, these writings have generally been misunderstood and regarded as the mere products of metaphysical mysticism or poetic rhapsody. In the Epistles of ST. ST. PAUL’S EPISTLE to the CORINTHIANS. Some even of the Christian sectarians, indeed, differed in their explanations of these prophetic manifestations, although all agreed in attributing them to supernatural causes. The Apostle attributed them to the immediate agency of the divine spirit—the <term>ἐνεργεῖν</term> <note>inner working or acting</note>; the demoniacal visionaries, on the other hand, were <term>ἐνεργούμενοι</term> <note>those acted upon or possessed by spirits</note>. Natural and artificial means, however, were occasionally employed with the aim of producing ecstatic states—such as fumigations (which are still retained in the Roman Catholic ritual) and mysterious preparatory ceremonies, as was the case with the ancient Pagan Oracles and Temple worship. These ecstatic states, however, continued to be attributed by some to a divine influence and by others to demoniacal agency—opinions which have not entirely ceased to be held even in the present day, as we shall have the opportunity to show in the further progress of our research.

It has been frequently maintained, as we previously observed, that the Pagan Oracles ceased after the Advent of JESUS CHRIST. But this has been shown to be an obvious error. The prophetic announcements of these Oracles, we may also add, are referred to by several of the early Fathers of the Christian Church—by JUSTIN MARTYR, EUSEBIUS, LACTANTIUS, JEROME, AMBROSE, AUGUSTINE, CLEMENT of Alexandria, etc. IRENAEUS, as we have seen, associated with certain ecstatic women whom he commanded to prophesy. The result of our investigation, it is thought, will demonstrate the universality of this conviction, founded upon a very general acquaintance with the relevant facts.

## CHAPTER XXVI.

AMONG the Celtic tribes, including the Gauls and Cymbrians, a state of affairs very similar to what we have been considering until now—making all due allowance for their respective levels of mental culture and civilization—appears to have generally prevailed. The Celts, according to the research of recent investigators (See GRIMM'S *Mythology*), appear at one time to have possessed a very extensive European territory; but they were subsequently driven eastward by the Teutonic and other northern tribes. All of these races—the Gauls, the Spaniards (in part), the Britons, and the Belgians—we should include in one category, and we shall, therefore, proceed to inquire into the state of affairs among them in relation to the subject of our investigation.

Some writers have assumed the hypothesis that all of these tribes had derived their notions of Magic from the Romans. But this hypothesis clearly appears to be based on a mistake; for it is quite certain that all of these nations had their own religious and magical customs before the period of the Roman invasion. (See GRIMM.) These customs were not even mixed with those of the Romans, but retained their own original cha-

racter. Indeed, the originality, authenticity, and antiquity of northern mythology, and its affinity with that of Germany, have been fully demonstrated by the learned research of GRIMM. Certain analogous religious customs, indeed, have been found to pervade all ancient and primitive nations. PLINY and TACITUS have described some of those which were prevalent among the Germans; but the latter had no Greek or Roman temples dedicated to Apollo, Aesculapius, etc.; and the names of their gods, mentioned by TACITUS, are not German, but Roman, adapted to the language and notions of his countrymen, and transferred to the German divinities, who were worshipped in their sacred groves under German names and German attributes.

The Celtic chiefs and rulers were called Druids. Like the MAGI among the Eastern nations, they were the legislators, judges, priests, and prophets among the people. POMPONIUS ascribes a high degree of attainment to these Druids, embracing physical science, and religious and moral culture. One of the most important tenets which they are said to have most strenuously taught among the people was that of the immortality of the soul. At a later period, indeed, these Druids appear to have been held in higher estimation in Britain than in Gaul.

We have already alluded to the apparent similarity between the character and duties of these Druids and those of the ancient MAGI, among the Egyptians and other Eastern nations; and there

would appear to have been even a considerable analogy between their sacred ceremonies and observances. Both were in charge of the government and education of the people, in the broadest sense of those expressions. Their primary doctrines were entrusted only to those who had been previously initiated, and they shared their instructions in sacred groves located in remote and solitary places. When performing his religious duties, the Druid, like the Egyptian priest, was dressed in a white garment. The <term>Druids</term> were also physicians: they cured diseases by means of magical rites and ceremonies; and, claiming regular communication with the gods, they exercised the gift of prophecy. Their women, too, called *Alrunes*, like the Pythonesses in the Egyptian and Greek temples, were famous for their fortune-telling and medical skill. Indeed, their medical knowledge and practice were in such high regard that even the Roman Emperor AURELIAN is said to have consulted them regarding his own case. (VOPISCUS; *Aurelian*; c. 44.) They were also familiar with the methods of inducing ecstatic states; and, as one of their primary medical tools, they used mistletoe, which they gathered at specific times and with certain solemn ceremonies, considering it a special gift from Heaven.—*Nihil habent Druidæ—ita suos appellant Magos—visco et arbore, in qua gignitur (si modo sit robur) sacratius. Jam per se roborum eligunt lucos, nec ulla sacra sine ea fronde conficiunt.*

It was primarily under such sacred trees that the ancient Germans offered their holy sacrifices, and their inspired Bards uttered their prophecies. Moreover, it is remarkable that, as in ancient times, the medical art in France continued, until a late period, to be combined with the priestly office. Many of the hospitals in that country had a clerical origin: they were, for a long time, supervised by ecclesiastics who were also physicians.

POMPONIUS (*De Situ Orbis*; Book III, ch. 6) relates a singular story concerning the priestesses on the island of *Sena*, near the coast of Brittany. This island, he says, was very celebrated because of an oracle sacred to a Gallic divinity. The priestesses who presided over it, nine in number, and who made a vow of perpetual virginity, were called *Gallicenes*. They were considered to be gifted with peculiar endowments; namely, the ability to excite storms by means of their magical incantations; to assume the form of any animal they pleased; to cure diseases that were incurable by others; to possess a knowledge of future events; and to hold the gift of prophecy. They were devoted primarily to the service of seafaring people when consulted by them. PLINY (XXX. 1) says of the <term>Druids</term> in Gaul and Britain that they prophesied and cured diseases.

We would probably be considered guilty of an unpardonable omission, however, if we were to pass over in total silence that peculiar and very remarkable variation of the condition in question which has long been known in the Highlands of Scotland (though not confined to that locality) under the name of *Second Sight*. An interesting and apparently authentic account of this was long ago communicated to the public by Mr. Martin, whose statements have been amply corroborated by subsequent and competent authorities.

There cannot be a reasonable doubt about the reality of this phenomenon, as it is confirmed by the most unimpeachable testimony and supported by the evidence of living witnesses. We hold it to be a species, or variety, of the somnambulistic or ecstatic condition, as must, we think, appear manifest from the numerous examples which have already been cited.

In answer to the skeptical objection that “the Seers are impostors, and the people who believe them are credulous, and easily imposed upon,” Mr. Martin states, that “the Seers are generally illiterate and well-meaning people, and altogether void of hidden motives; nor could I ever learn that any one of them made the least gain by it; neither is it respectable among them to have that faculty. Besides, the people of the islands are not so credulous as to believe implicitly, before the thing foretold is accomplished; but when it actually comes to pass afterward, it is not in their power to deny it, without violating their own senses and reason. Besides, if the Seers were deceitful, can it be reasonable to imagine that all the islanders, who do not have the second sight, should combine together and violate their own understandings and senses to force themselves to believe a lie from age to age? There are several persons among them, whose birth and education raise them above the suspicion of participating in an imposture, merely to gratify an illiterate and contemptible sort of persons; nor can a reasonable man believe that children, horses, and cows, could be involved in a conspiracy to persuade the world of the reality of the second sight.”

We formerly referred to many instances of the development and exercise of this peculiar faculty among the primitive nations of the earth—among the early Eastern sages, and also among the Greeks and the Romans. The same natural phenomena have been manifested, although perhaps more rarely, in all countries in modern times; and they have also sometimes been excited by artificial means, as shall be shown hereafter. The peculiar characteristic feature of second sight and similar conditions is this: they intrude unexpectedly and without being requested by the individual; indeed, they sometimes occur in spite of his repugnance toward such an intrusion. This particular modification of the ecstatic state, however, is not restricted, as some suppose, to any one age or any one country. We shall have opportunities, in the following pages, to refer to many instances of the manifestation of similar phenomena occurring in various circumstances. ## CHAPTER XXVIII.

THE introduction of Christianity among the nations of Europe brought about an important modification, both in the belief of the people regarding the nature of these apparently supernatural powers, and also in the manifestations of the phenomena themselves. In the pagan world, these manifestations—

<meta>This page continues the discussion from the introduction of Chapter XXVIII regarding how the shift to Christianity altered European perceptions of supernatural manifestations, specifically contrasting the pagan view of spirits as earthly neighbors with the Christian view of them as otherworldly entities.</meta>

manifestations were generally believed to result from a certain sympathetic connection, or harmony, existing between mankind and certain superior beneficent or malevolent beings—demons, elves, fairies, etc. In those early times, indeed, there was no such absolute separation of the perceptible and supersensible worlds as was subsequently implied in the Christian distinctions between heaven and earth, the temporal and the eternal, and the spiritual and the physical natures. Spirits and demons were considered as beings belonging to the sphere of actual terrestrial existence: heaven or hell had nothing to do with the business of their mission upon earth. They were, in fact, the tutelary spirits, the beneficent genii, or the playful tormentors of the human race—beings who occasionally held familiar interaction with the children of this world, sometimes administering advice and warning, revealing secret things, and laying open the mysteries of the future. No definite distinction was made in the case of an extraordinary occurrence, whether it proceeded from some strange external influence, or was the natural result of certain physical, physiological, or psychological causes.

In Christianity, on the other hand, spirits were accounted beings of an absolutely different substance and nature, descending or ascending from another sphere of existence for the express purpose of exercising a supernatural influence over the destiny of mortals; and this influence appeared so much the

Among the Greeks and Romans, accordingly, **magic** originally had a completely different character from that which it later assumed in the Jewish-Christian creed. Among the former, the magical arts were not attributed to the influence of hellish powers of darkness, as in the Christian scheme, but to beings who enjoyed an intimate and private interaction with other beings of an even higher order. But mistaken views of Christianity later led to a completely different state of opinion regarding the relationship of humanity toward the spiritual world; and those extraordinary psychological phenomena, which occasionally present themselves in the human organism, were now attributed—according to the differences in how they manifested—sometimes to the immediate action of God, and more frequently to that of the Prince of Darkness—the work of God, or that of the Devil.

Indeed, there is no blessing conferred by God upon His creatures which, as a result of the perversity of human nature, does not have its established counterpart, or which, through its abuse, may not be converted into a curse. ### CHAPTER XXIX.

The word *witch* is probably derived from the Teutonic *wissen*—to know. In the Old Norse, a witch was called *Haegse*, which signifies wisdom. (See OLAUS WORMIUS, in *Lexico Runico*.) This last name was subsequently converted into *Hexe* in modern German.†

---

* Soon after the appearance of *Isis Revelata* some years ago, an elaborate review of that publication appeared in the first number of a west-country periodical, which quickly met its end. In that paper, the *Wise Man of the West*, through an extremely ludicrous blunder, attempted to ridicule the author for his supposed belief in witchcraft; the witty writer of the article was evidently unable to distinguish between a belief in the reality of certain psychical states and a belief in the reality of the objects represented in the hallucinations that originate in that particular condition. *Ne sutor ultra crepidam* <note>Latin: "The cobbler should not go beyond his last"—a proverb advising people not to offer opinions on matters outside their expertise.</note>.

† In ancient Germany, the female Druids were called *Alrunes*, and, subsequently, *Feas*, or fairies. TACITUS, probably by mistake, calls them *Deas*. The word *Fea*, or *Fæ*, appears to have been derived from the Greek *φάω* <note>To speak.</note>, to speak, from which are also derived *φάτις* <note>An oracle or report.</note> and *φάτης* <note>A prophet or spokesperson.</note>, oracle or soothsayer—in Latin, *Vates* <note>A prophet or seer.</note>.

The word WITCH—*Stria, Striga, Venefica*—was used, in

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the etymology and evolving perception of witchcraft, moving from the Teutonic and Druidic origins mentioned on the previous page to the shifts in meaning brought about by Christianity and the definition of superstition.</meta>

<page-num>253</page-num>
<header>A HISTORY OF MAGIC, etc.</header>

After the introduction of Christianity, the word, as well as the individual to whom it was applied, came into bad repute because of the general opinion that the knowledge and the power themselves were unlawfully derived from the Devil. The old Celtic name—*Alrune*—was the most ancient and most general name given to the German prophetesses. The word has much the same meaning as *witch*, and could be understood either in a good or in a bad sense. Indeed, the primitive meaning of these names was nearly identical with those of Magicians, Diviners, <term>Mantics</term> <note>Prophets or diviners.</note>,

early times, to denote a female supposed to be acquainted with the real or presumed influence of certain mineral or herbal substances, or their compounds, upon the human organism, and was employed in much the same sense as <term>herbaria</term> <note>Women who collect or use herbs for medicinal or magical purposes.</note> or <term>Φαρμακωτρία</term> <note>Greek: Pharmakotria—a sorceress or poisoner who works with drugs and potions.</note>. It is a mistake to consider the word "Witch," even in its modern definition, as equivalent to those of "Prophetess" or "Soothsayer." The word *Witch*, in the Bible, evidently includes the character of a poisoner—<term>Venefica</term> <note>Latin: A female poisoner or sorceress.</note>. The corresponding Hebrew expression is obscure and has clearly been misinterpreted.

Superstition, we may observe, consists not so much in the belief of extraordinary facts without probable grounds—this is mere credulity—as in ascribing these real or supposed facts to erroneous, and frequently absurd or supernatural causes. For example—in ancient times, two armies engaged in battle; the leader of one of these armies received, as he thought, an omen of victory. The victory was obtained and credited to the omen, and the omen itself was credited to some supernatural influence. The same principle applies, even in modern times, to a superstitious farmer in relation to his crops or his cattle. Superstition, therefore, may be a weakness, but it cannot be considered a crime. It arises from ignorance of the laws of causality or from a defect in reasoning power.

Soothsayers, prophets, etc., among other nations. Cicero says: *Sagæ a sagiendo dictæ, quia multa scire volunt. SAGIRE ENIM SENTIRE ACUTE EST.* (De Divinatione, Book I.) This is excellently expressed; for, as we shall eventually have the opportunity to show, the prophecies of these seers, or Alrunes, or by whatever other name they may be designated, appear to have been derived immediately from an internal presentiment—a subjective feeling—rather than from any previously acquired knowledge of the natural world, or from mere calculation of consequences. From *Haegse* probably comes the English word *Hag*, which was used by Shakespeare and others as a synonym for *witch*.

We have already observed that, upon the introduction of Christianity, these prophets—the women, in particular, who appear to have been quite numerous throughout Northern Europe under pagan worship—fell into disrepute, along with everything else related to paganism, insofar as it was deemed incompatible with the new faith. These prophets, prophetesses, and wonder-workers, indeed, came to be very generally denounced by the converts to the new faith as individuals who were supposed to be engaged in an unholy alliance with Satan and his evil spirits. Thus, it happened that purely psychical phenomena, although certainly of an abnormal description, became associated with religious faith; and the monstrous doctrine of actual bodily possession by the Devil or his

The common populace could not resist such authentic narratives and convincing arguments, backed by scriptural authority; and even the most enlightened men of the times were incapable of dispelling the universal delusion—a delusion which, even after the Reformation, was equally prevalent in Protestant and in Roman Catholic communities. Skepticism on this point, indeed, ultimately became heresy—the reputed witch was also a heretic; and the Bible was liberally quoted by the blind zealots of both persuasions, in confirmation of the orthodox opinion. This diabolical infatuation continued to prevail for a long period. As late as the year 1780, a witch was actually tried, condemned, and executed at Glarus, in Switzerland.

In the year 1484, the formal legal process against individuals accused of witchcraft was introduced into the states of Germany, and certain other countries, by a bull of Pope Innocent VIII; and in 1489, a publication appeared, under authoritative sanction, with the title *Malleus Maleficarum* (the Witch-hammer), which was long held in high regard as containing the authentic code of criminal procedure in cases of witchcraft. This most extraordinary specimen of philosophical skill, legal acumen, and learned subtlety prescribed, in minute terms, the rules for the detection and punishment of the alleged crime. Not only were many natural diseases and abnormal states of the body included under the category of diabolical possession,

Indeed, no country can be said to have entirely escaped the curse of these infamous and deluded prosecutions. Catholics and Protestants competed with each other in this cruel and unholy warfare; neither rank, sex, nor age was exempt from the risk of this general prosecution; and the entire European world was subjected to the terrors of criminal accusation and the resulting mental and bodily torture. It has been calculated that several hundred thousand individuals fell victim to this general delusion; and all these horrors flowed from the imaginary dominion of his Satanic Majesty over the souls and bodies of Christian people, under the pastoral supervision of the Christian clergy!

We previously observed that the actual existence of such a figure as the Devil was not originally a Jewish idea, but a Zoroastrian notion—a Chaldean or Babylonian hallucination—which had been borrowed by those people (the Jews) and transferred into their religious code during or after their captivity and exile. Even the doctrine of good and bad angels, and their continual interference in the affairs of this earthly realm, appears to have been derived from the same source. The manic belief, however, in the arbitrary empire of Satan and his angels or emissaries upon earth, and in the reality of diabolical witchcraft, continued to prevail to a greater or lesser degree throughout the entire European community until recently. This occurred although the progress of science and civilization gradually contributed to softening some of its harsher features and to diminishing the severity of its unholy convictions. Religion, too, has assumed a milder and more pleasing form; and the metaphorical notion of the Devil himself, in the conceptions of humanity, instead of being clothed with a frightening personality and exhibited as a bugbear to mankind, is generally admitted by all cultivated minds to represent merely the evil and per-

<summary>This page discusses the intellectual shift regarding the Devil and witchcraft, moving from a literal belief in Satan's earthly empire toward a metaphorical understanding of evil, supported by a quote from Thomas Burnet on the mystery of invisible natures.</summary>

<header>AN HISTORY OF MAGIC, &c.</header>

verse dispositions that deform the nature of man, or the diseases, moral or physical, by which human beings may be afflicted.

In the earlier periods of Christianity, as in the infancy of human society, little distinction was made between the natural and the supernatural. The laws by which this universe is governed under the Supreme Being had not yet been carefully and scientifically investigated; and to the rude and untutored mind, every uncommon occurrence was represented as magical or supernatural, and consequently attributed to divine or <term>demoniacal</term> influence. Almost everything, in short, was a miracle to the people; and every individual, according to his natural ingenuity or capricious fancy, attempted to explain particular phenomena in his own way. Hence the diversity of religions and the multiplicity of popular deities in the pagan creed.

The general diffusion of Christianity, however, introduced a new feature into the theory of <term>demonology</term>. As the God of the Jews and Christians was the only true God, the false gods of the pagan world were declared to be the Devil and his emissaries—the arch-enemies of the orthodox faith and the authors of all the evil, doctrinal or practical, that exists, or is supposed to exist, in the universe. "The <term>demons</term>," says TATIAN (*Orat. ad Græc.*), "are the founders of all false religions; and to gratify their pride, they cause themselves to be worshipped as gods by the heathen." The Devil himself he designated as πρωτόγονος δαίμων—the chief

<summary>This page explains that in early Christianity, a lack of scientific knowledge led people to view uncommon natural events as supernatural or demonic. It also describes how Christianity redefined pagan gods as the Devil and his emissaries to establish the singular authority of the Christian faith.</summary>
<keywords>Christianity, demonology, Tatian, paganism, supernatural, demoniacal influence, the Devil, religious history</keywords>

of the demons. (See MEYER’S *Historia Diaboli, seu de Diaboli Malorumque Spirituum Existentia.* Tubingen, 1780.) These demons, indeed, were the reputed authors of all those false miracles which were supposed to be performed for the purpose of promoting and confirming pagan worship; and they were also believed, of course, to have been the patrons of the heathen oracles and of all magical arts. According to the same accounts, they constantly endeavored to injure mankind in every possible way by introducing plagues, famine, diseases, etc., among the people. (ORIGEN. *Advers. Celsum.*, viii. § 31.) From their nature, too, they were believed to be capable of affecting the souls as well as the bodies of men. (TERTULLIAN.) JUSTIN expressly says that they (the demons) entertain the most deadly hatred against Christians because they will not flatter or worship them, and also because Christians were enabled to put them to flight in the name of JESUS and by the power of the Holy Cross.

The miracles performed by this infamous and omnipresent being were as much a matter of faith as those of God and our Savior; and consequently, it happened that many individuals thought it more advisable to enter into a secret alliance with Satan than to expose themselves to his anger and persecution. Furthermore, such a contract was believed to enable them to exercise magical power over others and to gratify their most wicked and abominable passions with secrecy, success, and impunity. The belief in the actual existence of such imaginary, shameful, and unholy contracts prevailed until at least the seventeenth century. As late as the year 1659, a famous Professor of the University of Jena in Germany wrote and published a learned treatise—*De Nefando Lamiarum cum Diabolo Coitu*. Indeed, even in our own times, have we not heard a popular clergyman condemning from the pulpit the diabolical practice of <term>Animal Magnetism</term>, which he, in his simplicity, no doubt, seems to have considered identical to <term>witchcraft</term>? *

In the dark times to which we have referred, there were, it is true, a few of the more enlightened

The general ignorance and laxity of morals appears to have reached its peak in the ninth and tenth centuries, when the most impudent and wicked contempt for all law and order became obvious; every species of wickedness was on the rise, and the gross barbarism and immorality of the priesthood—and, consequently, the wretched discipline of the church—afforded free scope for the most depraved and debasing licentiousness. Even when the secular sword of justice was raised, it seldom fell upon the heads of the most harmful offenders; and the forms of legal procedure in these rude and dark times were poorly adapted for the proper investigation and ultimate suppression of those blatant evils, which had been allowed to become so deeply rooted and so universally spread throughout the Christian community. The meager knowledge and defective policy of the age were insufficient to provide the established authorities with the necessary means of detection and punishment. At that period, indeed, science was at its lowest point. Everything that appeared to deviate from the ordinary routine of life, or to go beyond the simple knowledge of the people, was included in the category of Magic. The sovereignty of his Satanic Majesty appears to have reached its peak around the period of which we have just spoken. In the 11th and 12th centuries, however, several influential events occurred—the Crusades among others—which tended to produce a greater spread of knowledge, and to improve the scientific and even the religious aspect of the European world. The throne of Satan was shaken, although not entirely overthrown, by the power of more liberal and enlightened opinion; and the influence of learning and wit contributed greatly—especially among the better-educated classes—toward diminishing the terrors caused by the common belief in demonology and witchcraft. > “there is but one step from the sublime to the ridiculous;”
and the truth of this observation is confirmed by the circumstances which occurred about the period we have just mentioned. The Devil, whose very name had previously inspired such reverential awe and apprehension, and whose power and influence throughout the universe had been

But this degradation did not last long. In the 13th century, another change appears to have come over the spirit of the times; the foul fiend seems to have been largely restored to his former dignity, power, and reputation. Witchcraft, too, as an almost necessary consequence, once more appeared to be on the rise, and was furthermore associated with a variety of religious heresies that sprang up in the Christian church around this period and appeared to threaten the destruction of the hierarchy. The general epidemic of insanity now broke out anew and raged more violently than ever. Scarcely an individual existed who was not, in popular opinion, either a witch or bewitched. Moreover, witchcraft quickly became synonymous with heresy, and heresy was considered either the parent or the offspring of witchcraft. The diabolical tribunals were again fully occupied, and the fires kindled by the fervor of an extravagant religious zeal again tortured and consumed thousands of miserable victims.

In proportion to the number and virulence of the prosecutions for heresy, witchcraft, etc., these fictitious and imaginary crimes increased and multiplied to an enormous extent. During the prevalence of the monastic life indeed, enthusiasm, fanaticism, and asceticism became epidemic and contagious; psychological disorganization, in one form or another, was almost universally diffused; a morbid state of feeling was engendered and propagated; and feverish visions and fantastic notions of angels, saints, devils, and demons became a common mania. The prevalence of sorcery and witchcraft at this period, and the activity displayed in the discovery, prosecution, and punishment of these abominations, appear from the accounts given by **RAYNALD**, who assures us that, particularly in Germany and Italy, such a multitude of individuals had been seduced into these crimes that the whole earth would have been overspread and devastated by the Devil if, in these countries, nearly thirty thousand heretics had not been publicly burned alive.

# CHAPTER XXXI.

The belief in witchcraft and sorcery, along with its accompanying persecutions, seems to have reached its full maturity in the 14th and 15th centuries. During these ages, indeed, ignorance and superstition brought many miserable victims to the altar of his Satanic Majesty; and the mistaken zeal resulting from a barbarous and illiterate state of society sent a vast number of wretched and insane individuals, of both sexes, to the stake. Heresy and witchcraft abounded in various forms and degrees; the accusation was easy, but the defense was difficult. An accusation based on mere suspicion, or arising from petty malice, became magnified into a serious charge. This was followed by a judicial process conducted by ignorant and prejudiced inquisitors, and the whole affair generally ter-

In an intellectual age like the present, it is almost impossible to imagine the utter degradation, the vulgar ignorance, and the monstrous depravity of the times we are now describing. Never was the darkening of the mental faculties so complete and so general—never were the spirit of observation and the resulting knowledge of the operations of nature at so low an ebb—never were the depraved dispositions of mankind more conspicuously developed than in the 14th and 15th centuries—and these were the peak days of the undisputed domination of the priesthood. The entire European world was handed over to the merciless and uncontrolled influence of Satan and his infernal emissaries, and the whole earth was converted into a hell. Many volumes might be filled with a listing of the multitude of disgusting atrocities and bloody crimes which were committed in those times under the cloak of religious zeal during this truly disastrous period of history. Our limits will not permit us to expand upon this most unpleasant subject; but we may refer our inquisitive readers to the following treatises:—TIEDEMANN; *Disputatio de quæstione, quæ fuerit artium Magicarum origo* <note>Discourse on the question of what was the origin of the magical arts</note>. The bull in question contains the commission to the judges appointed to investigate these crimes; and it is likewise declared that these sorcerers made use of small images and mirrors in their magical conjurations: *Conflari imagines plumbeas vel etiam lapideas fabricarunt, malignos spiritus invocarunt, ut per eos contra salutem hominum molirentur, aut eos interimendo violentia carminis* <note>They manufactured cast images of lead or even stone and invoked evil spirits, so that through them they might plot against the safety of men, or destroy them by the power of a spell</note>, etc. Ten years later, the same Pope still complained of the unhallowed addiction of mankind to the unlawful arts of sorcery. “So deep was the darkness,” says he, “that several persons, *solo nomine Christianos* <note>Christians in name only</note>, abandon the true light, make a compact with hell, and force the demons to comply with their illicit demands.” *Daemones nempe immolant, hos adorant, fabricant imagines vel speculum, vel phialam, magice daemones illibi alligantur. These offensive superstitious practices prevailed so extensively throughout Europe that the French Sorbonne, at the instigation of the enlightened Chancellor GERSON, in the year 1398, issued a publication containing twenty-seven articles against sorcery and the superstitious use of images in mirrors and in stone, and also against the invocation of demons and spirits, with a view to enlighten and calm the people. GERSON's own treatise is entitled *De erroribus circa artem magicam* <note>On errors concerning the magic art</note>. At Langres, too, a special synod was held in 1404, chiefly for the purpose of arresting the progress of sorcery.

In the 15th century, the belief in witchcraft and sorcery may be said to have at length reached its climax. In this age, however, and for a long period thereafter, it is remarkable that females chiefly became liable to the charge of practicing these crimes, and that the regular form of legal process against the persons accused or suspected of such practices was authoritatively introduced by the famous bull of Pope Innocent VIII, to which we have already alluded. This notable bull was subsequently reinforced by the publication of the no less celebrated *Malleus Maleficarum* <note>The Hammer of Witches</note>. It may seem proper that we should give our readers some short account of the nature and objects of this memorable papal bull, and of the subsequent and related publication referred to. The following are the principal contents of the Papal bull, issued by Pope Innocent VIII in the first year of his pontificate. His Holiness begins by expressing his sorrow and regret at learning that, in several parts of Germany, some of which are pointed out by name, many individuals of both sexes, heedless of the salvation of their own souls, have renounced the Catholic faith, mingle with demons and lecherous devils (*incubus et succubus abuti* <note>to be abused by an incubus and succubus</note>), and by means of their aid, make use of various magical arts and devilish contrivances, torment men and animals, work a great deal of mischief, destroy the fertility of the earth itself—vineyards, gardens, and meadows; render men impotent and women unfruitful (*ne actus conjugales reddere valeant* <note>that they may not be able to perform the marriage act</note>); and practice many other infamous vices (*quamplurima nefanda excessus et crimina* <note>very many unspeakable excesses and crimes</note>). His Holiness, therefore, by virtue of this bull, conferred upon three commissioned ecclesiastics full powers to preach the word of God in those parts, to search for heretics, to prosecute them with excommunication, censure and punishment, interdict and suspension, or other more effective means (*ac etiam formidabiliores sententias* <note>and even more formidable sentences</note>), without appeal. He orders his venerable brother, the Bishop of Strasbourg, to publish the contents of this mandate, either by himself, or by another, as often as he shall be required to do so by the Inquisitors, intimating, at the same time, that he will not permit

This supreme apostolic authority granted the Inquisitors an easy and unaccountable task; for whatever measures their judgment or their whims might lead them to pursue, they were subject to no contradiction or opposition from any side. Their jurisdiction, indeed, was absolute and unlimited, and there was no appeal from their decisions. Until then, the people had fully acknowledged the supreme authority of the Roman Pontiff only in matters of faith; consequently, not in the case of ordinary criminals, such as witches and sorcerers were alleged to be. But now, sorcery was to be considered heresy: the two offenses were associated together and blended into one and the same crime, and placed exclusively under the jurisdiction of the ecclesiastical power. The legal process against witches and sorcerers had not previously been formally authorized; and the judges themselves, in such cases, might have been summoned to appear and answer for their conduct before a

But, henceforth, heresy and sorcery were merged together as one and the same crime: The unbeliever was a sorcerer, and the sorcerer—or even the person reputed to be bewitched—was an unbeliever and in alliance with the Devil. Indeed, even to suggest a doubt regarding the reality of witchcraft was sufficient to subject the unfortunate skeptic to the suspicion of patronizing diabolical arts. The Pope had ruled it so, and his holiness was infallible.

### CHAPTER XXXII.

The *Malleus Maleficarum*—a work to which we have already referred—was written in support of the Papal bull of Innocent VIII, and in furtherance of its objectives, by three ecclesiastics who were appointed by his holiness as the inquisitors for carrying its enactments into effect. These were <term>SPRENGER</term>, <term>GREMPER</term>, and <term>HENRICUS INSTITOR</term>—all three accounted learned men in their day and generation. They were expressly named *Inquisitores hereticæ pravitatis* <note>Inquisitors of heretical depravity</note>; and they were armed, as we have seen, with very ample and stringent powers for the execution of their delegated task. Other

<summary>This page explains how the Papacy unified the crimes of heresy and sorcery, effectively criminalizing skepticism toward witchcraft. It also introduces the Malleus Maleficarum, authored by three Inquisitors to enforce the Papal bull of Innocent VIII.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the *Malleus Maleficarum*, detailing its official endorsements and its role as the definitive, unchecked authority for witchcraft trials.</meta>

<page-num>275</page-num>
<header>AN HISTORY OF MAGIC, &c. 275</header>

ecclesiastics are said to have assisted in the composition of this remarkable work; and several lay writers are also quoted as authorities for many of the alleged facts. The Papal bull was prefixed to the work, along with the solemn approval and sanction of the theological faculty of Cologne. The authors of this learned production even managed to obtain a diploma from the Emperor Maximilian, although he was himself a skeptic in regard to the matter of sorcery. The *Malleus Maleficarum*, thus sanctioned and patronized by ecclesiastical and secular authority, became from then on the great and infallible code of witchcraft; and, as may easily be believed, because of the barbarous spirit of the age, combined with the ignorance and intolerance of the Church, its requirements were enforced in a most arbitrary and capricious manner by the judges appointed to carry its legal processes into execution; while against their proceedings, however irregular or unjust, there was no appeal either to the supreme spiritual or temporal jurisdiction. The many atrocities which must naturally have resulted from such opinions, fortified with such full powers in those days of intellectual darkness and ignorance, may be easily imagined. It would be tedious, as well as disgusting, to enter into the details of specific instances of gross abuse. One circumstance, however, we consider deserving of special notice, and that is, that even at this early period the female sex was considered to be much more addicted to the crime of witchcraft than the male—

...a distinction which, as we shall probably have occasion to observe hereafter, is common to the manifestation of all those psychical phenomena which have their origin in an inordinate excitement of the sensory faculties. We shall also pass over the different types of alleged witchcraft, along with their various modes of manifestation, with the single exception alleged to have been exhibited in various instances: the presentiment and prophecy of future events, and the discovery of hidden things—a faculty which was frequently exhibited by the accused, and which has been demonstrated to have been very generally developed in many similar abnormal states of the organism. In short, it would appear that the reputed witches, in general, were in fact individuals laboring under some particular form of the somnambulistic or ecstatic affection—an affection which frequently takes its form and direction from the peculiar character of the times, and to which the female sex is more particularly liable. To use the language of the *Marquis de Puysegur*, these females might probably be pretty correctly designated as *Somnambules desordonnées* <note>disordered somnambulists</note>; they exhibited phenomena of very much the same character as those which are occasionally manifested by natural and magnetic somnambulists. But the witch-persecutors were by no means skilled in psychology, and to them the Devil was the active promoter of all such manifestations.

attributed to witchcraft are absolute physical impossibilities, diametrically opposed to the most simple and obvious laws of nature, and could never have been credited by any individual of common sense in an enlightened age, and possessing the most elementary knowledge of natural science. But in the times of which we are now speaking, all inquiry into the laws of nature was itself accounted impious and heretical; and any individual who attempted to cultivate such studies was deemed an atheist, and was, moreover, presumed to have sold himself to Satan.

Nor was sophistry lacking to confirm the reality of these monstrous hallucinations. When any one, more sagacious than his neighbors, attempted to object to some of the feats alleged to have been performed by the individuals accused of witchcraft, on the ground of the impossibility of the fact, he was immediately met and refuted by an allusion to some of the fictions of the heathen poets. Thus, to prove the possibility of the transformation of men into beasts, it was seriously alleged that the soldiers of ULYSSES were changed by CIRCE into hogs, and those of DIOMED into birds: IPHIGENIA was changed into a doe, and LYCAON was transformed by JUPITER into a wolf. Nay, even an occasional scrap of Scripture was not lacking to confound the skeptics. A long period, indeed, must necessarily pass, before the voices of the more rational and more enlightened members of the community can even obtain a hearing amidst the general ignorance and barbarism ; and their more intelligent notions exert their due influence in correcting the dangerous errors which may have become deeply rooted in the minds of their fellow citizens. To us, in this more enlightened age, it appears altogether marvelous that such monstrous absurdities, as are gravely authenticated as clear and incontrovertible facts in these judicial proceedings, should have been seriously believed by any individuals endowed with the smallest particle of reason or of common sense: and we can attribute the circumstance only to the general epidemic infatuation. But in those days, medicine was probably no further advanced than theology. The religious sentiment itself, indeed, when incessantly and exclusively cultivated in an ascetic form, is itself a fertile source of such sensory disorders. The Roman Catholic system, with its mystical doctrines, its various superstitious rites, ceremonies, fasts, penances, and other religious observances, was particularly calculated to produce these conditions, especially in susceptible constitutions; and, accordingly, they appear to have prevailed very extensively under that institution. They were encouraged by the priesthood, who considered them as marks of divine grace; and, because of their infectious character, they were often found to pervade an entire community. These remarkable states, which in several instances appeared in an epidemic form, were in reality a specific variety of the somnambulistic or ecstatic condition. They were generally considered to be of either a divine or a diabolical origin, according to the characteristic features of the phenomena. The former were always believed to be caused by celestial influences, and to them was ascribed an angelic origin and character; the latter were held to be the consequence of satanic possession, and were

In former times, the power of exorcism was believed to be exclusively restricted to the clergy of the Roman Catholic Church; the Protestants, being heretics, were held by the Catholics to be totally incapable of properly performing the rite. The preceding narrative highlights, very clearly, some exceedingly curious facts, but shows, at the same time, little progress in the knowledge and treatment of such abnormal manifestations. Much, indeed, still remains to be learned about this interesting subject, regarding which it is rather strange that we have obtained so very little information from professional physicians in modern times. Indeed, the Church, from the earliest period, appears to have appropriated all such enigmatic cases for itself and used them for its own specific purposes. Hence the epithet—*Morbus sacer* <note>Latin for "sacred disease," a term historically applied to epilepsy and other conditions believed to have a supernatural origin.</note>. It is to the Animal Magnetists <note>Followers of Franz Mesmer who believed in a "magnetic" fluid or force that could influence the body and mind; precursors to modern hypnotism.</note>, unquestionably, that we have recently become indebted for all that we really know regarding these curious phenomena, which have been so frequently observed, both naturally and artificially, in ancient as well as in modern times. But the Church appears to be quite jealous of the inroads of secular science into what she had long been accustomed to consider her proper and exclusive domain.

### CHAPTER XXXIV.

In a preceding chapter, we noticed the common opinion that miracles, so-called, had ceased after the crucifixion of Jesus Christ, or at least after the death of his Apostles. This opinion, however, is not supported by the facts of history; nor does it derive any authority from the express declarations of the Savior himself. On the contrary, JESUS declared that miracles would still be performed by his Apostles and worshippers; and he expressly refers to *faith* as the active principle in producing these phenomena.

The Roman Emperor CONSTANTINE lived three hundred years after JESUS and showed a lively zeal in the spreading of Christianity and in the destruction of pagan temples. According to the testimony of EUSEBIUS, this emperor ordered the destruction of a temple in Cilicia, which was frequently visited by people who came to worship the presiding demon <note>In this context, the "demon" (or dæmon) refers to a local deity or spirit associated with a site, rather than a malevolent entity.</note> and obtain relief from their various complaints. In fact, the demon in such cases was an entirely fictional figure; it was not the demon but the priests who performed the cure by means of certain processes similar to magnetism <note>A reference to "animal magnetism" or mesmerism, where trance-like states were used for healing.</note>. The patients were put to sleep, dreamed, and were ultimately cured.

<summary>The author argues that miracles did not cease with the Apostles and provides a historical account of how Emperor Constantine destroyed a temple where priests used magnetic processes to heal patients under the guise of a local deity.</summary>

<keywords>miracles, Jesus Christ, Apostles, Savior, faith, Emperor Constantine, Christianity, Eusebius, Cilicia, demon, magnetic cures</keywords>

--- Page 363 ---
<meta>This page continues the historical survey of healing through magnetism and dreams, moving from Constantine to the later Roman emperors and the testimony of early Christian and pagan writers.</meta>

<page-num>291</page-num>
<header>A HISTORY OF MAGIC, etc.</header>

This fact is confirmed by **Origen**, who tells us that the cures performed in dreams by **Æsculapius** existed in his time in full vigor; that is to say, long after the introduction of **Christianity**. (**Origen**, *Against Celsus*.) **Iamblichus**, who lived after **Constantine**, tells us that the **Temple of Æsculapius** still produced oracles and curative dreams; and he adds: *Many similar things happen daily that surpass human speech and reason.* (**Iamblichus**, *On the Mysteries*.)

Under the **Emperor Julian**, magnetism had lost none of its effectiveness. This emperor himself informs us that, when sick, he had frequently been cured by remedies pointed out by **Æsculapius**, and he appeals to **Jupiter** as a witness to the facts. (*Aesculapius has often healed me with indicated remedies, and Jupiter is witness to these things.* **St. Cyril**, *Against Julian, book 7*.) In the reign of **Valentinian**, in the Western Empire, during the fourth century, oracles in dreams were still highly regarded, as we are informed by **Eunapius**, who wrote in those times (*in Oedesio*).

Under the **Emperor Valens**, **Magnetism** was confused with the magical arts and was, therefore, exposed to persecution. **Ammianus Marcellinus** informs us that this emperor caused an old woman to be put to death, who was accustomed to cure intermittent fevers by pronouncing some harmless words. She was sent for, with the knowledge of **Valens** himself, to cure the daughter of this emperor. This simple woman actually restored the girl's health, and, for her reward, the emperor

<summary>The author documents the continued practice of temple-based dream healing and magnetism through the 4th century, citing both pagan and Christian witnesses. It also describes how these practices began to be persecuted as "magic" under Emperor Valens, despite their medical success.</summary>

caused her to be put to death as a criminal. (**Ammianus Marcellinus**, *Book 29*.) We may recall the story of the Athenian woman whom her countrymen condemned to death for performing cures without using any medicine. In both of these cases, we may assume that some magnetic processes were employed without the parties involved being aware of the principle.

**Apuleius** informs us that the ancient physicians were acquainted with the effectiveness of words and verses in the cure of wounds and made use of them without reserve, just as **Ulysses** is represented to have done by **Homer**; and the author adds that nothing which operates as a remedy or a comfort to the sick can be regarded as criminal. (**Apuleius**, *Apologia*, *Book 1*.) This humane principle had already been established by a solemn enactment of **Constantine**, by which occult remedies, when found to be useful, may be said to have been legalized. (See *Theodosian Code, Book IX, Title 16, Law 3, concerning Sorcerers and Astrologers* <note>The Latin 'Mathemat.' or Mathematici referred to astrologers in this legal context.</note>.) The Emperor **Justinian** deemed this law worthy of being preserved in his code. This law, however, was subsequently abolished by the pious and feeble **Leo VI**, who, although a dedicated enemy of the art of divination, left behind no fewer than seventeen predictions on the fate of **Constantinople**.

We may here observe, what may be easily understood, that in these cures, the effectiveness does not reside in the mere words but in the intention with which they are pronounced. The magnetists hold

it as a fundamental principle, that the intention of doing good is the very soul of their art. The verbal formulas are merely the accessories, which ignorance, quackery, and superstition have elevated into real causes.

This doctrine has been clearly announced by **ST. AUGUSTINE**, in his treatise *On Christian Doctrine* (*De Doctrina Christiana*), *Book 2*: “When it is uncertain,” he says, “from where the virtue of a remedy proceeds, everything depends upon the intention we have in making use of it.”

**CHARLES VALLÉ**, a famous French physician, cured his epileptic patients by <term>insufflation</term> <note>The act of blowing air, in this case into the ears.</note> into their ears, without pronouncing a word. **DEGOUST**, a judge at Nimes, was in the daily practice of curing fever patients by the use of friction on the arms. At first, he used amulets also, but afterwards gave them up, and cured by means of friction alone.

Under the Emperor **VALENS** and his successor, Christianity spread throughout the whole of the Roman empire; and we hear no more of the temples of *Aesculapius*, of *Isis*, and of *Serapis*; but, as we shall presently see, the magnetic practices did not desert them, when consecrated to a different worship. Magnetism, for a time, took refuge among the tombs and relics of the saints; the <term>pernoctations</term> <note>The practice of staying overnight in a sacred place to receive a cure or divine vision.</note> continued under the same forms, and with the same success. This change appears to have taken place in the fourth and fifth centuries.

Christianity had spread throughout the whole

<meta>This page continues the argument that magnetic healing is a natural phenomenon that persisted during the transition from Paganism to Christianity, though it became subject to religious exploitation.</meta>

provinces of the Roman Empire, and the temples of the false deities had been either demolished or converted into Christian churches. Had magnetism been merely a product of ancient heathen worship, it would naturally have disappeared when its causes ceased to operate; but if it is dependent upon natural laws and inherent in the constitution of the species, it must still have continued under every form of religious worship—under Christianity as under Paganism. In reality, we find this last to have been the case. Upon the general diffusion of the Christian faith, we see it transferred to the priests of the triumphant religion, especially among the monks, just as it was previously among the pagan priesthood. The churches succeeded the ancient temples, in which the traditions and processes of magnetism had been preserved. There were the same customs of <term>pernoctation</term> <note>The practice of staying overnight in a sacred place to receive a cure or divine vision.</note>, the same dreams, the same visions, and the same cures.

But the best blessings of Providence are liable to abuse in human hands. It was not long before a trade was established in the merits of particular saints—a speculation was made regarding the advantages that might be derived from the greater or lesser celebrity of their patrons, just as in the case of the ancient temples—and the number of miracles was enormously increased. Pretended relics were carefully collected; particular miracles were performed in a specific church or a specific chapel that had not occurred in another; they had their saints for different diseases, just as we have bark <note>Likely referring to Jesuit's bark or cinchona, used as a specific remedy for fevers.</note>

SAINT BASIL the Great and ST. GREGORY practiced medicine, as did also a number of other ecclesiastics, and, in France, the Royal Physicians were generally selected from that class.

Thus it is clear that medicine was studied and practiced in the Christian churches and monasteries, no doubt because the priests found this custom established in the pagan temples which they superseded; and, in these temples, the idolatrous priests frequently resorted to magnetism. It is curious enough, however, that these practices did not exist in the Christian churches as long as the worship of ISIS, SERAPIS, AESCULAPIUS, etc., persisted, but were only introduced after the latter had fallen into disuse.

We could quote a vast variety of similar cases, but it seems unnecessary to us. In the meantime, we shall simply refer our readers to the works of the early Church Fathers, and, particularly, to the collection of the *Bollandists*.

We may conclude this chapter with the observation that this occult, *hypnoscopic* science, now called Animal Magnetism, has always had its supporters and its enemies. The clergy, especially, have generally opposed it with great stubbornness, whether with the goal of monopolizing the exclusive practice of the art, or for the purpose, in certain circumstances, of turning the phenomena into miracles. The subject, however, is now becoming more widely studied, and the minds of mankind are more open to rational conviction and a just appreciation of the facts.

## CHAPTER XXXV.

At this stage of our investigation, it may, perhaps, be appropriate to present a few examples of historical figures whose character and actions showed, during their lives, a strong prevalence of ecstatic conditions, and whose entire conduct has generally been attributed to insanity or deception. It is necessary, however, to convince those of our readers who may be skeptical about this entire subject that these examples should be of the kind that can be supported by adequate and unquestionable documentary evidence; and this, in the following instances, we shall endeavor to provide.

One of the most remarkable recorded instances of this constitutional tendency toward ecstatic conditions in active life is shown to us in the history of **JOAN OF ARC**, the Maid of Orleans. This case is indeed all the more remarkable because it displays not only all the most prominent characteristics of the visionary state, but also because it demonstrates the successful influence of this apparently innate idiosyncrasy in the most important affairs of active life, and in a female subject.

The history of the achievements of this singular person is well known and exhibits all the traits

DAULON urgently urged her to provide him an opportunity to see this council; but she answered him, saying that he was not yet worthy nor sufficiently virtuous. For this reason, he spoke no more to her on this subject.

JOAN was a simple girl, raised in the country and quite uneducated. “I understand neither A nor B,” she said to the envoys who were sent by the King to Poitiers for the purpose of examining her. She could not write her name but signed with a cross. She had made a vow of perpetual chastity and had never experienced any of the physical conditions typical of her sex.

It is remarkable that the prophetic ability of the Maid of Orleans ceased after she had fulfilled her mission by conducting the King to Reims. From that time, she wished to retire into solitude and was reluctantly persuaded to remain any longer with the army. Her subsequent fate is well known. Taken prisoner by the enemies of her country and subjected to the most shameful indignities, she ended her brilliant career of heroism and clairvoyance with a cruel and disgraceful death at the stake. But the memory of this remarkable person—although in one instance profaned by obscene ribaldry and diabolical malice—has been preserved and honored by some of the noblest efforts of poetic genius.<note>See SOUTHEY’S poem and SCHILLER’S drama.</note>

<summary>This page describes Joan of Arc’s humble and uneducated background, noting that her prophetic abilities seemed to end after the coronation at Reims. It details her capture and execution, while noting that her legacy was later redeemed by great poets.</summary>

## CHAPTER XXXVI.

In his very interesting and instructive work on *Vital Magnetism*, the learned and ingenious Dr. PASSAVANT of Frankfurt has associated with the memory of JOAN OF ARC that of another female *clairvoyant* of a somewhat different character—that of ST. HILDEGARDIS, a contemplative seer—whose history is, perhaps, in some of its circumstances, still more intimately connected with the doctrine of *Animal Magnetism*.

In her eighth year, ST. HILDEGARDIS was placed under the charge of a pious lady, who brought her up in the greatest simplicity and taught her nothing but the Psalter. All external accomplishments were utterly neglected in her education. In her book, titled *Scivias*, she says: 

> “When I attained the age of forty-two years and seven months, a fiery light from the opened heavens penetrated my entire brain and inflamed my whole heart and breast, like a flame which does not burn but warms, or like the sun, which warms an object upon which it casts its rays. And, suddenly, I received the gift of understanding and interpreting the Scriptures, namely, the Psalter, the Gospels, and other books, both of the Old and New

But, says her biographer, whatever was lacking in her external faculties was supplied by the spirit of internal truth and power; and while her body was wasting away, the zeal of her spirit grew. The Pope, Eugene III, the pupil and friend of Bernard of Clairveaux, urged by the latter, sent several people to her place of residence in order to collect more specific accounts of the seer. He himself was so impressed by her writings that he read them to those in his circle.

It is remarkable that before she went into the convent of St. Robert, near Bingen, she fell into a state of complete catalepsy. She lay like a stone in bed, says the narrator, without being capable of the slightest motion. The Abbot, who heard of this but did not believe it, went to visit her; and it is said that when he attempted, with all his strength, to raise her head or to move it from one side to the other and could not succeed, he was astonished at the wonderful phenomenon. He acknowledged that it did not proceed from any human suffering, but from a divine rapture (*divina correptio*).

After many negotiations for the purpose of having her removed to the place she had determined in spirit (the convent of St. Robert, near Bingen), the Abbot went to the afflicted patient and commanded her, in the name of God, to arise and go to the place which Heaven had appointed for her residence. <term>HILDEGARDIS</term> immediately rose up, as if

In regard to her visions, she wrote this to the monk WIBERT OF GEMBLACH: “God works as he wills, for the glory of his own name, not for that of the earthly creature. I have a constant anxiety; but I raise my hands to God, and, like a feather which has no weight and is driven here and there by the wind, I am supported by Him alone. What I see, I cannot know with certainty as long as I am engaged in bodily occupations and my soul is blind <note>that is, without spiritual vision</note>; for in both lies all human weakness. Ever since my childhood, when my bones, nerves, and arteries were not yet completely formed, I have had such visions up to the present time, when I am seventy years old. My soul becomes elevated, as God wills, in these visions, up to the very height of the firmament and to all mundane spheres <note>through the changes of different airs</note>; and it extends itself over various nations, although these may be in distant regions and places. *These things, however, I do not perceive with my outward eyes, nor hear with my external ears, nor through the thoughts of my heart (the thoughts of my heart), nor by means of any comparison of my five senses; but in my soul alone, with open eyes, without falling into ecstasy; for I see them in my waking state, by day and by night.*”

**Hildegardis** appears to have also possessed the faculty which, in later times, has been occasionally observed in ecstatics, of appearing to persons at a distance. “But what shall we say to this fact,” asks her biographer, “that the virgin, in times of great trouble, warned, by her apparition, such persons as had her image present to them in their prayers?”

A young man, **Ederick Rudolph**, once passed a night in a small village, and when he went to bed, he sought the prayers of the holy Virgin; that is, probably, he had a vivid impression of her in his thoughts. Upon this, she appeared to him in a vision, in the same dress which she usually wore, and revealed to him that, if he did not speedily leave that place, his life would be in danger from the enemies who were in pursuit of him. He immediately left the place, along with some of his companions. Those who remained behind were surprised and overpowered by their enemies, and acknowledged that they had acted foolishly in disregarding the warning of the vision. The biographer of the saint relates several other cases in which **Hildegardis** is said to have appeared to distant patients, who had her in their thoughts, and to have cured them.

For this reason, she became the oracle of princes and bishops for many years.

Born in the year 1098, **Hildegardis** died on September 17, 1179, as she had long before predicted to her fellow residents in the convent. Gifted with great spiritual energy, she suffered almost constantly from severe physical infirmities, which she bore, however, with the most exemplary fortitude, patience, humility, and resignation to the divine will. We may observe that the lives of **St. Theresa** and several other female saints recorded in the early history of the Christian Church share many similarities with that of **St. Hildegardis**.

There are many individuals, we do not doubt, who will be prepared to meet the facts related in the preceding narrative with a smile of incredulity, or perhaps of contempt, believing their own narrow minds to be the only true standard for the possibilities of nature and of providence. Such persons must be left to the enjoyment of their presumed omniscience. Indeed, to attempt to reason with individuals of this description would probably be a futile and unnecessary task. We must, therefore, simply leave these facts to make their own impression upon such minds as are capable of com- <note>The text ends mid-word, likely "comprehending".</note>

Ignorance is, perhaps, as frequently displayed in the unreasonable rejection as in the too hasty admission of alleged facts.

## CHAPTER XXXVII.

In commemorating those historical figures who, at various times, have shown an unusual gift of natural or constitutional clairvoyance, we must not omit to notice the phenomena presented to us in the person and fortunes of FLAVIUS JOSEPHUS, the Jewish warrior and historian. All of the actions of this extraordinary person, indeed, especially during his intercourse with the Emperors VESPASIAN and TITUS, exhibit all the essential characteristics of the magnetic idiosyncrasy. It appears certain, indeed, that JOSEPHUS, like SOCRATES and several other eminent men whom we have already mentioned, was a natural or habitual <term>crisiac</term> <note>A term used in early magnetic theory to describe someone prone to entering a trance state or "crisis."</note>, and possessed the faculty of predicting future events. Thus, among other

Josephus, after having fought valiantly against the Romans, refused to surrender to them after the capture of the important town of Jotapat. In order to secure himself from danger, he went down into a well, which connected, by a side opening, with a spacious cavern, where he found about forty of the bravest among the Jews who had taken refuge there. But he was soon betrayed, and his hiding place was discovered.

Vespasian, who had occasion to know the courage of Josephus, was desirous of saving him. He proposed a surrender, which the Jewish leader twice refused. But his resolution, at length, having been somewhat shaken by Nicanor—his former friend and one of the principal chiefs of the Roman army, whom Vespasian had sent to reason with him upon the subject—*he began to reflect on the dreams he had previously experienced, in which* God *had revealed to him both the misfortunes that would befall the* Jews*, and the ultimate triumph of the* Romans*; for he perfectly understood the interpretation of dreams and grasped everything that was obscure in the divine responses. Indeed, he was well-versed in the* Sacred Scriptures*, and the books of the prophets: For he was himself a priest, and born of the priestly race.* At length, as if full of inspiration and focusing his mind upon the horrible images presented to him in his last dreams, he secretly addressed his prayers to God:

> "Creator," he said, "since you have been pleased to abandon the Jewish nation; since you have chosen my spirit to predict the future, I yield to the Romans, and I shall live. I take you to witness that I shall not go over to them as a traitor, but as your servant." — *De Bello Judaico*; *Book iii. ch. 14.*

We learn from this passage that Josephus was a <term>*crisiac*</term>, and that he foresaw the future in his dreams. Indeed, he appears to have had a presentiment of the defeat of the Jews and the ultimate victory of the Romans; and he considered himself called upon to carry to the latter the substance of the oracles he had received in his dreams, without incurring the charge of treason; and this induced him to surrender.

But he found great opposition to his project from his companions in misfortune. They declared that they would rather die than surrender; indeed, they even proposed to sacrifice Josephus and then to kill themselves. Josephus attempted, in vain, to dissuade them from carrying this project into execution: They listened only to the counsels of their despair. Their swords were already uplifted over the head of their leader when, by a sudden inspiration, he declared to them that he agreed to their project; but he thought that a reciprocal death ought to end their lives; that the lot should determine, successively, who should kill and who should be killed, until all had perished; that, by this means, no one should

<meta>This page continues the account of Flavius Josephus’s survival during the siege of Jotapata, specifically detailing how he avoided the collective suicide pact of his companions through what the author characterizes as a "crisiac" foresight.</meta>
