This proposition was eagerly accepted. The lots were drawn, and they all perished by the hands of their associates, with the exception of Josephus and one of his companions, whom he persuaded to live after having promised to preserve and protect him.

Nothing can be more extraordinary than this foresight of Josephus, which gave him the presentiment that the lot should not fall upon himself, but that he should escape it. It appears to have been a sudden spark of spiritual illumination which suggested to him the drawing of lots, and, at the same time, the certainty that the lot should not fall upon himself. He says of himself, indeed, that while communing with his companions, he felt himself, as it were, full of the divinity: *Quasi Deo plenus* <note>"As if full of God."</note>.

Josephus lived a long time after Jesus Christ; and, being a Jew, he was consequently regarded as an enemy to the Christian faith. In the career of Josephus, therefore, we can hardly adopt the presumption of any direct interference of the Deity. What, then, was the cause of his prophetic power? Was it not the same as that of the foresight of all other <term>crisiacs</term>—that internal instinct which informs the somnambulists, the sibyls, and the enthusiasts of all ages?

Josephus ultimately went over to the Romans, and soon found a friend and protector in the person of Titus, the son and successor of Vespasian. It was the intention of the latter to send Josephus to

--- Page 391 ---
<meta>This page continues the account of Flavius Josephus following his capture by the Romans, describing how he avoided being sent to Nero by prophesying the imperial future of Vespasian and Titus.</meta>

<language>English</language>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>319</page-num>
<header>A HISTORY OF MAGIC, etc.</header>

Nero, the reigning emperor. Josephus, however, having been informed of this intention, requested an audience with Vespasian in order to make him aware of some matters which he could not reveal to any other individual. This audience was granted to him in the presence of Titus and two of Vespasian's friends. “You wish to send me to Nero,” Josephus said; “and why send me there, seeing that he, and those who shall succeed before you, have so little time to live? You alone are the one I should regard as emperor, and Titus, your son, after you, because you will both be elevated to the imperial throne. I speak on behalf of God. Until then, let me be confined in a secure prison, so that I may be punished as an impostor if I am found to have abused the name of God and deceived you.”

Vespasian at first believed that Josephus only spoke to him in this manner out of fear and for the purpose of winning his favor. But he soon changed his opinion when he found that all that Josephus had previously foretold had actually come to pass—in particular, his prediction regarding the fate of Jotapat. Vespasian also secretly asked the other prisoners whether these facts were true, and he was assured that everything was correct. Vespasian, however, had Josephus strictly watched, while at the same time treating him with great kindness; Titus later became his close friend and patron.

<vocab>Nero, Josephus, Vespasian, Titus, Jotapat, prophecy, prediction</vocab>

<summary>Josephus gains Vespasian's favor by correctly prophesying that Vespasian and his son Titus would both become emperors. Although initially skeptical, Vespasian confirms Josephus's previous accurate predictions and decides to treat his prisoner with kindness.</summary>
<keywords>Josephus, Vespasian, Titus, Nero, Jotapat, prophecy, imperial throne, Roman history</keywords>

--- Page 392 ---
<meta>This page continues the account of Flavius Josephus and his relationship with the Roman emperors, following his accurate prophecy regarding Vespasian's rise to power.</meta>

<language>English</language>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>320</page-num>
<header>A HISTORY OF MAGIC, etc.</header>

The prophecy of Josephus regarding the fortunes of Vespasian caused a great sensation at the time and was noted by several contemporary authors—specifically by Dion Cassius and by Suetonius. Subsequently, Josephus himself obtained the honors of a Roman citizen and had an annual pension bestowed upon him during his life. After the death of Vespasian, he continued to enjoy the favor and friendship of Titus, and even of his successor, Domitian. See the work of Josephus, *De Bello Judaico*, and other contemporary authorities.

---

### CHAPTER XXXVIII.

In the preceding short chapter, we may appear to have diverged somewhat from the regular course of our narrative; we must now resume the proper order of our history by returning to the period when the ideas of the pagan world were essentially modified by the doctrines and worship of Christianity.

For several ages after the fall of Paganism, indeed, little change was shown in the religious or philosophical opinions of mankind, if we except the important introduction of the purer idea of only one God and of one Savior, Jesus Christ. Ancient notions regarding magic and sorcery still continued to occupy the minds of mankind; the

<meta>This page continues the discussion on how, despite the rise of Christianity, ancient beliefs in magic and the influence of evil spirits persisted alongside a deep suspicion of scientific and linguistic scholarship.</meta>

the doctrine of evil spirits—devils and demons—was still maintained, and their influence even exaggerated; the sciences, as they were then called, of <term>geomancy</term>, <term>hydromancy</term>, <term>pyromancy</term>, <term>necromancy</term>, etc., were still held in high regard by all ranks of the people; the fortune-tellers, the astrologers, the *<term>genethliaci</term>* or *<term>horoscopi</term>*, were in high repute, and the authenticity of their respective sciences was proven from Scripture; and even the mathematician was associated with the magician and the conjurer. He who presumed to study the Hebrew language was accounted a Jew, and consequently an enemy to Christianity; the classical scholar was more than suspected of being a pagan and a polytheist.

In times of intellectual darkness and ignorance, indeed—as the author has elsewhere observed—the ways of Providence, in the administration of the affairs of the universe, were very imperfectly understood. Far from having any notion that the ordinary government of this sublunary world is carried on by means of general laws—or secondary causes—established by the great Creator of all things from the beginning of time; every remarkable, every unusual event, was considered to be beyond the limits of the operation of nature, and directly attributed to an immediate mysterious interposition of the Deity. Even the wise, and otherwise enlightened Athenians—to whom literature, philosophy, and the fine arts are so infinitely indebted—even the enlightened Athenians, under their polytheistic system, had an utter aversion to

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the historical aversion to natural philosophy, detailing how ancient Athenians and later Europeans often viewed scientific explanations and technological advancements as heresy or sorcery.</meta>

<page-num>322</page-num>
<header>AN HISTORY OF MAGIC, &c.</header>

all those philosophers who attempted to account for any of the phenomena of nature by means of general laws. The fate of SOCRATES is well known. Even among the inhabitants of that, in other respects, most intelligent nation, an eclipse was generally considered to be a prognostic of some grievous impending calamity, caused by the anger of the gods. ANAXAGORAS, one of their most famous philosophers, was accused of atheism, imprisoned, and persecuted (like GALILEO in modern times) for attempting to explain the eclipse of the moon by natural causes; and PROTAGORAS, another of their wise men, was subsequently banished from Athens for maintaining similar heretical doctrines.

Even in more familiar instances, and in much more recent times, occurrences of far easier explanation were generally held to be naturally impossible; and, consequently, they were attributed, as usual in those times, to an infamous compact with the *Evil One*. When the first German printers carried their books to Paris for sale, this rapid multiplication of copies appeared so supernatural, in comparison with the tedious process of manuscript labor, that these ingenious tradesmen were actually condemned to be buried alive as sorcerers, and only escaped the cruel and barbarous punishment by a hasty flight. Hence, probably, the well-known fiction of the Devil and DOCTOR FAUSTUS. In a treatise written in French around the year 1260, the author, after stating that the magnetic needle might be highly useful at sea, observes that "no

But—to come still closer to our own case—during the 15th and 16th centuries, and even at a later period, the belief in sorcery and witchcraft, as we have already seen, was so universally prevalent among all ranks, learned and unlearned, that to express a doubt of their reality was sufficient to draw down upon the unfortunate skeptic a heavy charge of impiety and atheism; and the preamble to a statute of Henry VIII of England, in the year 1511, actually sets forth—

> “that smiths, weavers, and women”—rather a singular association of people—“boldly and customarily take upon themselves great cures, and things of great difficulty, in which they partly use sorcery and witchcraft.”

This is, probably, the first interdict, or injunction, issued in modern times against the practice, crude as it may have been, of <term>Animal Magnetism</term>.

Even at the present day, however—in this age of intellect and reason—we are by no means certain that this belief in the occasional production and cure of diseases, through the influence of supernatural agency, is yet entirely extinct; nor that those ingenious and inquisitive individuals, who have endeavored to investigate and illustrate the magnetic doctrines and practice, have entirely escaped the

Do we not still hear of certain hysterical, epileptic, and other spasmodic complaints which are commonly ascribed to demonic possession, and are cured either by the direct intervention of God or by seeking the aid of the Devil? What was the meaning of the *morbus sacer* of the ancients? And upon what principle, even at the present day, do the Roman Catholic priests, and even some of the Protestant clergy, proceed in their solemn exorcisms?

Now, an acquaintance with the curious discoveries of <term>Animal Magnetism</term>, as we shall see in the following pages, has this among its other advantages: it tends to clear away all this ancient mist of an ignorant and harmful superstition, to dispel common prejudices, and to give freedom to the mind; while it also rescues us from the awkward alternative of rejecting facts for the reality of which there

<note>* HIPPOCRATES, the leader of the ancient physicians, has left us a special treatise upon the <term>*Morbus Sacer*</term>, which he holds to be "in no way more divine, nor more sacred than other diseases." And this notion of its divinity he attributes to the inability of physicians to understand its nature, and to the simplicity of the method by which it is cured.

We may take this opportunity to observe that the complete works of HIPPOCRATES have been recently translated into English by <term>Dr. FRANCIS ADAMS</term> of Banchory, one of the most learned—if not the most learned—physicians our country can currently boast. It seems to us that his colleagues are much indebted to this gentleman for his clarifications of ancient medicine.</note>

<meta>This page concludes the first volume of the history, continuing the argument that scientific advancement—specifically the study of Animal Magnetism—provides a rational framework for understanding phenomena previously dismissed as supernatural or demonic. <page-num>325</page-num>

there exists the most ample and unimpeachable evidence—which would otherwise render all human testimony suspicious—by explaining all of the phenomena according to simple and intelligible principles; in the same way that the modern discoveries of chemistry and astronomy have annihilated the vain notions and fanciful pursuits of the alchemists and astrologers of a former age. Let us remember that, in the words of our great poet—

> “Miracles have ceased,
> And therefore we must necessarily admit the means
> By which things are perfected.”

We have now brought our inquiry into the interesting facts we have undertaken to investigate to a period at which a mighty revolution began to influence the sentiments of mankind in regard to religious belief, science, and civil policy; and it will henceforth be our business to trace the manifestations of the same phenomena among mankind during the further progress of society and civilization. If, throughout the whole course of this perhaps tedious, but certainly most interesting investigation, it appears that the phenomena mentioned have continually forced themselves into notice—although in somewhat different forms and aspects under every change of religious, social, and political institutions—we will certainly have good reason to conclude that there are certain permanent and indestructible elements in the nature and constitution of the human species to which the manifestations in question must necessarily be referred.

--- Page 398 ---
<meta>This page concludes the first volume of the history, transitioning from the historical investigation of phenomena to a defense of the author's scientific approach as being consistent with divine providence and natural law.</meta>

<page-num>326</page-num>
<header>AN HISTORY OF MAGIC, &c.</header>

And holding, as we do, that there is no original element in nature which has been created without its special uses in the mundane economy and the design of an all-wise creative Providence, we may be permitted to investigate the nature and properties of these several influences. We may also point out the apparent purposes for which they seem to have been made subservient in the general economy of the universe, without exposing ourselves to an irrational charge of heresy or impiety. To use the words of **St. Augustine** <note>The text uses the older form "St. Austin."</note>—*errare possum, haereticus esse nolo* <note>Latin: "I can err, but I do not wish to be a heretic."</note>. We shall be satisfied if the views we may be able to suggest are found to have a tendency to dispel some erroneous conceptions upon this curious but obscure subject and, in any degree, to promote the best interests of humanity. But, above all, we must endeavor, as far as it is in our power, to present a complete view of the facts, insofar as this object can be accomplished; and thus enable every intelligent and candid reader to form a sound opinion upon this interesting and much-disputed subject.
